> **ABOUTME:** History sink for findings that are done — drained from findings-unresolved.md, or
> filed straight here when found and fixed in one go. Ordered by when they resolved, not by ID.

# Resolved findings

History of codebase findings (issues discovered mid-work) that have been addressed. Two ways in,
both fine:

- **Drained** from [`findings-unresolved.md`](findings-unresolved.md) when a parked finding is
  fixed, so the active file stays a working set.
- **Filed straight here** when a finding is found *and* fixed in the same stretch of work. Routing
  it through the active queue just to move it out again records nothing extra. This is a normal
  path, not an exception — including the common variant where the work spans several sittings and
  only gets written up at the end.

**Newest first — by resolution date, not by ID.** A finding discovered long ago can resolve today,
so the numbers here are deliberately out of order and must stay that way; only
`findings-unresolved.md` and `decisions/working-notes.md` are ID-ordered (D130, and the gate that
enforces it excludes this file for exactly this reason).

One consequence of the direct path, worth knowing rather than avoiding: an ID allocated straight
into this file skips the merge-conflict collision check that ID-ordering gives the active queue, so
a same-number clash between two PRs surfaces at `make check` (`NoDuplicateFindingHeadings`) instead
of at rebase. Later, but still before it can ship.

### DF221 — `state.State` accumulated write-only fields, and nothing could have noticed (RESOLVED 2026-08-19)

- **Discovered:** 2026-08-17, after deleting the second of them ([DF219](#df219--statestatedevcontainermountwarnings-was-written-on-every-create-and-read-by-nothing-resolved-2026-08-17)) · **Workstream:** feedback routing / D145
- **Severity:** LOW individually; the pattern was the finding
- **Disposition:** **RESOLVED 2026-08-19**, swept before the v0.12.0 cut at the owner's direction.
- **Rides:** **any**.
- **Description:** `state.State` carried five fields assigned on every create and read by nothing — `ConfigJSON`, `Devcontainer`, `DevcontainerMounts`, `DockerdRequired`, `WorkdirMode`. With [DF205](#df205--isolationexplicit-is-written-never-read-and-wrong-anyway-resolved-2026-08-15) (`IsolationExplicit`) and [DF219](#df219--statestatedevcontainermountwarnings-was-written-on-every-create-and-read-by-nothing-resolved-2026-08-17) (`DevcontainerMountWarnings`) already deleted as one-offs, that made **seven of one shape in a single release**.
- **The generator, which is the part that mattered:** `State` is wide, assembled at one site, consumed at many. Adding a field is free, nothing forces a consumer to appear, and — the property that makes it invisible rather than merely untidy — **a field with no reader has no test that can go red.** No amount of care finds these; only counting does, and nobody counts until the third one.
- **Swept 2026-08-19.** The five fields and their assignments are gone from `internal/orchestrator/state/state.go`, `create/create.go` and `lifecycle/restart.go`. Behaviour is unaffected: each was a redundant copy of something reaching the sandbox by another route — devcontainer mounts through `mergeDcMounts` into `profileResult.mounts`, dockerd auto-start through `runtimeconfig.LifecycleConfig.DockerDRequired`.
- **Two more found by following through**, which is the argument for not stopping at the named list. `buildSandboxStateResult` was left holding a `configData []byte` parameter with no remaining use, and `resolvedCreateInputs` had `dcMounts`/`dcMountNotices` fields stored but only ever read as locals. Deleting a dead field without checking whether its producer died too just moves the defect one level up.
- **What prevents the eighth** — the question the finding insisted on answering before closing: **`TestArch_EveryStateFieldHasAReader`** (`internal/orchestrator/state/field_readers_test.go`). It parses the struct, scans every other `.go` file in the repo for a `.Field` reference that is not the field's own composite-literal assignment, and fails on any field with none. Verified by planting a dead field: it names it and fails. The reader search is textual and over-approximates deliberately — it errs toward missing a dead field rather than blocking a live one, which is the right direction for a gate nobody should have to argue with. Cited from `architecture/code-map.md`.
- **The shape is not confined to this struct.** `runtime/caps.Writer` was an exported alias with zero users, deleted the same week. The gate covers `state.State` because that is where the count justified one; a wider "no unread exported field" rule was not attempted, and a future occurrence elsewhere is not fenced.
- **Pointer:** `internal/orchestrator/state/state.go`; `internal/orchestrator/state/field_readers_test.go`; `internal/orchestrator/create/create.go`; `internal/orchestrator/lifecycle/restart.go`.

### DF220 — a mount-strip test named a branch it never reached, because its assertion matched the path it printed (RESOLVED 2026-08-17)

- **Discovered:** 2026-08-17, converting `FilterMounts` to structured notices ([D145](../decisions/working-notes.md)) · **Workstream:** feedback routing
- **Severity:** LOW (a test asserting nothing, in the security-relevant mount filter)
- **Disposition:** **RESOLVED 2026-08-17.**
- **Rides:** **any**.
- **Description:** `TestFilterMounts_WorkdirConflict` (`internal/orchestrator/archetype/devcontainer_test.go`) mounted `/other/path:/workdir/myproject` and asserted the resulting warning contained `"workdir"`. But `/other/path` does not exist, so the mount was stripped by the **source-missing** rule two branches earlier; the target-conflict rule was never evaluated. The assertion passed because `FilterMounts` quotes the offending mount spec back into its message, and that spec contains the string `workdir`. So the test that existed to cover the workdir-conflict strip covered the source-missing strip, and had done since it was written.
- **Why it survived:** the message is prose containing user data. A substring assertion over that cannot distinguish "the rule I meant fired" from "my fixture happens to contain this word", and nothing about the passing test says which. This is the general hazard D145's records address — the level, the event and the reason were all recoverable only by reading English.
- **Fixed 2026-08-17** as part of the D145 conversion: `FilterMounts` returns `[]feedback.Notice` instead of `[]string`, each carrying `Fields["reason"]` (`docker_socket` / `credential_dir` / `source_missing` / `workdir_conflict`). The test now uses an existing source directory — `t.TempDir()` — so the target-conflict branch is actually reached, and asserts on the reason rather than on a fragment of the message. The sibling tests moved to the same shape.
- **Pinned by** `TestFilterMounts_WorkdirConflict`, `TestFilterMounts_DockerSocket`, `TestFilterMounts_CredentialDir`, `TestFilterMounts_MissingSourcePath`, `TestFilterMounts_TypeBindFormat` (`internal/orchestrator/archetype/devcontainer_test.go`). Verified red on revert: restoring the `/other/path` fixture flips the reason to `source_missing` and the test fails, which is the confirmation the old form could not give.
- **Pointer:** `internal/orchestrator/archetype/devcontainer.go` (`FilterMounts`); `internal/orchestrator/archetype/devcontainer_test.go`.

### DF219 — `state.State.DevcontainerMountWarnings` was written on every create and read by nothing (RESOLVED 2026-08-17)

- **Discovered:** 2026-08-17, tracing where devcontainer mount warnings go for [D145](../decisions/working-notes.md) · **Workstream:** feedback routing
- **Severity:** LOW
- **Disposition:** **RESOLVED 2026-08-17.**
- **Rides:** **any**.
- **Description:** `state.State` carried `DevcontainerMountWarnings []string`, assigned from the create pipeline (`internal/orchestrator/create/create.go`) and read by no code anywhere — the warnings were already printed at their production site, so the field was a second, dead copy. Deleted.
- **The forest, per GEN §18:** this is **the second of its shape in one release**. [DF205](#df205--isolationexplicit-is-written-never-read-and-wrong-anyway-resolved-2026-08-15) deleted `IsolationExplicit` from the same struct for the same reason — a field written by the create path and consulted by nobody. Two is not yet three, but the generator is visible: `state.State` is a wide struct assembled in one place and consumed in several, so adding a field is cheap, and nothing fails when the consumer never arrives or later goes away. A field with no reader has no test that can go red. Worth a sweep of the remaining fields for a third; not done here, and named rather than silently left.
- **Fixed 2026-08-17.** The field is gone from `internal/orchestrator/state/state.go` and its one assignment from `create.go`. The warnings themselves became `[]feedback.Notice` threaded through `resolveAndApplyArchetype` and emitted at the same point in the sequence as before, so the user-visible ordering is unchanged.
- **Pinned by** the compiler, which is the honest answer for a deletion: no test can go red for a field nobody read, and inventing one would test the deletion rather than any behaviour. The behaviour that *is* pinned — the warnings still reaching the user, in order — is `TestResolveArchetype_DevcontainerFiltersMounts` (`internal/orchestrator/create/archetype_resolution_test.go`).
- **Pointer:** `internal/orchestrator/state/state.go`; `internal/orchestrator/create/create.go`.

### DF202 — a file-defined agent's declared credentials were an invisible, persistent grant (RESOLVED 2026-08-16)

- **Discovered:** 2026-08-13, during the config-key trust audit · **Workstream:** config/trust seams
- **Severity:** LOW (re-rated from MEDIUM — see below)
- **Disposition:** **RESOLVED 2026-08-16.**
- **Rides:** **any**.
- **Description, corrected under [D144](../decisions/working-notes.md#d144--ambient-environment-read-once-at-the-cli-name-freely-in-an-agent-definition-never-grant-invisibly):** originally filed as an ambient-config-leak violation — the claim that a file-defined agent naming `api_key_env_vars`/`auth_hint_env_vars` (`internal/agent/fileagent.go:84,88`) reopens the arbitrary-`${VAR}`-interpolation hole `internal/config/host_env.go:155-158` deliberately closed. D144 rejects that framing: an agent's declared credential keys and `${VAR}` config interpolation are not the same seam. `~/.yoloai/agents/*.yaml` is the user's own data dir, not attacker-reachable without write access to their yoloAI state, and naming a key there is the same act as `--env DIAMOND_KEY=$DIAMOND_KEY` — only persistent. Not an escalation reached by a different name.
- **What the original framing got right, restated:** the grant was invisible. A declaration written once in a file was consumed silently, forever, on every sandbox using that agent — and a shipped declaration (reviewed) and a file-defined one (not) were indistinguishable at the point of use.
- **Fixed 2026-08-16** by D144 line 2's disclosure. `agent.Definition` gains a `UserDefined` marker (`internal/agent/agent.go`), set by `fileagent.go`'s `toDefinition`; threaded through `envsetup.EnvSpec` (`AgentName`/`UserDefined`, `internal/envsetup/envspec.go`, compiled by `internal/orchestrator/envspec/envspec.go`'s `BuildEnvSpec`) to `envsetup.DescribeInjectedCredentials`, which the launch path (`internal/orchestrator/launch/launch.go`'s `discloseInjectedCredentials`) writes to `state.State.Output` naming exactly the keys resolved from the host-env snapshot — never the merely-declared set, never an `env:` value the user typed directly — and the declaring agent, flagged `user-defined` when it is one. Silent when nothing resolved, so the line's presence always means something was granted. Both residual risks D144 named are what this closes: the grant is visible at the moment it happens, and a file-defined declaration reads differently from a shipped one right there in the disclosure line.
- **Pinned by** `TestDescribeInjectedCredentials_NamesTheShippedAgent`, `TestDescribeInjectedCredentials_NamesTheUserDefinedAgentAndSortsKeys`, `TestDescribeInjectedCredentials_NothingResolvedIsSilent`, `TestDescribeInjectedCredentials_OnlyNamesKeysResolvedFromHost` (`internal/envsetup/envsetup_test.go`); `TestDiscloseInjectedCredentials_WritesLineWhenKeyResolved`, `TestDiscloseInjectedCredentials_SilentWhenNothingResolved`, `TestDiscloseInjectedCredentials_NilOutputDoesNotPanic` (`internal/orchestrator/launch/disclose_credentials_test.go`); `TestBuildEnvSpec_UserDefinedAgentIsMarked` (`internal/orchestrator/envspec/envspec_test.go`); `TestLoadFileAgents_ValidMinimal` (`internal/agent/fileagent_test.go`). D144 line 1 (the seam this all sits above) is separately pinned by `TestArch_LibraryNeverReadsAmbientEnv` (`internal/envsetup/envsetup_test.go`), cited from `docs/contributors/principles/security-principles.md` §11.
- **Pointer:** `internal/agent/agent.go` (`Definition.UserDefined`); `internal/agent/fileagent.go` (`toDefinition`); `internal/envsetup/envspec.go` (`EnvSpec.AgentName`/`UserDefined`); `internal/envsetup/envsetup.go` (`DescribeInjectedCredentials`); `internal/orchestrator/envspec/envspec.go` (`BuildEnvSpec`); `internal/orchestrator/launch/launch.go` (`discloseInjectedCredentials`).

### DF211 — `--no-profile` cannot change any outcome, and its help text describes a feature that does not exist (RESOLVED 2026-08-15)

- **Discovered:** 2026-08-14, while checking whether the user config selects a default profile ([D143](../decisions/working-notes.md)) · **Workstream:** config/trust seams
- **Severity:** LOW
- **Disposition:** **RESOLVED 2026-08-15.**
- **Rides:** **any**.
- **Description:** `ResolveProfile` (`internal/cli/cliutil/client.go:385-393`) returned `""` when `--no-profile` was set, `""` when no profile flag was given at all, and the flag's value otherwise. `--profile` and `--no-profile` were mutually exclusive (`internal/cli/lifecycle/new.go:75`). So `--no-profile` produced exactly the result of passing nothing: **a no-op on every path.**
- **Its help text documented a feature that was never built.** `internal/cli/lifecycle/new.go:52` read *"Use base image even if config sets a default profile"* — but no config key ever selected a default profile. `YoloaiConfig` had no such field and `ResolveProfile` consulted no config file. The flag was the vestige of a default-profile feature that does not exist, and the help text was the only place it was still described as real.
- **Fixed 2026-08-15**, by the owner's decision: deleted rather than repaired — a real default-profile feature is later work, not a reason to keep a no-op flag around in the meantime. The flag registration and its `MarkFlagsMutuallyExclusive("profile", "no-profile")` are gone from `internal/cli/lifecycle/new.go`'s `addCreateFlags` (shared by `new` and `run`), and `ResolveProfile` collapses to a bare `--profile` read — kept as a function rather than inlined at its three call sites (`new.go`, `run.go`, `mcp.go`), since a generic-looking one-liner duplicated across two packages is worse than the small helper. Swept from `docs/GUIDE.md`, `docs/contributors/design/commands.md`, and the `--no-*` convention example in `docs/contributors/standards/cli.md` (replaced with `--no-broker`, which is real). `docs/BREAKING-CHANGES.md` carries the Unreleased entry: no behaviour is lost, since the flag never had any — an invocation using it now fails with `unknown flag` instead of silently doing nothing.
- **Pinned by `TestNewCmd_NoProfileFlagRemoved`** (`internal/cli/lifecycle/new_test.go`), asserting `cmd.Flags().Lookup("no-profile")` is nil, and by `TestResolveProfile_IgnoresNoProfileFlag` (`internal/cli/cliutil/client_test.go`), asserting a stray `no-profile` flag on the command is not consulted. Verified red on revert of `new.go` and of `client.go` independently — each alone restores a different half of the old behaviour, and each has its own pin.
- **Pointer:** `internal/cli/cliutil/client.go` (`ResolveProfile`); `internal/cli/lifecycle/new.go` (`addCreateFlags`); `internal/cli/lifecycle/new_test.go`; `internal/cli/cliutil/client_test.go`; `docs/BREAKING-CHANGES.md`.

### DF195 — `--env` comma-splits its value on `new`/`run` but not on `start`/`restart`/`reset`, so an ordinary value cannot be passed on the path that creates the sandbox (RESOLVED 2026-08-15)

- **Discovered:** 2026-08-12, building the P2 chokepoint harness · **Workstream:** CLI
- **Severity:** LOW–MEDIUM (a shipped flag rejects ordinary input on two commands and accepts it on three, and the error blames the user's value)
- **Disposition:** **RESOLVED 2026-08-15.**
- **Rides:** **any** for the fix itself. Arguably **breaking** in one direction: anyone relying on `--env A=1,B=2` to set *two* variables in one flag lost that — and it worked today only by accident.
- **Description:** `internal/cli/lifecycle/new.go:66` declared `--env` as `StringSlice`, which splits every value on commas. So `--env NO_PROXY=localhost,127.0.0.1` was parsed as two values, the second had no `=`, and the command exited 2 with `invalid --env value "127.0.0.1": must be KEY=VAL` — an error naming a fragment the user never wrote as a value. Commas are ordinary in environment values: `NO_PROXY`, `JAVA_TOOL_OPTIONS`, `GOFLAGS`, any comma-separated list.
- **Why it was a defect rather than a preference: three sibling paths already did it the other way.** `restart.go:42`, `start.go:41` and `reset.go:46` all declared the *same* `--env` flag as `StringArrayVar`, which does not split. So `--env NO_PROXY=localhost,127.0.0.1` was rejected by `yoloai new` and accepted by `yoloai restart` — the same spelling, the same flag name, opposite behaviour, decided by which command you reached it through.
- **Fixed 2026-08-15.** `new.go:66`'s `--env` registration changed from `StringSlice` to `StringArray`, and `:219`'s read from `GetStringSlice` to `GetStringArray`, matching `start`/`restart`/`reset`. `run` shares this registration through `addCreateFlags`, so it is fixed by the same change — confirmed there is no separate `--env` declaration for `run`. `docs/BREAKING-CHANGES.md` carries the Unreleased entry: `--env A=1,B=2` now sets one variable whose value contains a comma rather than two variables, on every command that accepts `--env` — repeating the flag is the one way to pass two values, which was already true on `start`/`restart`/`reset`.
- **Pinned by `TestNewCmd_EnvFlagDoesNotSplitOnComma`** (`internal/cli/lifecycle/new_test.go`), which parses `--env NO_PROXY=localhost,127.0.0.1` through the real `new` command and asserts it survives as one value. Verified red on revert of the `new.go` flag-type change (reverting it back to `StringSlice` splits the value into two, the second failing `KEY=VAL` parsing inside `parseEnvSlice`).
- **Pointer:** `internal/cli/lifecycle/new.go:66,219`; against `internal/cli/lifecycle/restart.go:42`, `start.go:41`, `reset.go:46`; `internal/cli/lifecycle/new_test.go`; `docs/BREAKING-CHANGES.md`.

### DF205 — `IsolationExplicit` is written, never read, and wrong anyway (RESOLVED 2026-08-15)

- **Discovered:** 2026-08-13, during the config-key trust audit · **Workstream:** config/trust seams
- **Severity:** LOW
- **Disposition:** **RESOLVED 2026-08-15.**
- **Rides:** **any**.
- **Description:** `internal/orchestrator/create/prepare_profile.go:247` sets `pr.isolationExplicit`, and it reaches `state.go:62` / `create.go:419`, but a repo-wide grep (including tests) finds **no reader**. It is also wrong at the source: `internal/cli/lifecycle/new.go:544` coalesces the `--isolation` flag with the config value (`cliutil.Coalesce(cliutil.FlagStr(cmd, "isolation"), cfgIsolation)`) before the create pipeline ever sees the result, so `opts.Isolation` is non-empty whenever `isolation:` appears in config — and the "explicit" bit is then set for a value nobody typed on the command line.
- **Why it was worth recording rather than deleting on sight:** it was exactly the provenance signal a future "did the user choose this, or did config?" rule would want (the same question DF206 answered wrong for network mode, until its own fix below), and it was a known-bad signal sitting in the state struct looking usable — the next reader who found the field had no reason to doubt it.
- **The bit was not merely unused — the question it asks is still live, and unanswerable where it sat.** [DF209](#df209--isolation-and-model-still-carry-from-personal-defaults-into-a-profile-by-a-second-route-df207df208-did-not-close-resolved-2026-08-15) is the proof: because `Coalesce` had already merged flag and personal config by the time `prepare_profile.go` ran, a personal `isolation:` overrode a profile's, and no change inside the create pipeline could tell the two apart to stop it. So "is there any remaining reason to distinguish `--isolation` from config?" had a concrete answer — yes, it is what makes `config.md:167`'s guarantee true for this key. That question belongs to [D143](../decisions/working-notes.md), not to this field.
- **Pointer:** `internal/orchestrator/create/prepare_profile.go:247`; `internal/orchestrator/state/state.go:62`; `internal/orchestrator/create/create.go:419`; `internal/cli/lifecycle/new.go:544`; the working counter-example at `prepare_profile.go:109`.
- **Fixed 2026-08-15** in `e4c39b0a`, deleted at all four sites — `profileResult.isolationExplicit`, its assignment in `applyCLIOverrides`, the copy into `state.State` in `create.go`, and the `state.State` field itself — rather than repaired. `state.State` is an in-memory pipeline struct with no persistence tags reaching disk, so no migration was needed. **No test accompanies the deletion:** dead code with zero readers cannot be made to go red by reverting it, so rule 10 does not apply. The provenance question the field wanted to answer is unaffected by its deletion and is still open — it is [D143](../decisions/working-notes.md)'s to answer structurally, not a field's to fake.

### DF206 — config and profile `network.allow` entries are silently discarded whenever the mode is set explicitly (RESOLVED 2026-08-15)

- **Discovered:** 2026-08-13, during the config-key trust audit · **Workstream:** config/trust seams
- **Severity:** MEDIUM (the discarded list is the egress allowlist)
- **Disposition:** **RESOLVED 2026-08-15.**
- **Rides:** **any**.
- **Description:** `internal/orchestrator/create/prepare_profile.go:154` and `:218` gated the entire network block on `opts.Network == NetworkModeDefault`. So any explicitly-set mode made the block skip, discarding every config- and profile-level `network.allow` entry without a word — **including when the mode was set implicitly by `--network-allow` itself**, which promotes `networkIsolated` to true at `internal/cli/lifecycle/new.go:197-199` before `prepare_profile.go` ever runs. `docs/GUIDE.md:679` documents this key as "additive with agent defaults", with no mention of this silent-discard condition.
- **This was a fourth instance of DF196's class.** [DF196](findings-unresolved.md#df196----network-none-silently-swallows-an-allowlist---network-allow-is-accepted-discarded-and-never-recorded) and [DF197](findings-unresolved.md#df197----port-is-refused-with---network-none-at-the-cli-only-so-a-profiles-or-archetypes-ports-are-accepted-and-silently-dropped) already establish that `--network-none`/`--network-allow` combinations and profile/archetype ports are accepted, discarded, and left unrecorded because a guard is written at the flag rather than at the point config, profile and flag are finally resolved together; this was the same shape one config key over, on the merge path rather than the CLI path. Both of those findings stay open — this fix did not touch them.
- **Severity was MEDIUM rather than LOW** because the discarded list is the egress allowlist: the user believed a destination was permitted and it was not, or believed their config had narrowed something when it was silently dropped.
- **Pointer:** `internal/orchestrator/create/prepare_profile.go:154,218`; `internal/cli/lifecycle/new.go:197-199`; `docs/GUIDE.md:679`; against [DF196](findings-unresolved.md#df196----network-none-silently-swallows-an-allowlist---network-allow-is-accepted-discarded-and-never-recorded), [DF197](findings-unresolved.md#df197----port-is-refused-with---network-none-at-the-cli-only-so-a-profiles-or-archetypes-ports-are-accepted-and-silently-dropped).
- **Fixed 2026-08-15** in `e87fe576`. Split the gate at both sites: only the mode *promotion* to isolated stays conditional on `opts.Network == NetworkModeDefault`; the allowlist merge (`opts.NetworkAllow = append(...)`) is now unconditional. **Not fixed here, and staying open on purpose:** under `--network-none` the merged entries still never reach the sandbox — `netpolicy.Compose` discards both lists for that mode — which is [DF196](findings-unresolved.md#df196----network-none-silently-swallows-an-allowlist---network-allow-is-accepted-discarded-and-never-recorded), a separate finding.
- **Pinned by two tests, one per site**, both verified red on revert of the production change: `TestApplyConfigDefaults_NetworkAllowMergesWhenCLIOverridesMode` (base-config site; replaces the old `TestApplyConfigDefaults_NetworkSkippedWhenCLIOverrides`, which had asserted the defect as intended behavior) and `TestResolveProfileConfig_NetworkAllowMergesWhenModeAlreadySet` (profile-merge site, the `--network-allow`-promotes case DF206 named as sharpest).

### DF210 — a profile's `os:` is parsed, merged, and never read (RESOLVED 2026-08-15)

- **Discovered:** 2026-08-14, while modelling the config layers for [D143](../decisions/working-notes.md) · **Workstream:** config/trust seams
- **Severity:** LOW
- **Disposition:** **RESOLVED 2026-08-15.**
- **Rides:** **any**.
- **Description:** `MergedConfig.OS` is written twice — seeded from the base config at `internal/config/profile.go:378` and merged across the profile chain at `:427` — and a repo-wide grep found **no reader**. So a profile declaring `os: mac` was parsed, validated as a known key, merged, and silently discarded. The effective OS comes only from the CLI, which resolves it as `Coalesce(FlagStr(cmd, "os"), cfgOS)` (`internal/cli/lifecycle/new.go:545`) — flag, else the *personal* config — so a profile could not influence it at all.
- **Same class as [DF203](findings-unresolved.md#df203--backendcapscapadd-is-one-boolean-for-three-features-so-devices-is-a-silent-no-op-on-two-backends-and-setup-is-refused-on-two-others), [DF204](findings-unresolved.md#df204--resources-limits-are-ignored-on-three-backends-not-four--podman-shares-dockers-handling) and [DF205](#df205--isolationexplicit-is-written-never-read-and-wrong-anyway-resolved-2026-08-15):** a key that is accepted, carried, and inert. Four instances at time of discovery, which was the argument that the config surface was not tested against "does setting this change anything". DF205 is now resolved (above); DF203/DF204 remain open.
- **What would close it:** D143's per-key policy table makes an unread key visible by construction, since a key with no consumer has no policy row. Until then, either apply `merged.OS` or reject `os:` in a profile — silently accepting it is the one option that should not survive.
- **Pointer:** `internal/config/profile.go:378,427`; `internal/cli/lifecycle/new.go:545`; `internal/cli/cliutil/client.go:69`.
- **Fixed 2026-08-15** in `02bb26e3`. Rejected rather than applied: `LoadProfile` now fails a profile whose `config.yaml` sets `os:`, via `checkOSKeyRejectedInProfile`, naming the key, saying it has no effect in a profile, and pointing at `--os` or the base config — the same house pattern `checkMountsKeyRemoved` established for `mounts:` (D142). Applying it was not viable: the effective OS is resolved at the CLI, before backend selection and before any profile is read, so no change inside this package could make a profile's `os:` take effect.
- **`MergedConfig.OS` and the public `ResolvedProfileConfig.OS` (`profile_config.go`) were deliberately left in place, not removed**, even though `internal/cli/profile/profile.go`'s own `printProfileInfoScalars` confirms zero readers, including display. Removing a field from a public Go/JSON API is its own breaking change with its own blast radius, independent of whether a profile can populate it, and is left for a future audit of `ResolvedProfileConfig` rather than bundled into this fix.
- **Registered as a deprecation** (rule 9, D127): `docs/contributors/deprecations.md`, incurred 2026-08-15, 12-month user-facing grace period, since a profile written on an earlier release may still carry `os:`.
- **Pinned by `TestLoadProfile_OSKeyRejected`**, verified red on revert of the production change.

### DF213 — a profile's `agent:` key is silently ignored on a default install (RESOLVED 2026-08-15)

- **Discovered:** 2026-08-14, during the D143 audit · **Workstream:** config/trust seams
- **Severity:** MEDIUM (a documented profile key silently does nothing; the user gets a different agent than the profile names)
- **Disposition:** **RESOLVED 2026-08-15.**
- **Rides:** **any**.
- **Description:** The baked-in template sets `agent: claude` (`internal/config/defaults.go:15`), but `config.GenerateScaffoldConfig` (`internal/config/defaults.go:127-138`) comments out every setting line when writing `defaults/config.yaml` on first run, so a fresh install's copy sets nothing. The CLI's `ResolveAgentFromConfig` (`internal/cli/cliutil/client.go:356-362`) reads through `config.LoadDefaultsConfig`, which merges the baked-in defaults back in, and falls back to `"claude"` — so `opts.Agent == "claude"`. But `ycfg` comes from `config.LoadConfig` (`internal/orchestrator/create/create.go:485`), which reads only the file on disk and does not merge baked-in defaults, so `baseAgent := ycfg.Agent` (`internal/orchestrator/create/prepare_profile.go:85`) is `""`. The guard `opts.Agent == baseAgent && merged.Agent != ""` (`prepare_profile.go:109`) is therefore false on every default install, and a profile's `agent:` is never applied.
- **The mechanism's shape is sound; its inputs are mismatched.** Both sides of the comparison must be drawn from the same loader. That is the fix, and it is small.
- **The existing test certifies the broken path.** `TestResolveProfileConfig_AgentPrecedence` (`internal/orchestrator/create/prepare_profile_test.go:194`) hand-constructs `ycfg = &config.YoloaiConfig{Agent: "claude"}`, exercising only the case where the personal config *does* set `agent:` — a state a default install never reaches. It must be rewritten, not extended, to cover the actual default-install path.
- **Pointer:** `internal/config/defaults.go:15,127-138`; `internal/cli/cliutil/client.go:356-362`; `internal/orchestrator/create/create.go:485`; `internal/orchestrator/create/prepare_profile.go:85,109`; `internal/orchestrator/create/prepare_profile_test.go:194`.
- **Fixed 2026-08-15** in `70a10af7`. `baseAgent` now falls back to the baked-in agent when the user's `defaults/config.yaml` is silent, so both sides of the comparison are resolved from the same layers the CLI used. `TestResolveProfileConfig_AgentPrecedence` was **rewritten, not extended**: its old form hand-built `ycfg = {Agent: "claude"}`, the one shape where the broken comparison happened to work, and its new default-install case is what goes red on revert.

### DF209 — `isolation` and `model` still carry from personal defaults into a profile, by a second route DF207/DF208 did not close (RESOLVED 2026-08-15)

- **Discovered:** 2026-08-14, while asking whether `IsolationExplicit` had any remaining purpose (DF205) · **Workstream:** config/trust seams
- **Severity:** MEDIUM (`isolation` selects gVisor, VM or `container-privileged`, so a personal default silently choosing the confinement mode of a profile sandbox is a security-relevant surprise)
- **Disposition:** **RESOLVED 2026-08-15.**
- **Rides:** **any**.
- **Description:** [DF207/DF208](findings-resolved.md) closed the leak that ran through the profile *merge base*. `isolation` and `model` reach the sandbox by a second route that fix does not touch. The CLI collapses flag and personal config into one value before the create pipeline runs — `isolation = Coalesce(FlagStr(cmd, "isolation"), cfgIsolation)` (`internal/cli/lifecycle/new.go:544`, and again at `internal/cli/cliutil/client.go:68`) — so `opts.Isolation` is non-empty whether the user typed `--isolation` or merely has `isolation:` in `~/.yoloai/defaults/config.yaml`. `applyCLIOverrides` then unconditionally overrides the profile's value (`internal/orchestrator/create/prepare_profile.go:255-257`, overwriting what `:165` set from the profile chain). `model` has the same shape at `:117` (`if opts.Model == "" && merged.Model != ""`): a personal `model:` makes `opts.Model` non-empty, so the profile's model never applies.
- **It cannot be fixed where it manifests.** The information needed — *did the user type the flag, or did config supply it?* — is destroyed at the CLI boundary by `Coalesce`, before `prepare_profile.go` is entered. No change inside the create pipeline can recover it. This is the same wall [DF205](#df205--isolationexplicit-is-written-never-read-and-wrong-anyway-resolved-2026-08-15) hit.
- **The `agent` key's comparison has the right shape, but it does not work.** `applyMergedProfileToOpts` carries the config value down separately so the two remain comparable — `ycfg.Agent` as `baseAgent`, tested against `opts.Agent == baseAgent` (`prepare_profile.go:109`) — which is the right *idea*: keep the config value in scope so it can still be compared once the CLI has collapsed flag-and-config into one string. But its two sides are drawn from different loaders (`config.LoadConfig` vs `config.LoadDefaultsConfig`), so on a default install `baseAgent` is never what `opts.Agent` actually resolved to and the guard never fires; see [DF213](#df213--a-profiles-agent-key-is-silently-ignored-on-a-default-install-resolved-2026-08-15). The consequence for this finding's own fix: making both sides read the same source is what makes the comparison trick usable — copying it as-is would only reproduce DF213 for a second key.
- **Shipped text was corrected rather than left overclaiming.** The `BREAKING-CHANGES.md` entry for DF207/DF208 originally said a profile "no longer inherits any setting" from personal defaults; it now names these two exceptions explicitly and points here.
- **Pointer:** `internal/cli/lifecycle/new.go:544`; `internal/cli/cliutil/client.go:68`; `internal/orchestrator/create/prepare_profile.go:117,165,255-257`; the working counter-example at `prepare_profile.go:109`; `docs/contributors/design/config.md:165,167`.
- **Fixed 2026-08-15** in `70a10af7`, together with [DF213](#df213--a-profiles-agent-key-is-silently-ignored-on-a-default-install-resolved-2026-08-15) — one defect in three keys. `model` and `isolation` are now compared against what config alone resolves to, so a personal value no longer reads as an explicit flag. `model` additionally takes the profile's value **even when empty**, so a personal `model:` is cleared rather than surviving. The `isolation` half is scoped to the profile path on purpose: suppressing it on the no-profile path would change which mode an unconfigured sandbox gets on backends whose base mode is not `container`, which is D143's work and needs its own breaking-change entry.
- **Residual, and it is why D143 exists:** an explicit flag whose value equals the resolved default is still indistinguishable from no flag. A comparison is standing in for provenance the pipeline does not carry.
- **Pinned by `TestArch_ProfileIgnoresPersonalDefaults`**, which dropped the DF209 exclusion its own comment predicted this fix would remove, and now builds `Options` the way the CLI would — carrying the personal values, because asserting against clean `Options` would pass while proving nothing.

### DF201 — `agent_files` list form copies credentials the string form strips (RESOLVED 2026-08-15)

- **Discovered:** 2026-08-13, during the config-key trust audit · **Workstream:** config/trust seams
- **Severity:** MEDIUM (list-form `agent_files` puts live credentials into the sandbox where the same key in string form would strip them)
- **Disposition:** **RESOLVED 2026-08-15.**
- **Description:** `CopyAgentFiles` branches on `expanded.IsStringForm()`. The string branch, `copyAgentFilesFromBaseDir`, applied the agent's `AgentFilesExclude` globs — the credential denylist (`internal/agent/agent.go:387` Claude excludes `.credentials.json`, `projects/`, `statsig/`; `:481` Gemini `oauth_creds.json`, `gemini-credentials.json`, `google_accounts.json`; `:604` Codex `auth.json`, `sessions/`; `:534` OpenCode the same). The list branch, `copyAgentFilesList`, **took no `spec` parameter and never called the exclusion filter at all** — it copied each entry verbatim with `workspace.CopyDir`/`copyFilePreserve`. So `agent_files: ["~/.claude"]` copied live credentials into the sandbox where `agent_files: "~"` would strip them.
- **The docs did not qualify the guarantee to one form.** `docs/GUIDE.md:723` states "Each agent excludes session data and caches" as a blanket property of `agent_files`. A user who picked the list form for its explicitness believed they were protected and were not.
- **Fixed.** `copyAgentFilesList` now takes the `EnvSpec` and applies `AgentFilesExclude`, so the denylist holds for both forms. A directory entry is walked by a new `copyDirExcluding` — the same filter and the same relative-path basis the string form uses — instead of a bare `workspace.CopyDir`. An entry naming an excluded path **directly** is refused too, with a `slog.Warn`, because that is how a config would ask for the credential file by name; a silently skipped entry the user explicitly listed would be its own surprise.
- **Pinned by `TestArch_AgentFilesNeverCopiesExcludedCredentials`**, cited from `principles/security-principles.md` under the one-convention rule, so the claim now fails `make check` rather than drifting. Verified red-on-revert in both directions: with the fix stashed, `.credentials.json` and `projects/` land in the sandbox's `agent-runtime/`.
- **The convention graduated out of this finding** (rule 7): the worked example lives in `security-principles.md` §"a security mechanism follows ONE convention", where it is the canonical instance — an audit reading *either* implementation alone finds it correct, and only the comparison shows the gap.
- **No `BREAKING-CHANGES` entry.** `docs/GUIDE.md:723` already documented the exclusions as a property of `agent_files` without qualifying them to a form, so nothing promised stopped working; what changed is that the promise became true on both paths.
- **Pointer:** `internal/envsetup/agent_files.go` (`CopyAgentFiles`, `copyAgentFilesList`, `copyDirExcluding`, `shouldExclude`); `internal/agent/agent.go:387,481,534,604`; `docs/GUIDE.md:723`.

### DF208 — the DF207 leak's sibling: restart/relaunch resolved `agent_args`, `agent_files`, and `env` for a profile-attached sandbox from the user's personal `defaults/config.yaml`, not baked-in defaults (RESOLVED 2026-08-13)

- **Discovered:** 2026-08-13, fixing DF207 · **Workstream:** config/trust seams
- **Severity:** MEDIUM
- **Rides:** any.
- **Description:** DF207 was the same shape at the `create` (`yoloai new`) call site. Three more call
  sites had it: `internal/orchestrator/lifecycle/lifecycle.go:106` (`resolveAgentArgs`) and
  `internal/orchestrator/lifecycle/restart.go:70,88` (`resolvedAgentFiles`, `resolveEnvForRestart`)
  each called `config.LoadConfig(layout)` — the personal `defaults/config.yaml` — and passed the
  result straight to `config.MergeProfileChain` as `base` whenever `meta.Profile != ""`, leaking
  `agent_args`, `agent_files`, and `env` on the restart/relaunch/attach path for any sandbox created
  with `--profile`. Left as a config-wide-shape defect (DF207's description already covered why:
  `mergedConfigFromBase` copies essentially the whole config out of `base`).
- **Why fixing this was urgent rather than parked:** `env`, `agent_args`, and `agent_files` are
  exactly the fields DF207's create-path fix could not persist into `environment.json` (they are
  recomputed on every restart, not stored). So between DF207 landing and this fix, a profile
  sandbox resolved clean values at create and personal-default-contaminated values on every restart
  afterward — the sandbox changed shape between its first launch and its second. That is a worse
  failure than the uniform leak DF207 fixed, which is why this could not stay parked.
- **The fix turned out to be two defects at one site, not one defect at three.** `resolveAgentArgs`
  and `resolveEnvForRestart` were a plain base-argument swap, exactly matching
  `prepare_profile.go`'s DF207 fix: both assign `merged.AgentArgs[...]` / `merged.Env` directly on
  success, so swapping `base` from the personal config to `config.LoadBakedInDefaults()` closes the
  leak outright. `resolvedAgentFiles` did not close with the same swap: `agent_files` is commented
  out of the baked-in defaults, so a profile that sets no `agent_files` of its own merges to a `nil`
  `MergedConfig.AgentFiles` regardless of `base` — and the pre-fix code specifically fell back to
  the *personal* `cfg.AgentFiles` whenever `merged.AgentFiles` was `nil`. A base-only swap was
  provably a no-op there: before and after, the function returned the personal value whenever the
  profile itself didn't set `agent_files`. Closing it required dropping that nil-fallback and
  returning `merged.AgentFiles` unconditionally on success, matching the unconditional assignment
  `prepare_profile.go:123` already used on the create path.
- **The chain-resolution/merge error fallback at `resolvedAgentFiles` also changed direction.** On a
  profile-chain error, the pre-fix code returned the personal `cfg.AgentFiles` — and `agent_files`'
  list form applies no credential-exclusion filter at all
  ([DF201](#df201--agent_files-list-form-copies-credentials-the-string-form-strips-resolved-2026-08-15)),
  so that fallback degraded a profile-active error into "copy the user's personal agent state,
  unfiltered, into a profile sandbox." The fix returns `nil` on error instead, when a profile is
  active; the no-profile path (`meta.Profile == ""`) is unaffected and still returns `cfg.AgentFiles`.
- **No new convention.** `config.md:165,167` already stated the rule correctly — "profile config
  merges over baked-in defaults only" and personal defaults "do not carry into profiles — no
  exceptions." The code was wrong at four call sites, not the doc, so nothing graduates out of this
  finding (or DF207's).
- **The original filing's option (a), caller-side, was taken over option (b), a library-side
  `MergeProfileChain` entry point that removes the `base` parameter's sharp edge entirely.** All
  four known call sites are now fixed, but the edge the filing named — `MergeProfileChain`'s
  signature accepts any `*YoloaiConfig` as `base` with nothing marking `LoadBakedInDefaults()` as
  the only doc-correct choice for a profile-active caller — is unchanged. A fifth caller could
  still repeat the mistake; that risk was named, not acted on, consistent with rule 7 leaving the
  choice to the owner.
- **Pins:** `TestResolveAgentArgs_PersonalDefaultsDoNotLeakIntoProfile`,
  `TestResolvedAgentFiles_PersonalDefaultsDoNotLeakIntoProfile` (fails on either half of that site's
  fix being reverted — the base swap or the nil-fallback removal),
  `TestResolvedAgentFiles_ProfileOwnValueStillApplies`,
  `TestResolvedAgentFiles_ErrorPathReturnsNilNotPersonalValue` (pins the error-direction change),
  `TestResolveEnvForRestart_PersonalDefaultsDoNotLeakIntoProfile`,
  `TestRestartPath_NoProfile_PersonalDefaultsStillApply` (no-profile regression guard),
  `TestRestartPath_ProfileResolution_MatchesCreatePathGuarantee` (restart-path resolution asserted
  against the same expectations as DF207's create-path headline test). All in
  `internal/orchestrator/lifecycle/profile_config_test.go`; verified red on revert per site.
- **Breaking change recorded** in `docs/BREAKING-CHANGES.md` under `## Unreleased`, extending the
  DF207 entry rather than duplicating it: the restart/relaunch path is now named alongside create.
- **Pointer:** `internal/orchestrator/lifecycle/lifecycle.go` (`resolveAgentArgs`);
  `internal/orchestrator/lifecycle/restart.go` (`resolvedAgentFiles`, `resolveEnvForRestart`);
  `internal/config/profile.go:493` (`MergeProfileChain`), `:374-419` (`mergedConfigFromBase`);
  `docs/contributors/design/config.md:165,167`; against DF207 (resolved, above).

### DF207 — personal defaults leak into profiles, contradicting a bold documented guarantee (RESOLVED 2026-08-13)

- **Discovered:** 2026-08-13, during the config-key trust audit · **Workstream:** config/trust seams
- **Severity:** MEDIUM
- **Rides:** any.
- **Description:** `docs/contributors/design/config.md:167` states, in bold: *"Personal defaults do not carry into profiles — no exceptions. When a profile is active, settings from `defaults/config.yaml` are completely ignored"*, and `:165` says profile config "merges over baked-in defaults only". The code did otherwise: `internal/orchestrator/create/create.go:~485` loaded the user's `defaults/config.yaml` via `config.LoadConfig` into `ycfg`; `internal/orchestrator/create/prepare_profile.go:~65` passed that `ycfg` as the `base` argument to `config.MergeProfileChain`.
- **The filed scope under-counted; the real leak was config-wide.** DF207 as originally filed named `Ports`, `CapAdd`, `Devices` and `Setup`. `mergedConfigFromBase` (`internal/config/profile.go:374-419`) in fact copies the whole config out of `base`: `Agent`, `Model`, `OS`, `ContainerBackend`, `TartImage`, `Isolation`, `AgentFiles`, `AutoCommitInterval`, `Env`, `AgentArgs`, `Resources`, `Network` (including the egress allowlist `Network.Allow`), `Ports`, `CapAdd`, `Devices`, `Setup` — so a profile could silently inherit not just privilege-bearing fields but the isolation mode and the egress allowlist from whichever machine ran it.
- **This is pre-existing and long-standing, not introduced by recent work.** The retired `mounts:` key had the identical characteristic before it was removed ([D142](../decisions/working-notes.md#d142--the-mounts-configprofile-key-is-retired-directories-is-its-strict-superset)) — the fix there was retiring the key entirely, which sidesteps this defect for that one field without addressing the general leak.
- **Fix.** `resolveProfileConfig` now builds the merge base from `config.LoadBakedInDefaults()` — the embedded `DefaultConfigYAML` — instead of the loaded `ycfg`, matching `config.md`'s documented guarantee exactly. No new convention: the doc already stated the rule correctly at `config.md:165,167`; the code was wrong, not the doc, so nothing graduates out of this finding.
- **`ycfg.Agent` as `baseAgent` is untouched and remains correct** — it is a different use, detecting whether the CLI overrode `--agent`, and precedence is unaffected: CLI `--agent` beats profile `agent:`, profile `agent:` beats a personal `agent:` default.
- **No test pinned the leak.** Neither `internal/orchestrator/create/create_prepare_test.go` nor `internal/config/profile_test.go` had a test asserting personal-config values reached a resolved profile; the `config.MergeProfileChain` unit tests construct their `base` argument as an opaque literal and correctly test the merge function's own contract, not the call site's choice of what to pass as `base`.
- **A sibling instance of the same shape was found and left unfixed — filed separately as DF208** (`findings-unresolved.md`). `internal/orchestrator/lifecycle/lifecycle.go:106` (`resolveAgentArgs`) and `internal/orchestrator/lifecycle/restart.go:70,88` (`resolvedAgentFiles`, `resolveEnvForRestart`) all load the user's `defaults/config.yaml` and pass it as `base` to `MergeProfileChain` on the restart/relaunch path, leaking `agent_args`, `agent_files`, and `env` — three of the fields `config.md` explicitly names as must-not-leak — whenever a profile-attached sandbox is restarted rather than created. Out of scope for this fix (task was scoped to the `create` path only); named per AGENTS.md rule 7.
- **Pins:** `TestResolveProfileConfig_PersonalDefaultsDoNotLeakIntoProfile` (the headline test — covers `cap_add`, `devices`, `setup`, `isolation`, `network.allow`, `env`, `agent_args`, `ports` in one profile resolution), `TestResolveProfileConfig_ProfileOwnValuesStillApply` (a profile's own settings still resolve), `TestResolveProfileConfig_NoProfileSeedsFromUserConfig` (the no-profile path is unaffected), `TestResolveProfileConfig_AgentPrecedence` (CLI > profile > personal default holds). All in `internal/orchestrator/create/prepare_profile_test.go`; verified red on revert.
- **Breaking change recorded** in `docs/BREAKING-CHANGES.md` under `## Unreleased`: a profile sandbox no longer inherits personal `env`, `cap_add`, `devices`, `setup`, `isolation`, `network.allow`, and the other fields listed above from `defaults/config.yaml`.
- **Pointer:** `docs/contributors/design/config.md:165,167`; `internal/orchestrator/create/prepare_profile.go` (`resolveProfileConfig`); `internal/config/profile.go:374-419` (`mergedConfigFromBase`); `internal/config/config.go:467-469` (`LoadBakedInDefaults`).

### DF162 — seatbelt's `:ro` mounts were not read-only whenever a broader rule granted write (RESOLVED 2026-07-30)

- **Discovered:** 2026-07-30, by un-skipping the mount conformance section on seatbelt ([DF161](#df161--mount-conformance-is-skipped-on-the-only-two-backends-whose-mounts-are-unusual-over-one-hardcoded-path-and-seatbelts-read-only-is-grant-omission-not-a-deny-resolved-2026-07-30)) · **Workstream:** backend conformance / sandbox-dir tiering
- **Severity:** HIGH (security), **seatbelt-only**, user-reachable without any compromise: `yoloai new --dir <path>:ro` gave the agent write access whenever `<path>` fell inside a broader grant. Not latent — no attacker needed, the confinement simply was not there.
- **Rides:** any.
- **What was wrong.** SBPL has no read-only primitive; seatbelt does not mount, it grants access to host paths in place. A `ReadOnly` MountSpec emitted `(allow file-read* (subpath src))` and nothing else — and an allow-read never matches a write, so it cannot override an earlier write grant. Read-only was therefore conditional on nothing else granting write, and the profile grants write broadly: `/tmp`, `/private/tmp`, `/private/var/folders`, `~/Library/Caches/org.swift.swiftpm`, `~/Library/Developer/Xcode`, the sandbox dir, and any enclosing read-write mount. The Xcode and temp cases are the realistic ones, and the Xcode one is squarely in this project's own iOS workstream.
- **Measured, on the real `--dir` path, before and after.** `TestSeatbelt_ReadOnlyMountHoldsUnderABroaderGrant` mounts a fixture under the per-user temp tree read-only and writes to it from a real sandbox-exec'd process. Before the fix the file came back reading `tampered`; after it, the write fails and the contents are unchanged.
- **Fix.** `writeProfileTrailingRules` emits an explicit `(deny file-write* (subpath …))` per read-only mount, in a trailing block after every allow. The block is **sorted by path depth** so the most specific rule matches last, which is what keeps nesting correct in both directions — a read-write dir inside a read-only one stays writable, a read-only dir inside a read-write one stays read-only. Grouping all denies then all allows would have got one of those two backwards. Confirmed against the kernel in both directions, not only in the profile text.
- **Why it survived this long.** The unit test asserting the old behaviour was correct and passed: it checked that a read-only mount produced no `file-write*` rule, which was true and was not the same claim as "a write is refused". The conformance section that *would* have caught it was skipped on seatbelt over an unrelated hardcoded path (DF161). A guarantee no backend-agnostic test covers is a guarantee held by whoever last read the code.
- **Graduated (D128 (3)):** the general rule — *a negative permission on seatbelt must be stated, positioned last, and ordered by specificity* — is in [backend-idiosyncrasies.md](../backend-idiosyncrasies.md#sbpl-has-no-read-only-mount--read-only-is-the-absence-of-a-write-grant-so-it-must-be-said-with-an-explicit-deny). The same shape produced the DF136 host-tier deny, and the two now share one mechanism.
- **Pointer:** `runtime/seatbelt/profile.go::writeProfileTrailingRules`; `runtime/seatbelt/integration_test.go`; `runtime/seatbelt/seatbelt_test.go`. Sibling: DF136 (same absence-of-grant shape, different path).

### DF161 — mount conformance is skipped on the only two backends whose mounts are unusual, over one hardcoded path; and seatbelt's read-only is grant-omission, not a deny (RESOLVED 2026-07-30)
- **Discovered:** 2026-07-30, rebasing `sandbox-share-tiering` onto main and looking for a home for the tiering invariant · **Workstream:** backend conformance / sandbox-dir tiering
- **Severity:** MEDIUM. Nothing is broken in the product today; the cost is that the guarantee is unverified exactly where it is least obvious, and (b) is a live constraint on a design already drafted.
- **Rides:** any — the harness half is test-only. The seatbelt half is a decision, not yet a change.
- **(a) One literal disables the section on two backends.** `runtime/runtimetest/conformance_iface.go` mounts at `/mnt/test` and then execs that same literal. Both macOS-family backends skip the whole `Mounts` section for that one reason: **seatbelt** is host-side, `/mnt` is not writable without root, so `mountSymlinks` silently declines to create the link (`runtime/seatbelt/seatbelt.go:646-648` documents this class for `/home`); **tart**'s macOS guest has no `/mnt` and cannot get one, the root volume being SIP-sealed. Tart's own skip string names the shared cause — *"same container-path assumption seatbelt skips"*. So mount conformance runs on the four container backends that already agree with each other, and skips the two whose mount semantics differ most, which is the population a conformance suite exists for.
- **The suite also bypasses the interface it certifies.** It execs the *requested* container path rather than asking the backend where the mount actually lands. `runtime.ResolveGuestMountPathFor` / `GuestMountResolver` (`runtime/runtime_optional.go:52-75`) exists for precisely this, tart implements it (`runtime/tart/tart.go:189`, re-rooting under `/Users/admin/host/...`), and production already treats "where does the guest see this?" as a question that must be *asked of the backend*: `setupAuxDir` records `MountPath: runtime.ResolveGuestMountPathFor(rt, …)` for every `rw`/`ro` dir, and its comment gives the reason — the recorded path feeds generated `CLAUDE.md`, `info` and the MCP `{dir:N}` placeholders, so advertising an untranslated path would point users at something absent in the guest (`internal/orchestrator/create/prepare_dirs.go:310-335`). The conformance suite asks the question the other way and answers it itself. A harness that certifies mount behaviour while bypassing the mount-path interface is testing its own assumption about the backend. Same shape as A15's by-catch, where the docker/podman conformance helper built `container.Config` through the SDK and had silently stopped covering `Labels`.
- **Measured on this machine (macOS, 2026-07-30), not predicted.** Moving the target to `/tmp/yoloai-conformance-mnt-{rw,ro}` and exec'ing through `ResolveGuestMountPathFor` un-skips seatbelt: `Mounts/ReadWrite` **passes**. Docker's `Mounts` passes unchanged under the same edit, so the indirection costs the container backends nothing. **Tart, run the same day** (`YOLOAI_TEST_TART_VM=1`, 63s, two real VM boots): un-skips and **both subtests pass**. So one hardcoded literal was the entire cause on both backends, and neither skip was hiding a capability gap — which is what made them cheap to keep.
- **(b) Un-skipping it immediately found something, and this is the half that matters.** With the suite's own fixture — `t.TempDir()`, i.e. `/var/folders/…` — `Mounts/ReadOnly` **fails: the write to the read-only mount succeeds.** Cause: `writeProfileSystemPaths` grants `(allow file-read* file-write* (subpath …))` over `/tmp`, `/private/tmp` and `/private/var/folders` (`runtime/seatbelt/profile.go:186-189`), while a `ReadOnly` MountSpec emits only `(allow file-read* (subpath src))` (`profile.go:205-216`). An allow-read does not revoke an allow-write, and last-match-wins only helps when the later rule is a **deny**. Re-run with `TMPDIR` outside that tree and both subtests pass. **So seatbelt's read-only enforcement is real, and its scope is conditional on nothing else granting write over the same path.**
- **Why (b) constrains the tiering design rather than just the test.** [sandbox-share-tiering.md](../archive/plans/sandbox-share-tiering.md)'s target seatbelt realization is exactly this shape — read on `ro/`, read+write on `rw/`, no rule for `host/`. That is sound only once the broad `(allow file-read* file-write* (subpath sandboxDir))` (`profile.go:193-200`) is gone, and it stays defeasible by any future broad grant that happens to cover the sandbox dir. Note the plan's confirmed-on-Tahoe evidence is about a `deny` overriding an earlier allow — a *different* mechanism from the allowlist the plan actually proposes, so it does not transfer. **Open decision:** whether `ro/` and `host/` carry explicit `(deny file-write* (subpath …))` as a backstop, making the tier robust against re-widening instead of merely correct today.
- **(c) The two backends the tiering plan converges do not enforce read-only the same way, and only running both shows it.** Tart's `ReadOnly` passes with the suite's ordinary `t.TempDir()` fixture; seatbelt's fails on it. That is not a test artefact — it is the mechanisms differing. Tart realizes read-only as a VirtioFS `:ro` **share**, which is a real read-only mount and holds regardless of what else is granted. Seatbelt realizes it as the **absence** of a write grant, which holds only while nothing else grants write. So "grant read on `ro/`" means something materially weaker on seatbelt than the same sentence means on tart, and the plan currently treats the two as one design.
- **A forward-looking detail from the tart run, which the `/tmp` target happens to hide.** `remapTargetPath` (`runtime/tart/mounts.go:22-37`) is the identity for `/tmp/...` — the test logged `mount requested at /tmp/yoloai-conformance-mnt-rw, guest path /tmp/yoloai-conformance-mnt-rw` — so this experiment would have passed on tart even without the resolver. It does **not** stay identity for the paths tiering will use: `/yoloai/...` is rewritten to `/Users/admin/.yoloai/...`. A tier conformance case that assumes the requested path will therefore be right on `/tmp` and wrong on `/yoloai`, i.e. it will pass here and fail once pointed at the thing it exists to check. Go through `ResolveGuestMountPathFor` even where it currently changes nothing.
- **Pointer:** `runtime/runtimetest/conformance_iface.go` (`Mounts`); `SkipMounts` in `runtime/seatbelt/integration_test.go` and `runtime/tart/integration_test.go`; `runtime/seatbelt/profile.go`. Bears on DF136 and DF148 and on the plan above. Fixing the suite is the prerequisite for putting the tiering invariant in it (GEN §18: converge on the mechanism that exists rather than adding a second one).
- **Resolved 2026-07-30, and it took two fixes because it was two problems.** The harness half: the mount section now binds under `/tmp` with a per-subtest suffix and execs `runtime.ResolveGuestMountPathFor(...)` rather than the requested path, and both `SkipMounts` are gone. The product half became [DF162](#df162--seatbelts-ro-mounts-were-not-read-only-whenever-a-broader-rule-granted-write-resolved-2026-07-30) — un-skipping seatbelt found that its read-only mounts were not read-only, which is a user-facing defect and not a test problem.
- **Verified on all six backends.** docker, podman, apple, seatbelt and tart on macOS; containerd (Kata runtime, as root) on Linux, `Mounts/{ReadWrite,ReadOnly}` green in 3.84s. The section that ran on four backends now runs on six, which was the point.
- **A predicted containerd failure that did not happen, recorded because the prediction was reasonable.** [Kata does not auto-create bind mount target directories](../backend-idiosyncrasies.md#kata-does-not-auto-create-bind-mount-target-directories) — a target absent from the image makes the mount *silently* fail. Neither the old `/mnt/test` nor the new `/tmp/yoloai-conformance-mnt-*` pre-exists, so the move looked capable of breaking containerd. It did not, and the reason took two probes and one retraction. The first guess — that `/tmp` is a tmpfs sitting off the virtiofs rootfs, so "targets must pre-exist" would not reach it — is **wrong**: inside a live Kata guest `/tmp` is not a separate mount, it is on the rootfs. The actual reason is the other branch: **the guest rootfs is mounted read-write** — `findmnt -no SOURCE,FSTYPE,OPTIONS /` reports `kataShared[/passthrough/<instance>/rootfs] virtiofs rw,nodev,relatime` — so kata-agent can create a target that is not in the image. The idiosyncrasy entry's stated mechanism ("the virtiofs-shared rootfs *may* be read-only at mount time") does not hold in this configuration, which is why its blanket rule did not bite. Recorded in [backend-idiosyncrasies.md](../backend-idiosyncrasies.md#kata-does-not-auto-create-bind-mount-target-directories) with the caveat that **the pre-created paths it mandates should not be removed on the strength of this** — the entry documents a real failure whose original conditions are not established here.
- **A measurement worth keeping as a specimen.** The probe was run as `mount | grep /tmp` from a shell whose cwd was `~/tmp`, so the one matching line was the **workdir bind at `/home/karl/tmp`** — `kataShared … virtiofs (rw,…)` — and not `/tmp` at all. Read quickly it looks like a direct answer about `/tmp`, and it is an answer about a different path that happens to contain the substring. Same shape as A13: a real command, run correctly, whose output did not mean what it appeared to.
- **The trap that was in this entry, discharged.** It warned that the `ReadOnly` assertion is only meaningful if its fixture sits outside every broad grant, since seatbelt's temp grant covers `t.TempDir()`. The eventual answer was not to move the fixture: it was to make seatbelt's read-only actually hold (DF162), which removes the constraint rather than working around it. The fixture stays where every other backend's is. Worth keeping as a specimen — the workaround was fully designed before the cause was looked at.
- **Convention graduated (D128 (3)):** *a negative permission on seatbelt must be stated, positioned last, and ordered by specificity* now lives in [backend-idiosyncrasies.md](../backend-idiosyncrasies.md#sbpl-has-no-read-only-mount--read-only-is-the-absence-of-a-write-grant-so-it-must-be-said-with-an-explicit-deny), not only here.

### DF158 — the base image pinned Node 20 on an unverified gVisor claim, capping Claude Code and shipping an EOL runtime (RESOLVED 2026-07-30)
- **Resolved 2026-07-30 — bumped to Node 22 LTS** (`ARG NODE_MAJOR=22`), restoring what `questions-resolved.md` #2 had decided and `standards/dockerfile.md` had documented all along. Verified on docker: node `v22.23.2`, and **Claude Code moved 2.1.197 → 2.1.220** on the same rebuild, so the cap is gone rather than merely widened.
- **Exercised the combination the old claim was about, as far as this hardware allows.** A `--isolation container-enhanced` (gVisor) sandbox came up clean — `sandbox.node_check` logged `v22.23.2 returncode=0`, `sandbox.ready` fired, no session death, no unsupported-syscall log; Claude 2.1.220 runs inside it; a JIT-heavy loop (the Maglev tier V8 12.4 enables, the one mechanism that plausibly fitted) completes normally. **This is amd64 and therefore not a test of the ARM64 claim**, which remains untestable here and unrefuted — it is simply also unevidenced.
- **The residual is documented where it will be met**, on the `ARG` itself: if gVisor on ARM64 does dislike Node 22 it surfaces in `container-enhanced` on that architecture alone, and `sandbox.node_check` already logs the node version before agent launch so it presents as a diagnosable failure rather than the silent session death that started this. Revisit with evidence, not with a hypothesis.
- **Recorded as user-visible**: `BREAKING-CHANGES.md` (Unreleased), which escalated the next release to `v0.11.0`. The escape hatch is a profile Dockerfile `FROM yoloai-base` installing the older runtime — *not* editing the base Dockerfile, which is embedded in the binary and not user-editable (an archived phase doc claims otherwise and is wrong; archives are not specifications).
- **Rides:** **breaking** — the sandbox's language runtime major changed.
- **The lasting lesson is [A18](../agent-failures.md)**, not the version number: an unfalsifiable diagnosis naming hardware nobody could check became a load-bearing pin and survived four and a half months, because nothing could dislodge it and it read as settled fact.

- **Discovered:** 2026-07-30, chasing why a freshly built base ships `claude-code` 2.1.197 when npm's `latest` is 2.1.220 · **Workstream:** profile-image lifecycle
- **Severity:** MEDIUM, and it grows on its own. The cap is permanent and widens with every Claude release.
- **Rides:** any.
- **The mechanism, and it is not what this entry first said.** `claude-code` 2.1.197 declares `engines.node >=18.0.0`; **2.1.198 onward declare `>=22.0.0`**. The base image pins `ARG NODE_MAJOR=20`. npm is behaving correctly — it installs the newest version whose engine constraint the image satisfies — so the image is frozen at 2.1.197 and every future Claude release widens the gap. Nothing is stale, cached or misconfigured. *An earlier version of this entry blamed BuildKit layer caching; that was wrong, and the cache is not the cause: a rebuild produces 2.1.197 either way.*
- **The other three agents are unaffected today:** `gemini-cli` needs `>=20`, `codex` `>=16`, `opencode-ai` declares none. So this is a Claude-only cap for now, and only because Node 20 happens to still clear the others' floors.
- **Raising the floor does not help; it makes it worse silently.** `npm install -g "<pkg>@>=2.1.200"` does install 2.1.220 — but only by ignoring the engine constraint, emitting `EBADENGINE` as a *warning* (`engine-strict` is false) and leaving a Node-22 package running on Node 20. It starts (`claude --version` works), so the failure would not be at install or at launch, but somewhere later and unattributable.
- **Node 20 reached EOL in April 2026**, and this repo decided against exactly that: `design/questions-resolved.md` #2 resolved the version as **Node 22 LTS**, in those words, *because* "Node 20 LTS reaches EOL April 2026". `standards/dockerfile.md` still documents Node 22. The Dockerfile says 20. The docs and the code have been contradicting each other since the downgrade, and the doc side was never updated — so a reader checking the standard is told the opposite of what ships.
- **What the pin actually rests on — and it rests on nothing observed (owner, 2026-07-30).** `bca8af21` (2026-03-17) states: *"Node.js 22 has syscall incompatibilities with gVisor ARM64 that cause silent immediate crashes with zero output, killing the tmux session before remain-on-exit fires."* **This project has never had a Linux ARM system.** Confirmed from the other side too: there is no ARM64 runner in any CI workflow, and the only ARM64 the repo touches anywhere is `darwin/arm64` — compile-only cross-lint (`LINT_PLATFORMS`) and the tart VM path on macOS. `linux/arm64` appears in no workflow, no matrix, no smoke tier. So the platform named in the diagnosis has never existed here, and the statement cannot have come from observation of it.
  The same commit's other changes suggest what probably *was* seen: it added a `node --version` probe logged as `sandbox.node_check` *"so gVisor syscall rejections surface immediately"*, and separately fixed a blind spot where a tmux session dying entirely went unrecorded. So a real symptom existed — silent session death — and "Node 22 + gVisor + ARM64" was the explanation attached to it, naming an architecture nobody could check. The probe was added because the cause could not be seen; the cause was then asserted anyway.
  Recorded at this length because the failure is not the wrong guess, it is that **an unfalsifiable diagnosis became a load-bearing pin and sat unexamined for four and a half months**, and would have kept sitting there — the Dockerfile comment reads as settled fact.
- **Researched 2026-07-30; no public corroboration exists, either way.** Searched the gVisor issue tracker, the Node issue tracker, gVisor's arm64 syscall table and general sources: nothing reports Node 22 crashing under gVisor on ARM64. Specifically ruled out: `membarrier` (gVisor #267 — p3, and gVisor's own note says callers warn and fall back rather than crash) and nodejs#62253 (an ARM64 heap-corruption report opened three days before our downgrade, which looks tempting and is not it — v24 only, gVisor never mentioned, closed "needs more info"). The one mechanism that fits the shape: **Node 22 ships V8 12.4, which enables the Maglev compiler by default on x64 and arm64** — a new JIT tier is a plausible source of an arch-specific fault under a re-implemented kernel, and it is the kind of thing that crashes without a syscall log line. Unconfirmed.
- **Absence of reports was the weakest part of the case, and is now moot.** It mattered only while the local claim was assumed sourced; with no such platform in the project, there is nothing to corroborate. For completeness: gVisor on ARM64 is a thin population, and this repo's combination (gVisor + ARM64 + Node under tmux) is thinner still. The honest position is that the claim is neither confirmed nor refuted.
- **It is not testable on the dev box.** That host is x86_64; Node 22 runs fine under `runsc release-20260622.0` there, which proves nothing about the claimed platform. Settling it needs ARM64 Linux with gVisor — reachable via a Linux VM on the Apple Silicon machine.
- **The decision, stated plainly:** we are holding every user on an EOL Node, and permanently capping Claude Code, to avoid a failure mode on a platform this project has never possessed, asserted without evidence, unsupported by any public report, in a non-default isolation mode. The risk side of this trade is not small — it is empty. Options: (1) retest on ARM64 and move to Node 22 if clean; (2) move to Node 22 regardless and treat gVisor-on-ARM64 as a known-risk combination, which the existing `sandbox.node_check` probe already instruments; (3) keep Node 20 and accept that Claude Code is frozen, which is not viable for long. **Not decided here** — it is an owner call with a real if unquantified risk on one side and a certain, growing cost on the other.
- **Pointer:** `runtime/docker/resources/Dockerfile` (`ARG NODE_MAJOR=20` and the agent install block); `bca8af21` (the downgrade); `docs/contributors/design/questions-resolved.md` #2 and `docs/contributors/standards/dockerfile.md` (both still say Node 22); `runtime/monitor/sandbox-setup.py` (`sandbox.node_check`). Sibling: [DF156](findings-resolved.md).

### DF156 — a `yoloai-base` rebuild did not invalidate profile images built on the old base (RESOLVED 2026-07-30)

- **Resolved 2026-07-30.** All three remedies landed, each where it fits, and the entry below is the working record that got there — including several corrections worth keeping.
  - **Rebuild**, on every path that creates a container. `EnsureProfileImage` seeds the chain checksum from the base image's own `yoloai.base.checksum` label, so a moved base changes every descendant's expected checksum by construction, at any depth. `ensureImageLineage` runs it on the **launch** path, not only at create, so `start` on a stopped or removed sandbox rebuilds too.
  - **Warn**, on the two paths that reuse an existing container — `Done`/`Failed` relaunch and suspended resume. Not a weaker substitute for the rebuild: no image is resolved there at all, so a rebuild would move the tag and leave the running container untouched. The only alternative is destroy-and-recreate, which discards whatever the user installed inside the sandbox, so it is offered rather than taken — the message names `yoloai restart`, which *is* the rebuild path above.
  - **Stop baking**, for the eleven `/yoloai/bin` scripts: delivered from the running binary and bind-mounted read-only on every launch, on the agent container and the firewall sidecar alike, so no rebuild is ever required for them and the missing-file class cannot arise.
  - **And the checksum split that follows**: those scripts left image identity, so a script-only release now rebuilds nothing and the lineage warning means real package drift.
- **Verified on docker 29.6.1, not reasoned.** Base Dockerfile edit → base rebuilt (`c0dcbd27…`→`eb14a98b…`), profile image invalidated and rebuilt (`yoloai.profile.checksum` `82fb701c…`→`93d29917…`, inherited base moved with it), container recreated on the new image. Script-only edit → nothing rebuilt, image keeps the old text, sandbox gets the new text, `start` silent. Profile image with `install-firewall.py` deleted → `--network-isolated` launches, firewall really installed.
- **What is NOT part of this, filed as [DF158](findings-unresolved.md):** nothing declares or checks a minimum agent-CLI version. That one survives every rebuild — a profile Dockerfile pinning an old `claude` stays pinned — so it is a different defect that this entry only mentioned in passing.
- **Rides:** **any** release. No flag, config key, default, or accepted input changed. One-time cost: the checksum split restales every existing `yoloai-base` once per host per backend.

- **Discovered:** 2026-07-29, while implementing DF152's label scheme · **Workstream:** profile-image lifecycle
- **Severity:** MEDIUM–HIGH, raised 2026-07-29 after tracing what the base actually contains. The original LOW–MEDIUM assumed the drift was silent. It is not always: when the base gains a *new* file the host later invokes, a profile sandbox fails hard at launch.
- **What is actually stale, verified.** `buildInputsChecksum` hashes 13 embedded files, and `runtime/docker/resources/Dockerfile` `COPY`s all of them into `/yoloai/bin/` and `/yoloai/tmux/`. Nothing bind-mounts over `/yoloai/bin`, so a profile image built `FROM yoloai-base` carries whatever version of the yoloAI *runtime scripts* the base had at that moment — and the host drives three of them by absolute path: `/yoloai/bin/install-firewall.py` (`launch.go`, the netns firewall sidecar), `/yoloai/bin/sandbox-setup.py` (the session runner), `/yoloai/bin/status-monitor.py` (`internal/agent/agent.go`). This is a host↔guest contract, and nothing versions it.
- **It fires on every upgrade, not rarely.** `EnsureProfileImage` calls `rt.Setup` first, so the base *does* rebuild when an embedded file changes — its staleness is tracked correctly. The profile's checksum is unchanged (its Dockerfile did not move), so it is skipped. The result is the failure mode inverted from what one would guess: **base and profile are guaranteed to disagree after any upgrade touching those 13 files**, with the new host driving old guest scripts.
- **The sharp case is a file being ADDED, and it has already happened once.** `install-firewall.py` entered the embed list in `b1f1c705` (tamper-resistant netns firewall). The sidecar spec is `Image: st.ImageRef` — the *sandbox's* image, i.e. the profile image — with `Argv: python3 /yoloai/bin/install-firewall.py`. Any profile image built before that commit lacks the file, so `yoloai new --profile … --network-isolated` runs a sidecar against an image that cannot satisfy it. A failed firewall install fails the launch by design (never run the agent unguarded), so this is a hard stop, and its symptom — `can't open file '/yoloai/bin/install-firewall.py'` — is the same string `backend-idiosyncrasies.md` already documents for a different cause, which will make it easy to misdiagnose. Whether a user hit it is unknown; the mechanism is not in doubt.
- **Disposition:** PARKED — pre-existing, not introduced by the label change, and deliberately not closed by it.
- **Description:** Profile staleness has never considered the base image. Under the old marker scheme the chain walk started with `prevDir = profiles/base`, and the parent check compared marker mtimes — but nothing ever writes a marker into `profiles/base` (the loop `continue`s on `base` before the record step), so `os.Stat` failed and the check returned "assume ok". It was inert for the common single-level profile, which is most of them. The label scheme preserves that behaviour exactly: `parentChecksum` starts at `""` for the root, so a profile's checksum folds in its own Dockerfile and its profile ancestors, and not the base's build inputs. Rebuild `yoloai-base` — a yoloAI upgrade with changed embedded resources does this — and every profile image still reports current while sitting on the previous base.
- **Why it was not closed here.** It is a one-line change under the new scheme: seed `parentChecksum` with the base's `buildInputsChecksum()` instead of `""`, and every profile rebuilds when the base does, at any depth. That is almost certainly the correct semantics. It is also a **behaviour change with real cost** — every yoloAI upgrade that touches an embedded resource would rebuild every profile image on next use, which for a chain of heavy data-science profiles is minutes of surprise. DF152's scope was moving the answer onto the image, not changing which question is asked, and bundling the two would have made a slow rebuild look like a regression from the label work.
- **The invariant is real and the project agrees with it — which is the point.** "The image must match the binary that launches it" is the intended contract, and it is enforced in two places: the *base* image carries `buildInputsChecksum` as a label/marker so `Setup` rebuilds it when the binary's embedded scripts change, and the runtime-config format is guarded end to end by `RUNTIME_CONFIG_SCHEMA_VERSION` (`runtime/monitor/setup_helpers.py`) against `runtimeconfig.SchemaVersion`, held together by `TestSchemaVersion_GoPythonAgreement`, a cross-language fence. So the contract is not folklore; it is stated by two mechanisms.
- **It is not enforced where the scripts actually come from.** For the four image backends the scripts a sandbox runs are the ones in its *profile* image, inherited through `FROM`, and nothing checks those against the binary. The schema fence is also narrower than it looks: it fires only when someone bumps the schema, so it catches a changed config *format* and not a file that was added (`install-firewall.py`), an argv contract that moved, or a behavioural change inside a script. And when it does fire its remedy — "re-create the sandbox" — does not work here: re-creating from the same stale profile image yields the same stale script. The user would need `--rebuild`, which the message does not say.
- **Taking the invariant seriously is what rules out remedy (a).** If these scripts are yoloAI's internal implementation — and they are; a user has no business customising `install-firewall.py` — then they are not part of what the user's Dockerfile builds, and they have no business in the user's image identity.
