Baking them into the base and inheriting them through `FROM` is precisely what couples *our* implementation changing to *the user's* image being stale. Rebuilding the profile on a base change therefore charges the user a full rebuild of their apt installs because our script moved, which is the wrong party paying. Delivering at launch (remedy c) makes the invariant structural instead: the scripts always come from the running binary, so they cannot mismatch, and the user's image contains only the user's things. That is also why tart, which already delivers at launch, has never had this problem.
- **The finding splits in two, and only one half is unusual (owner, 2026-07-29).** Baking things into a base and having downstream images inherit them is ordinary image semantics: if the base moves, downstream is stale, and that is a cost to bear rather than a defect. The question is which of the base's contents earn an exception, and the criterion is **direction of coupling**, not how often they change.
  - **Downstream-coupled content** — apt packages, the Go toolchain, node — is what the user's layers are *built against*. A change there genuinely makes their image stale and a rebuild is the correct answer. `buildInputsChecksum` hashes the base **Dockerfile** too, so this half is real and stays: it is the ordinary case, and remedies (a) rebuild or (b) warn are the live question for it.
  - **Sideways-coupled content** — the 13 embedded scripts — is what the *host process* is built against. No user layer references `install-firewall.py`; nothing in their Dockerfile is compiled against it. These sit in the image only because the image is a convenient delivery vehicle. That is the half that produces hard launch failures, and the half remedy (c) removes.
  Change frequency is why the second half hurts often enough to be noticed, not why it is different — if these scripts changed rarely the same defect would exist and be *harder* to diagnose, because no one would have the pattern in mind.
- **So (c) does not close this finding on its own**, and calling it done would be wrong. It removes the sharp class — a missing file, a moved argv contract — and leaves the ordinary one: a base Dockerfile change still leaves profile images on the old base. That residue fails as drift rather than as a failed launch, so it is much less urgent, but it is what (a)/(b) would still be deciding. A natural consequence is that `buildInputsChecksum` wants splitting to match: a script portion that no longer needs to participate in image identity at all, and an image-content portion that does.
- **Why a rebuilt base does not reach existing profile images** (asked, and worth writing down because the intuition runs the other way). `FROM yoloai-base` is resolved **when the profile is built**, not when it runs. The resulting profile image is a complete, immutable image whose lower layers *are* the base's layers as they were at that moment, referenced by digest. Rebuilding `yoloai-base` re-points the **tag** at a new image; images already built do not follow tags. Demonstrated directly: build `base` with `/marker=v1`, build a profile `FROM` it, then rebuild only the base with `/marker=v2` — the base reads v2 and the profile still reads v1. So the only thing that moves a profile onto a new base is rebuilding the profile, and nothing triggers that.
- **And the manual recovery is narrower than it looks.** `yoloai system build --all` does *not* refresh profiles after a base change: `runSystemBuildAll` passes the `--rebuild` flag through as `force`, so without it each profile's checksum still matches and it is skipped. Refreshing requires `--rebuild` explicitly (`yoloai system build <profile> --rebuild`, or `--all --rebuild`). A user who notices the drift and reaches for the most-thorough-looking command gets a no-op.
- **The coupling is not limited to the 13 scripts, and the rest cannot be delivered at launch (owner, 2026-07-29).** The host also depends on *packages* in the image: `python3` as the interpreter it invokes the scripts with, `tmux` as the session mechanism it drives via exec, and the agent CLIs (`claude`, `codex`, `gemini`) whose flag surfaces `internal/agent/agent.go` hardcodes. A release that starts passing a flag only a newer agent CLI accepts will misbehave against a profile image pinned to the old one. None of that is fixable by mounting: an npm-installed CLI with native dependencies cannot be bind-mounted in from a host of a different OS. **For the package half, rebuild is the only remedy** — which is the "downstream-coupled" content, behaving exactly as image semantics say it should, except that the failure is a malfunction rather than mere drift.
- **Nothing declares a floor, either.** The agent definitions carry no minimum CLI version and nothing checks one, so a host that outruns the agent in an old image has no guard: the failure surfaces as the agent behaving oddly, not as a legible version error.
- **But detection is already free, which was not obvious and changes the design.** **Image labels are inherited through `FROM`** — verified directly: build a base with `--label yoloai.base.checksum=BASEV1`, build a child `FROM` it with no label flags, and the child reports `yoloai.base.checksum:BASEV1`. So a profile image *inherits* whatever checksum label its base carried. **Corrected: that is only true on docker/podman today.** `runtime/docker/build.go`'s `buildBaseImage` passes `--label baseChecksumLabel=…`; containerd's `buildDockerImage` (`runtime/containerd/image.go`) and apple's `buildBaseImage` pass **no `--label` at all**, so their base images carry no checksum and their profile images inherit nothing to compare. Stamping it there is small — both already compute the value for their host-side marker — but it is work, not a property we already have. And on every backend the *check* is unwritten: nothing reads the inherited label or acts on it. Comparing that inherited value against the current `buildInputsChecksum()` answers "was this profile built on an older base?" exactly, and it covers packages too, since the checksum includes the base Dockerfile that installs them. It also composes with this finding's sibling work: a profile image now carries *both* `yoloai.base.checksum` (inherited, which base) and `yoloai.profile.checksum` (stamped, which Dockerfile chain).
- **That makes remedy (a) the wrong implementation of the right idea.** Seeding `chainChecksum` with `buildInputsChecksum()` infers the base from the binary; reading the inherited label states what the image actually *contains*. The label is the more truthful source and needs no change to the checksum scheme at all.
- **So the likely end state is a split remedy, not one of the three:** deliver the sideways-coupled scripts at launch (structural, no rebuild ever), and use the inherited base label to detect package-level drift — then warn or rebuild, which is the decision that remains. The detection half is close to free; only the policy is open.
- **Where the auto-rebuild actually runs, which decides where this check belongs.** yoloAI does auto-rebuild, so this is a refinement rather than new machinery — but `EnsureProfileImage` is reached from only three places: the **create** path (`internal/orchestrator/create/prepare_profile.go`), **`system build`** (`system.go`), and the DF154 recovery path in `launch.go`, which fires *only* when `Create` fails with a missing image. **`start` and `restart` evaluate image freshness nowhere.** A stopped sandbox is removed and recreated (`handleStoppedOrRemovedStatus`), so it does pick up whatever the recorded tag currently points at — but nothing on that path rebuilds anything, so if no other command has triggered a rebuild it recreates from the *old* image; and a suspended sandbox is resumed in place (`handleSuspendedResume`), keeping the filesystem it already has.
- **So "check at launch" is not where the code checks today.** After an upgrade, `yoloai start <existing-sandbox>` runs the new binary against whatever is in the store, with no freshness question asked at all. Implementing the check at create only would leave every pre-existing sandbox — the long-lived ones, which is most of them for this tool — silently on the old contract. If the intent is that a mismatched image cannot be launched, the check has to sit on the launch path, not just the create path, and it has to be able to say something useful for the resume case, where rebuilding the image does not change the container that already exists.
- **The intended design (owner, 2026-07-29), and it is implementable with data that exists today.** Deliver the scripts at launch; and check the *resolved* image close to the point of launching it, however the launch was reached, rather than at create. If the image descends from `yoloai-base`, verify it descends from the *right* one. Verified against real images built by the current binary — a profile image carries all three labels it needs:
  ```
  com.yoloai.managed      : true                 (inherited — "this is ours")
  yoloai.base.checksum    : 4e27711a…            (inherited — WHICH base it descends from)
  yoloai.profile.checksum : 8063007c…            (its own Dockerfile chain)
  ```
  So one check covers both shapes: a profile sandbox reads the inherited value, and a bare-base sandbox reads the base's own — the same label, no special case. `com.yoloai.managed` answers "is this ours" without inferring from the name.
- **Absence of the label must read as stale, not as "not ours".** An image built before the scheme, or by a backend that does not yet stamp, has no `yoloai.base.checksum`. Treating that as "not ours, skip the check" reintroduces exactly the bug; treating it as stale costs one rebuild on first use after upgrade. The base path already set this precedent — `checksumLabelStale` documents that a missing label reads as stale and rebuilds once.
- **What is still needed to build it:** (1) containerd's and apple's *base* builds must stamp `yoloai.base.checksum` — they compute the value already for their host-side marker, they just do not label with it; (2) the check itself, on the launch path; (3) an answer for **resume**, where the container already exists and rebuilding the image changes nothing — that case can only warn or refuse, never self-heal; (4) a small interface widening, because `ProfileImageChecksum(ctx, tag)` reads one hard-coded label and the check needs a second from the same image. `ImageLabels(ctx, tag) (map[string]string, bool)` is the natural shape, keeps the interface at two methods, and would also absorb docker's separate `baseImageStale` reader.
- **Partly built 2026-07-29.** The base-lineage half is implemented: `ImageLabels(ctx, tag) (map, bool)` replaces the single-label reader on all four backends; containerd's and apple's base builds now stamp `yoloai.base.checksum` (they computed the value already and simply did not label with it); the profile chain is **seeded with the base image's own label** rather than `""`, so a base rebuild invalidates every profile built on the previous one *through the existing mechanism*, with no `force` and therefore no gratuitous base rebuild; and `ensureImageLineage` runs this check on the **launch** path, not only at create, so a sandbox created before an upgrade is brought up to date when it is next started. Verified by recomputing the checksum both ways against a real built image: the stamped value matches `chain(seeded with base)` and not `chain(seeded with "")`.
- **A bug this nearly shipped, worth recording.** The first version of `ensureImageLineage` called `EnsureProfileImage` unconditionally. For a sandbox with **no** profile that resolves an empty profile chain and errors, so *every non-profile launch would have failed*. The create path never hits it because it returns early on `Profile == ""` — an implicit guard that does not transfer. Now explicit, and all three shapes are verified end to end on a real daemon: no-profile create, stop/start, and profile create.
- **Still open: the resume case — but it needs no new metadata, which corrects an earlier note here.** This entry previously said detection would require recording the base checksum in `environment.json` at create. It does not. **The container already records the image it was created from**, by ID rather than by tag: `docker container inspect` reports `.Image` (`sha256:…`) alongside `.Config.Image` (the tag). Reading *that* image's labels gives the lineage the container actually contains, which is exactly the right question and is immune to the tag having been re-pointed since. The container's own record beats a note written alongside it.
  This also dissolves the false-negative worry: comparing the *tag's* current labels would agree after an upgrade with nothing rebuilt, but the container's image ID names the specific image it holds. **Correction, verified on this host:** the id is *not* guaranteed to stay resolvable — `docker image inspect` on the live sandbox's id fails with `No such image`, and it is absent from `docker images -a`. Docker kept the layers alive for the running container and dropped the image record. So "read the old image's labels" is not always available.
  That does not break the design, because every branch lands on the right answer: image record **gone** → cannot vouch → warn; present and **matching** → quiet; present and **differing** → warn; present but **unlabelled** → warn, by the absence rule. The route to the answer differs, the answer does not — and for the live sandbox above it is the first branch, not the fourth as first written here.
  **Observed live on this host, on a real sandbox** (read-only, nothing modified): a 7-day-old sandbox's container reports image `sha256:24f46d20…`, that image carries **no** `yoloai.base.checksum` at all, and the current base carries `ee6c8d19…`. Under the agreed rule — absence means out of date — it would warn. So the defect is not merely constructible; there is a live instance of it.
- **The `ImageID` attempt, 2026-07-29 — withdrawn 2026-07-30, and worth keeping because every one of its four defects was invisible from inside the design.** `runtime.InstanceInfo` gained an `ImageID` string, populated per backend, with `warnIfImageLineageStale` resolving it back through `ImageLabels`. Six unit tests passed. It never ran once.
  - **It was unreachable.** It hung off `handleSuspendedResume`, i.e. `StatusSuspended`, which is set only by `InstanceInfo.Suspended`, which only **tart** ever sets — and tart implements no `ProfileImageBuilder`, so the function's first line returned on every backend, always. The intersection of "reports Suspended" and "has images" is empty. Worse, yoloAI never suspends anything: `tart.Stop` is a deliberate hard stop (VZ cannot restore VirtioFS snapshots), so the status needs an out-of-band `tart suspend` to occur at all.
  - **The reachable case was never wired.** `StatusDone`/`StatusFailed` → `handleTerminalStatus` → `relaunchAgent` → `tmux respawn-pane` **inside the existing container**, re-resolving no image. That is a container-backend sandbox that finished a task, sat through an upgrade, and is asked to run another — the ordinary life of a long-lived sandbox, and the actual target.
  - **The tests could not have caught either**, because `lineageFake` reported `Suspended: true` *and* implemented `ProfileImageBuilder` — a shape no backend has. A fake free to invent a capability combination the product does not contain will certify a dead path as working.
  - **Two of the three backends could not answer.** Verified on macOS 26.5.1: apple's digest is at `configuration.image.descriptor.digest`, not `…image.digest`, so the field read was always empty and always fell through to the **reference** — a tag, the one value the design forbade. And fixing the path would not have helped: `container image inspect <digest>` returns `image not found`, and rebuilding a tag **deletes** the superseded image rather than leaving it dangling as docker does, so its labels are unrecoverable in principle. containerd's `ctr.Image()` yields a *name*, which `ImageLabels` re-resolves — reading whatever the tag points at now, i.e. the very question the id was meant to avoid.
- **Correction to the field evidence recorded above.** The live sandbox whose id failed `docker image inspect` was **not** a tag-move case; that image had been deleted. Verified directly on docker 29.4.0/overlay2: build a tag, create a container, rebuild the tag — the container's `.Image` pins the old id, the old image survives as dangling, and inspecting it returns the *old* labels. Docker's branch worked as designed. The finding generalised one pruned image into "the id is not guaranteed to stay resolvable" and built a branch on it. The branch is still right for a genuinely deleted image; its stated evidence was the wrong scenario.
- **Resume/relaunch half built 2026-07-30, on the instance's own labels instead.** The shape was the defect, not the backends: asking for an opaque identifier and then resolving it through a second call means three backends put three incomparable things in one string, and the caller cannot tell which it holds. So `InstanceInfo.ImageID` became **`InstanceInfo.Labels`** — the instance's container labels, which every backend can both write and read, and which need no resolution step at all. This is DF152's own argument ("a label cannot outlive its image, because it *is* the image") applied one level down: a container label cannot outlive the container.
  - `instanceLabels` stamps `yoloai.base.checksum` at create, read off the image `ensureImageLineage` has just made current — observed rather than asserted, so an image carrying something unexpected is preserved as evidence.
  - Docker folds an image's labels into its containers' at create (verified), so on docker the value arrives inherited and **pre-existing sandboxes need no migration**. Apple does not (a container from a labelled `yoloai-base` reports `labels: {}`), so there the stamp is the only source.
  - The comparison is against **`ExpectedBaseChecksum()`** — a new third method on `ProfileImageBuilder` returning `buildInputsChecksum()` — never against the base tag in the store. Reading the tag agrees with the sandbox exactly when nothing has been rebuilt yet, which is the state every upgrade *starts* in, so it would stay silent through the commonest instance of the drift it looks for. Switching to the id had fixed only the tag-moved half; this is the half that remained. Note this introduces no churn: the checksum is a content hash of 13 embedded files, so a rebuilt binary with unchanged resources yields an identical value, and the comparison is literally the expression `baseImageStale` already evaluates — "is this stale?" and "would this binary rebuild the base?" are one predicate.
  - It runs on `handleTerminalStatus` (Done/Failed) as well as the resume path, and **warns only**: a rebuild changes the tag, not the container that is about to run. The remedy named is **`yoloai restart <name>`** with the bare sandbox name — `Restart` is Stop+Start, which lands on the stopped branch and re-creates through `ensureImageLineage`, keeping the host-side sandbox directory. The first message said `yoloai destroy <instance-name>`, which both discarded the sandbox needlessly and printed a principal-prefixed name no yoloAI command accepts.
  - The standing guard is a **conformance** case, not a unit test: `runtimetest`'s `InstanceLabelsRoundTrip`, gated on `ProfileImageBuilder`, asserts Create's labels come back from Inspect. No fake can satisfy it. Verified failing-on-revert against the live apple backend. It also exposed that the docker/podman conformance helper builds its `container.Config` via the SDK and silently dropped `Labels`, so that field was uncovered there.
- **Reevaluated: launch should NOT switch to the instance record, and that is not a compromise.** The two paths ask genuinely different questions. At create or recreate the container does not exist yet and `Create` resolves the **tag**, so "is the image I am about to use current?" is a question about the tag — which is what `ensureImageLineage` already compares, and it can *act* on the answer by rebuilding. Where a container already exists, the only meaningful question is "what is this holding?", and only its own record answers that; there nothing can be fixed, so it warns.
- **Three ways out, and the third is the one worth arguing about:**
  - **Rebuild.** Seed `parentChecksum` with `buildInputsChecksum()` instead of `""`. One line, correct, and the "cost" is not a regression — if the scripts changed, the profile image genuinely *is* stale. The real cost is that `FROM yoloai-base` moving invalidates every layer above it, so it is a full profile rebuild (apt installs and all), on the next use after every upgrade.
  - **Warn.** Fold the base checksum in but report a mismatch instead of acting on it, leaving the user to run `--rebuild`. Cheap, honest, and it converts a silent skew into a visible one — but it leaves a broken launch broken for the added-file case.
  - **Stop baking the scripts in — and note that half the backends already don't.** *Corrected 2026-07-29:* this was first written as "the most invasive" option on the grounds that VM backends have no host bind mount to lean on. That is wrong, and backwards. **tart already delivers at launch**: `runtime/tart/mounts.go`'s `writeVMSetupScripts` writes `sandbox-setup.py`, `setup_helpers.py`, `tmux_io.py` and `status-monitor.py` from the embedded copies into the sandbox dir on every launch, and execs them from there — so tart is *structurally immune* to this finding. Seatbelt has no container at all and runs the same scripts as host processes. Only the four image backends bake, and they are exactly the four with the bug.
    So this is not a new mechanism to invent; it is **making the container backends do what tart already does**, and the divergence between the two delivery styles is itself the pattern this workstream keeps hitting — a mechanism on one path and not its sibling.
    Mechanically it is a **mount, not a copy into the container**: the host materialises the embedded scripts into a directory once per binary version (the shape `dockerrt.WriteBuildContextDir` already uses for the build context) and bind-mounts it read-only at `/yoloai/bin`. Mounts are resolved before the entrypoint executes, so even `entrypoint.sh` can come from it. The precedent is in-tree and exact: **`tmux.conf` is already both baked and mounted** — `COPY`'d into the image as a default and bind-mounted at `/yoloai/tmux/tmux.conf` when a host copy exists (`internal/orchestrator/mounts/mounts.go`), with a comment already handling the virtiofs uid/permission wrinkle on the VM path. Keep the bake as the fallback, add the mount as the truth, and the class disappears with no rebuild ever required.
    Remaining cost to price honestly: one more mount on every sandbox, a host-side materialisation directory to place and reclaim, and confirming the read-only mount behaves on apple and containerd (docker/podman are the same shape as the existing `/yoloai/*` mounts).
    **Two of those costs are now measured, on Linux/docker 29.6.1.** The read-only bind over `/yoloai/bin` does supply the container's own `ENTRYPOINT` — mounts resolve before the process starts, verified by binding a directory whose `entrypoint.sh` ran in place of the image's — and writes to it are refused. And there is no materialisation directory to place or reclaim: `<sandboxDir>/bin` **already exists for every sandbox** (`config.BinDirName`, created with the rest of the sandbox dir) and is already where tart and seatbelt write these same scripts. It is simply empty on the container backends. So the work is to populate a directory that is already there and already has a name, which is the same convergence this remedy is about.
    Note also that the bake is a **weaker fallback than it sounds.** Keeping the `COPY`s protects backends that do not mount; it does not protect a mount that resolves badly, because the mount shadows the baked copy completely — a bad mount means the container cannot start at all. That is arguably the right direction (fail closed), but it is not belt and braces.
- **Not a security fix, and the record matters more than the conclusion (owner, 2026-07-30).** It is tempting to add a second justification here, because `/yoloai/bin` is in fact **writable by the sandbox user**: the base Dockerfile runs `chown -R yoloai:yoloai /yoloai` and then `COPY`s the scripts in as root, and directory ownership governs create and delete regardless of file ownership. Demonstrated live as uid 1001 — a direct write to `status-monitor.py` is refused, `rm` followed by recreate succeeds, and `install-firewall.py` can be replaced with a no-op that exits 0.
  **That is not an argument for the mount.** These scripts run *as the agent's own principal*, so anything the agent gains by rewriting one it gains by killing the process and running its own copy; read-only raises the effort by nothing. The only shape that would make it a real control is a file the agent can write that a *more privileged* principal later executes, and that set is empty here, checked: `install-firewall.py` runs in a sidecar created from `Image: st.ImageRef`, a fresh container off the image rather than the agent's writable layer; live allowlist patches exec as root but pass a host-authored Go string, never a path under `/yoloai/bin`; `runtime-config.json` is already mounted read-only; and every post-launch use of a `/yoloai/bin` script (`status-monitor.py` from the agent's own notification command, `agent-run.sh`, `yoloai-resume`, `diagnose-idle.sh` which is suggested *to* the agent) runs as `yoloai`.
  Recorded because a rejected rationale is invisible in the result: the next contributor to notice those permissions will reach for the same inference, and the mount will be sitting there looking like it was built for that reason. The grounds that do hold are the ones above — a missing file is a hard launch failure, and baking couples a yoloAI release to a full rebuild of the user's image. [A16](../agent-failures.md).
- **Remedy (c) built 2026-07-30 — scripts are delivered at launch, and the sharp case is closed.** The eleven `/yoloai/bin` scripts are materialised from the running binary into `<sandboxDir>/bin` on **every** launch and bind-mounted read-only at `runtime.RuntimeScriptDir`. Gated on a new optional capability `runtime.RuntimeScriptProvider`, implemented by the four image backends (containerd and apple delegating to the docker package's embeds, as they already do for `ExpectedBaseChecksum`); tart and seatbelt deliberately do not implement it, because they already deliver at launch and had nothing to convert. Mechanism chosen per backend rather than imposed: all four already translate a read-only `MountSpec` (apple as `-v …:ro`, containerd via `oci.WithMounts`) and all four already receive a read-only file mount into `/yoloai/` for `tmux.conf`, so the path was exercised in production before this used it.
  - **The load-bearing half is the sidecar, and a mount alone misses it.** A sidecar runs from an *image* and inherits none of the target's mounts, so delivering the scripts to the agent container leaves the firewall installer reading whatever the profile image baked — which is this finding's hard-failure case, not its drift case. `NetnsSidecarSpec` therefore gained `Mounts`, and docker's runner applies them. Without that field the remedy demonstrates as working (fresh scripts visible in the agent container) while the only path that fails a launch stays broken.
  - **Verified end to end on docker 29.6.1 by reproducing the finding.** A profile image built `FROM yoloai-base` with `RUN rm /yoloai/bin/install-firewall.py /yoloai/bin/status-monitor.py` stands in for one built before those files entered the embed set. `yoloai new --profile … --network-isolated` against it: **before** the sidecar mount, the launch fails with `python3: can't open file '/yoloai/bin/install-firewall.py'` after three retries — the exact string this entry predicted, and the one `backend-idiosyncrasies.md` documents for an unrelated cause; **after**, the sandbox comes up, `/yoloai/bin` is a read-only bind carrying both scripts, and the netns holds real `OUTPUT` rules plus the `allowed-domains` ipset.
  - **One list, not four.** `buildInputsChecksum`, `createBuildContext` and the new writer were three hand-maintained copies of the same thirteen files; they now share `baseBuildInputs()`. Order in that table is load-bearing — it is the hash order, and the hash is the base image's identity label, so reordering silently restales every `yoloai-base` in existence. Two tests parse the Dockerfile and assert its `COPY … /yoloai/bin/` set and its `chmod +x` set match the table, which is the drift that produced this finding in the first place and the one thing the shared table still cannot catch by itself.
  - **Not closed by this.** The ordinary half remains: a base *Dockerfile* change (apt packages, toolchains, agent CLIs) still leaves profile images on the old base, and that content genuinely cannot be delivered at launch. It now fails as drift rather than as a failed launch, and the detection half above reports it.
- **Checksum split done 2026-07-30, in its own commit — and the split is smaller than this entry predicted.** `buildInputsChecksum` now hashes only `imageIdentityInputs()`: the Dockerfile and `tmux.conf`. The eleven `/yoloai/bin` scripts are out, because they are delivered from the binary and mounted over on every launch, so the copies in the image are never the ones that run and hashing them charged the user a full rebuild of their apt layers whenever a yoloAI Python file moved.
  - **There is no second, "script portion" checksum**, which this entry had assumed there would be. Nothing would read it — delivery is unconditional, so the delivered scripts are current by construction — and a value computed for symmetry with no consumer is the speculative API D125 forbids. The split is a subtraction, not a partition.
  - **`tmux.conf` stays on the identity side, and it looks like it should not.** It is a yoloAI-owned file like the scripts and is materialised host-side into `defaults/` — but its mount is *config-gated*: under `tmux_conf: host`, `/yoloai/tmux/tmux.conf` is not mounted and the baked copy is the one that runs. Unconditional delivery is what earns a file the exclusion, not being ours.
  - **Costs one rebuild, once.** Changing the expression changes the value, so the first launch after upgrading past this restales every existing `yoloai-base` and rebuilds it per host per backend — the same one-time price the missing-label rule already accepts.
  - **Verified end to end on docker, both directions.** After a script-only edit and a binary rebuild: the base image is *not* rebuilt (label unchanged), the image's baked copy still holds the old text, the sandbox's `/yoloai/bin` holds the **new** text, and `yoloai start` is silent. After a *Dockerfile* edit: `yoloai start` warns. That is precisely the semantics the split was for — the lineage warning now means real package drift rather than "yoloAI edited a Python file".
- **Pointer:** `internal/orchestrator/profiles/profile_build.go` (`EnsureProfileImage`, the `parentChecksum := ""` seed and `chainChecksum`); `runtime/docker/build.go` (`baseBuildInputs`, `buildInputsChecksum`, `WriteRuntimeScripts`); `runtime/runtime_optional.go` (`RuntimeScriptProvider`, `NetnsSidecarSpec.Mounts`); `internal/orchestrator/launch/launch.go` (`deliverRuntimeScripts`, `installFirewallSidecar`). Sibling: [DF152](#df152--profile-image-staleness-could-not-travel-with-the-image-because-the-interface-took-neither-a-context-nor-a-tag-resolved-2026-07-29).

### DF157 — on the restart path every line of launch progress was rendered as a warning (RESOLVED 2026-07-30)

- **Resolved 2026-07-30** — `recreateContainer` set `state.State.Output` to `&noticeWriter{notices: n, level: NoticeWarn}`, so *every* line any launch helper wrote to that stream became a `NoticeWarn`. `st.Output` is not a warning channel: it is the launch path's general progress stream, and of its four consumers only two warn (`filterAvailablePorts`, and the adjacent advisory loop) while the other two stream an image build. `noticeWriter` now reads each line's level from the line, keying on `launch.WarningPrefix` and stripping it.
- **Measured, not reasoned.** `yoloai restart --json` on a sandbox whose base had moved put **80 lines on stderr, every one of them `Warning:`-prefixed** — `Warning: #0 building with "default" instance using docker driver` and 79 more of the same. After the fix: **0 on stderr, 0 `Warning:` lines on stdout,** and stdout is a single parseable JSON document. Both halves observed on real docker, pre-fix and post-fix.
- **Not merely cosmetic, which is why it is worth the entry.** `RenderNotices` sends a `NoticeWarn` to **stderr unconditionally** — the `jsonMode` guard is only on the info branch, deliberately, so a warning can never corrupt the JSON on stdout. A build stream misfiled as warnings therefore inherited the one level that `--json` cannot suppress, so every machine consumer of `restart --json` received 80 lines of BuildKit chatter it had no way to silence.
- **Latent, and exposed rather than introduced.** The wiring was correct when written — its own comment says *"incidental progress (e.g. a port-availability warning from filterAvailablePorts)"*, and at that time a warning was the only thing that ever reached it. `ensureImageLineage` (`ae0597c9`, [DF156](findings-unresolved.md)) is what first made the restart path build images. The class is a single-level channel acquiring a second kind of message, and nothing failed at the moment the second kind arrived.
- **Why the level is read from the text and not from a second sink.** One writer cannot carry two levels, so the alternative was a second `state.State` field. The prefix already *is* the level on the create path, where `Output` is a terminal and there is nowhere else to put it — so keying on it gives one source of truth that both paths share, instead of a second channel a future helper can be wired into wrongly while still looking right on the create path. `WarningPrefix` is exported from `launch` for exactly that reason: the producers and the classifier share the literal, so they cannot drift. The cost, stated plainly: the level is stringly-typed, and an unmarked warning silently becomes info.
- **The convention graduated out of this finding**, per the rule that a resolved finding is archaeology: it lives on the `launch.WarningPrefix` docstring and on `noticeWriter`, where a contributor writing to `st.Output` meets it.
- **Tests** (each reverted and confirmed red): `TestNoticeWriter_ReadsLevelFromEachLine` pins both halves — an unprefixed build line is `NoticeInfo`, a prefixed line is `NoticeWarn` *with the prefix stripped*, the latter because `RenderNotices` re-adds it and retaining it rendered `Warning: Warning: …`; `TestNoticeWriter_ClassifiesAcrossSplitWrites` covers the buffering seam, since a build streams in arbitrary chunks and the prefix can arrive split from its line; `TestFilterAvailablePorts_MarksItsWarning` holds the producer end against a really-bound port, because an unmarked warning is now demoted rather than merely restyled — and it asserts on the constant, not on the word.
- **Rides:** **any** release. No flag, config key, default, or accepted input changed; a notice's level and stream changed, which is a fix to the documented contract rather than a withdrawal of one. No `BREAKING-CHANGES` entry.
- **Discovered:** 2026-07-30, while verifying [DF156](findings-unresolved.md) end to end on Linux — by watching a restart, not by reading the code · **Workstream:** profile-image lifecycle
- **Severity:** LOW–MEDIUM. Loud and misleading for a human (~35–80 spurious warnings on an ordinary restart, which trains the user to ignore the channel); genuinely wrong for a `--json` consumer.
- **Pointer:** `internal/orchestrator/lifecycle/notice.go` (`noticeWriter`), `internal/orchestrator/lifecycle/restart.go` (the `Output:` field), `internal/orchestrator/launch/launch.go` (`WarningPrefix` and its two producers), `internal/cli/cliutil/notice.go` (`RenderNotices`, which is why the level matters).

### DF150 — the profile build-staleness marker was not backend-keyed, so alternating backends skipped a build that never happened (RESOLVED 2026-07-27)

- **Resolved 2026-07-27** — `lastBuildFile = ".last-build-checksum"` was written unqualified into the profile directory. The profile directory is shared across backends; the image stores are not. Build profile `dev` under docker, run it under `--backend podman`: the checksum matched, podman skipped the build, and `podman run yoloai-cli-dev` failed trying to pull a tag that only ever existed in docker's store. It failed in both directions. Replaced with `profileChecksumPath(profileDir, backendKey)` → `.last-build-checksum-<backendKey>`, and extracted `ProfileImageNeedsBuild`/`RecordProfileBuildChecksum` into free functions taking the key, since the scheme has more than one consumer. The docker `Runtime` methods pass `r.binaryName`, which is already `"docker"` or `"podman"` and so distinguishes the two **through the embedding** — podman constructs the docker runtime with `docker.NewWithSocket(ctx, sock, "podman", layout)`, so no override is needed and the inherited method cannot report the wrong store.
- **The fix existed one function away and had for months.** `baseImageChecksumPath(layout, backendKey)` solved exactly this for the base image (DF56) and its comment names the four stores. Nothing carried the invariant to the second consumer, because the reasoning lived only in this file — which is a sink nothing sweeps and no router points at. That is the general failure, and it is [DF151](findings-unresolved.md); the invariant now lives in `architecture/host-layout.md` and on the `runtime.ProfileImageBuilder` contract, where a contributor meets it.
- **Build-context filter widened with it.** `createProfileBuildContext` skipped the marker by exact name; it now prefix-matches `lastBuildPrefix`, so every backend's keyed marker stays out of the build context along with the pre-fix unkeyed one. Missing this would have baked a checksum file into user images.
- **Upgrade path:** a profile dir written before this carries an unkeyed marker, which names no store and so vouches for nothing. It reads as stale — one extra rebuild per profile per backend, then the keyed marker takes over. No migration: the marker is a cache, and a spurious rebuild is the safe direction. Not a `BREAKING-CHANGES` entry — no flag, config key, default, or accepted input changed.
- **Tests** (all three confirmed to fail against the pre-fix code): `TestProfileImageNeedsBuild_MarkerIsPerBackend` pins the docker→podman case directly; `TestProfileImageNeedsBuild_LegacyUnkeyedMarkerForcesRebuild` pins the upgrade path; `TestCreateProfileBuildContext` now writes a keyed marker for two backends plus the legacy one and asserts all three are excluded.
- **What this does NOT fix — [DF152](findings-unresolved.md).** A host-side marker keyed by backend *name* is exact only where one name means one store. The docker backend can be pointed at OrbStack, Docker Desktop or Colima, each a separate store, and this marker cannot tell them apart. The base image already solves that by stamping the checksum onto the image (`baseChecksumLabel`) so staleness travels with the image; the profile path cannot, because `ProfileImageNeedsBuild` takes neither a context nor a tag and so cannot inspect an image. Filed rather than fixed, because closing it changes the `ProfileImageBuilder` interface — which PR #44 is currently implementing.
- **Rides:** **any** release — a bug fix with no flag, config, default, or accepted-input change.
- **Discovered:** 2026-07-27, while writing the invariant down for [DF151](findings-unresolved.md) — not by review, and not by the PR that would have extended it to a third backend · **Workstream:** PR #44 review (first external contribution)
- **Severity:** MEDIUM — a failed run with a misleading error (an image pull for a local-only tag), on demand, on any host with two container backends. No data loss.
- **Pointer:** `runtime/docker/build.go` (`profileChecksumPath`, `ProfileImageNeedsBuild`, `RecordProfileBuildChecksum`, `createProfileBuildContext`); `runtime/docker/build_test.go`; `runtime/runtime_optional.go` (`ProfileImageBuilder`); `docs/contributors/architecture/host-layout.md`.

### DF154 — nothing verifies the profile image exists, and a pre-flight check cannot be the fix (RESOLVED 2026-07-29)

- **Discovered:** 2026-07-29 · **Workstream:** PR #44 follow-up. **Revised same day** after the owner raised TOCTOU — the first draft of this entry recommended adding an existence check, which is wrong for two independent reasons recorded below.
- **Severity:** MEDIUM (silent skip then a confusing failure — `run` attempts to pull a local-only tag — with no path back except deleting the marker by hand or passing `--force`)
- **Resolved 2026-07-29** — both halves are built. The diagnostic (`missingImageHint`, `internal/orchestrator/launch/launch.go`, wired into both `rt.Create` sites and covered by `TestMissingImageHint`, verified to fail on revert). A failed launch now names the image, says the build record tracks the Dockerfile's checksum rather than the image's existence, and gives the rebuild command. **Recovery followed**: `createWithImageRecovery` rebuilds the profile chain and retries `Create` exactly once when the classifier fires. The plumbing worry turned out to be unfounded — `state.State` already carries `Layout`, `Profile` and `Output`, and `profiles.AutoBuildSecrets` is a pure function of `Layout.HomeDir`, so nothing had to be threaded through. The rebuild goes through a `var rebuildProfileImage = profiles.EnsureProfileImage` seam (the `dockerInfoOutput` idiom) so the retry policy is testable without a real backend. Five subtests, three of which fail on revert; the other two are negative controls that assert recovery does *not* fire, so they correctly still pass. **Force is deliberate** — the rebuild revalidates the base image too, because whatever removed the profile image (a prune, a backend switch) commonly took the base with it. **Scoped to sandboxes with a profile**: a missing base is already detected, since `Setup` decides base staleness from a checksum stamped on the image itself.
- **Rides:** **any** release — a recovery path and an error message; no flag, config, default or accepted-input change.
- **Description:** `EnsureProfileImage` (`internal/orchestrator/profiles/profile_build.go`) gates the build solely on `force || builder.ProfileImageNeedsBuild(profileDir, prevDir)`, and `ProfileImageNeedsBuild` reads only the checksum marker on disk. **No code anywhere asks the backend whether the image is present.** The marker is not evidence that the image exists; it is evidence that a build once ran. Delete the image by hand, prune it, or write the marker from a backend that turned out to address a different store, and yoloAI reads "fresh", skips the build, and fails later at `run` with a pull of a tag no local store has.
- **Why the obvious fix is not the fix.** "Check whether the image exists before deciding" is a **TOCTOU** check: it is true at the moment it runs and the image is used later, so a concurrent `system prune`, a background Docker GC, or a second yoloAI process can invalidate it in the gap. It narrows the window; it cannot close it. Worse, the check is not even reliable at the instant it runs — `runtime/docker/docker.go`'s `imageExists` documents that `ImageInspect` **transiently reports a present image as NotFound** on the Docker Desktop containerd store, and that believing a single NotFound rebuilt `yoloai-base` from scratch (~3 min) on every smoke run. It needed a cross-check against `ImageList` plus bounded backoff to become trustworthy. A naive existence probe wired into the profile path would re-introduce precisely that defect, on a path that runs on every `yoloai new --profile`.
- **Where the fix belongs instead: the use site.** The TOCTOU-correct shape is to attempt the operation and handle the failure, not to test a precondition. When launch fails because the image is absent, that is a *recoverable* condition and yoloAI has everything it needs to recover: rebuild the profile image and retry once, or at minimum fail with an error that names the real cause ("profile image `yoloai-cli-dev` is recorded as built but missing from the docker store; rebuild with `yoloai system build dev`") instead of surfacing a registry pull error for a tag that was never in a registry. This is also the only shape that covers the cases a pre-check structurally cannot: the image deleted *during* the run, and any backend whose store yoloAI cannot cheaply interrogate.
- **A pre-check is still worth having as an optimisation, not a guarantee** — it turns the common case (image pruned between sessions) into a clean rebuild rather than a failed launch. If added, it must route through the hardened `imageExists` rather than a fresh `ImageInspect`, and it needs a per-backend analogue that does not exist yet for apple or containerd. Treat it as the fast path and the use-site handler as the correctness story, never the reverse.
- **Related race, unexamined:** two concurrent `yoloai new --profile dev` invocations both read the marker as stale, both build the same tag, and both write the marker with no lock. Probably benign (last writer wins, same content) but nothing has verified it, and it is the same check-then-act shape.
- **This is still the root DF150 and DF152 sit on.** Both are about the marker lying about which store it describes. A use-site handler makes that class cost one unnecessary rebuild instead of a failed run — worth weighing against fixing each proxy problem separately, since it is one remedy for the whole class rather than one per proxy.
- **Sequenced in** [`plans/profile-image-lifecycle.md`](../archive/plans/profile-image-lifecycle.md) — the remaining recovery half leads the sequence, because it is what lets every upstream staleness check be an optimisation that is allowed to be wrong.
- **Pointer:** `internal/orchestrator/profiles/profile_build.go` (`EnsureProfileImage`, the gate); `runtime/docker/build.go` (`ProfileImageNeedsBuild` — marker-only); `runtime/docker/docker.go` (`imageExists`, `confirmImagePresentByList` — the hardened probe and why it had to be); `runtime/runtime_optional.go` (`ProfileImageBuilder`). Related: DF150 (resolved), DF152, DF153.

### DF153 — containerd is image-based but did not implement `ProfileImageBuilder`, so profile Dockerfiles were silently skipped there (RESOLVED 2026-07-29)

- **Resolved 2026-07-29** — `runtime/containerd/profile_image.go` implements the interface: `docker build` into docker's store, then the same two-path import (zero-copy namespace link, falling back to `docker save | ctr import`) that Setup uses for the base image. Checksums go through the shared scheme keyed `"containerd"` (DF150), which matters here more than anywhere: the build happens in *docker's* store and the sandbox runs from *containerd's*, so a docker-side marker vouching for containerd would be exactly wrong. `--secret` works, unlike apple, because the build is a real `docker build`.
- **The prerequisite was the work, as the plan predicted.** `const imageRef = "yoloai-base"` was threaded through 11 sites and six methods, so containerd's image pipeline was written for exactly one image. It is now parameterised by tag, with `Setup` passing the const so base behaviour is provably unchanged — and `dockerRefFor(tag)` replaces the second hardcoded const `dockerImageRef`, verified to reproduce it byte-for-byte for the base case. No parallel pipeline was added; there is one image path with a parameter.
- **Verified against a real daemon** (containerd v2.2.5 + Docker 29.6.1, containerd-snapshotter mode): `TestIntegration_BuildProfileImage_LandsInContainerdNamespace` builds a profile Dockerfile and asserts the image resolves in the yoloai namespace **with a complete descriptor tree**, not merely a root manifest — a half-finished link leaves the latter and fails later at run. `TestIntegration_ProfileImageChecksum_IsKeyedToContainerd` pins the DF150 keying on this backend.
- **`requireAvailable` was too strong for image tests and is now split.** Its third stage demands CAP_SYS_ADMIN because lifecycle tests bring up CNI; the image tests create no containers, so `requireDaemon` asserts only that the daemon is reachable. Keeping the stricter gate would have reported the image pipeline as untestable on any unprivileged host — a false negative about the code under test, not about the host.
- **Found on the way: `tryLink` discarded the reason it failed.** A false return costs a `docker save | ctr import` — 50-80s here — and the fallback looked like the normal path, so nobody could tell "docker isn't in snapshotter mode" (expected) from a content or permission error (not). It now names the cause on the output stream and at debug level. Same discarded-diagnostic class as DF144/DF145.
- **A speed claim that did not survive its control, recorded because the reasoning was tempting.** Adding the BuildKit attestation opt-out to the containerd build paths coincided with a profile build going from 76s to 0.84s, which read as cause. A control run with the flags removed was *also* fast (1.94s). What actually governs the fast link is whether the layer blobs exist as content objects: BuildKit leaves fresh layers in the snapshotter, and an earlier `docker save` — i.e. the slow fallback itself — materialises them, so the *first* import on a host is slow by construction and later ones link. A full base rebuild with the flags on still fell back, which is the same fact from the other side. The flags are kept for the reason the docker backend states (attestation indexes destabilise images on a containerd store), not for speed.
- **Rides:** **any** release — a backend gains a capability it lacked; no flag, config, default or accepted-input change.
- **Discovered:** 2026-07-27 by the PR #44 contributor, who filed it rather than fixing it (rule 7) · **Workstream:** profile-image lifecycle
- **Severity:** MEDIUM (silent skip — a profile with a Dockerfile built nothing and every sandbox on it ran an unmodified `yoloai-base`, with no error)
- **Pointer:** `runtime/containerd/profile_image.go`; `runtime/containerd/image.go` (`dockerRefFor`, the parameterised pipeline, `tryLink`'s diagnostic); `runtime/containerd/profile_image_integration_test.go`; `runtime/docker/build.go` (`AttestationOptOutFlags`, exported for the second consumer). Plan: [`plans/profile-image-lifecycle.md`](../archive/plans/profile-image-lifecycle.md).

### DF155 — concurrent profile builds race on the staleness marker, and nothing locks it (RESOLVED 2026-07-29 — verified benign)

- **Resolved 2026-07-29 by running the check the entry named**, rather than by reasoning about it. Two concurrent `yoloai system build <profile>` runs against a cold profile, on both image-based backends, on one host (containerd v2.2.5, Docker 29.6.1). **Both backends: both runs exit 0, both produce the same image, and the artifact is intact.**
  - **docker** — both builds exported the *same config digest* (`sha256:28760882…`); BuildKit deduplicates identical inputs. One image, one marker, and the layer's content is present in the built image.
  - **containerd** — the sharper case, since each run does its own `docker build` *and* its own import into the yoloai namespace. Both produced the same manifest digest (`sha256:1fce3892…`), both imports succeeded, both the short tag and the fully-qualified alias are registered exactly once, and a follow-up build confirms the descriptor tree verifies.
- **The marker write is a non-event.** Both writers compute the checksum from the same Dockerfile, so both write identical bytes; last-writer-wins converges on the correct value. That was the predicted outcome and it held.
- **What it actually costs is duplicated work, not corruption.** The two containerd runs each performed a full 104s import, concurrently — so a genuine collision wastes a build and an import rather than breaking anything.
- **So: still no lock, but the reasoning has moved.** The original entry argued against locking *on suspicion*. The suspicion is now settled, and the remaining case for a lock is efficiency alone. If someone wants it, the mechanism already exists and is proven — `dockerrt.AcquireBaseLock(layout, tag)`, which `Setup` uses for exactly this reason on the base image — so it would be a reuse rather than a new failure surface. It is not required for correctness, and the stale-lock-after-crash cost is real, so this stays a deliberate non-fix rather than an oversight.
- **Rides:** n/a — no code changed.
- **Discovered:** 2026-07-29, while closing DF154 · **Workstream:** profile-image lifecycle
- **Severity:** LOW, and now confirmed rather than assumed
- **Pointer:** `internal/orchestrator/profiles/profile_build.go` (`EnsureProfileImage`); `runtime/docker/build.go` (`RecordProfileBuildChecksum`, `AcquireBaseLock`).

### DF151 — the documentation system routes agents to archived plans, and hides its conventions in append-only sinks (RESOLVED 2026-07-29)

- **Discovered:** 2026-07-27 · **Workstream:** PR #44 review (first external contribution)
- **Severity:** MEDIUM (project infra, not product — but it is the mechanism that is supposed to make external contributions correct, and its first live test failed in four places)
- **Resolved 2026-07-29** — the four faults in the trace are fixed (D128), the class they belong to is named (D129, GEN §17), and the last parked item (the dead-name gate) is withdrawn on measured evidence rather than left owed. Nothing remains open.
- **The programme it started did not end, and does not live here.** Reviewing each outside contribution as an execution trace of the docs is an activity, not a defect; a findings queue is for defects, and one that never drains stops being a working set. Its home is [`procedures/outside-contributions.md`](../procedures/outside-contributions.md) — the method, plus a **cycle log** that grows an entry per contribution. That is also the page a reviewer opens *before* reviewing, which is where accumulated lessons have to be if they are to be read at all (GEN §17, applied to this record itself).
- **Description:** PR #44 is the first non-trivial outside submission and therefore the first real execution of the contributor doc system on an agent with no project history. Fifteen review findings; tracing each back to what the docs say produced two structural causes rather than fifteen unrelated slips. **(1) An archived plan was read as a specification.** The contributor's code comment cites `design/plans/apple-container-backend.md`, which rule 8 had moved to `archive/plans/`. That file's line 85 is the source of the pre-D126 image name in *both* doc edits, the AC1 absolute-context reasoning copied into the code comment, and the stale path itself. `archive/README.md` already warned, correctly and in the right words, that nothing there is a live reference — but an agent that greps arrives mid-file and never passes through an index, so every word of the warning was off the path taken. **(2) The two engineering conventions the PR broke lived only in `findings-resolved.md`** — the backend-keyed marker scheme at `:929` and the DF144/DF145 subprocess-diagnostic rule. AGENTS.md classifies the `*-resolved` sinks as append-only archaeology exempt from sweeps, so both conventions were, by the docs' own rules, unreachable. A third contributing fact: **AGENTS.md had no testing rule at all** — rules 1–9 were entirely publishing ritual (breaking changes, name sweeps, commit format, IDs, findings, plans, deprecations), and `CONTRIBUTING.md` explained how to *run* tests without ever saying to write one. The single largest review comment was "the fix's core is untested".
- **What was done (D128):** every file under `archive/` carries a first-lines `> **ARCHIVED …**` banner (the one carve-out to "frozen means frozen", because a warning is the only edit that cannot imply someone vouched for the contents); AGENTS.md gained rule 10 (a behavior change carries a test that fails when reverted, argv included) and a converse to rule 7 (a fix that establishes a convention graduates it out of the finding into `standards/`/`architecture/`/the interface docstring); the two orphaned conventions were backfilled; `ProfileImageBuilder` moved to `runtime/runtime_optional.go` so backends can compile-assert it; and the live pre-D126 name drift in `design/plans/shared-cache-volumes.md` was fixed.
- **What was parked, and is now withdrawn — the dead-name gate (investigated 2026-07-29, not built).** The proposal was to grep non-exempt docs for a yoloAI-generated name missing its principal segment, sourced from `config.InstancePrefix`, on the reasoning that since it panics on an empty principal there is no unscoped form and any principal-less `yoloai-<x>` is provably dead. **That reasoning was wrong, and the error is worth keeping.** `InstancePrefix` governs *instance and profile-image* names; it does not govern the `yoloai-` namespace, which legitimately hosts several unscoped members — `yoloai-base`, `yoloai-base-<key>`, `yoloai-base-tmp-<hex>`, `yoloai-resume`, the `yoloai-test-<purpose>-…` reservation in `testing-principles.md`, and containerd's netns `yoloai-<containerName>` (`runtime/containerd/cni.go`, `netnsNameFor`). Generalising one function's invariant to a whole prefix is what made the rule look mechanical.
- **The measured signal decides it.** Run across the tree, the rule produces roughly twenty hits: most in append-only history that rule 2 exempts anyway, several on legitimately unscoped names, and **one** genuine drift. A gate at that ratio is the D122 failure mode by construction — "a hook that noisy gets disabled" — and it would be blanket-suppressed within a release, leaving less protection than none because the suppression would read as coverage. Not built, deliberately; this is the cost-vs-benefit floor (GEN §1) applied to a gate rather than a feature.
- **What the investigation did produce**, which is the argument for doing it by hand: the one true positive was `testing-principles.md` still claiming production owns `yoloai-<sandbox>`, and a second turned up outside the proposed gate's scope entirely — `runtime/tart/tart.go`'s `instancePrefix` doc comment still described `"yoloai-" by default, or "yoloai-<principal>-" when the Layout is principal-scoped`, a pre-D126 reading of a function whose body cannot produce the unscoped form. Both fixed. A `.md`-only gate would have missed the second one, which is the more dangerous kind: a comment a reviewer reads as if the compiler had checked it (GEN §16).
- **Three variants were measured before dropping it (2026-07-29), not one.** (a) *Match the literal segment after `yoloai-`, allowlisting known principals plus the pseudo-principals `base` and `test`* — the owner's proposal, and the principal enumeration is sound: the set is closed at `{cli}` in this tree, since a downstream embedder's principals live in the embedder's repo. It fails on a different axis. `yoloai-` is also an English adjective prefix here — "yoloai-specific vars" (`standards/go.md`), "a yoloai-owned network" (`plans/guest-network-families.md`), "wipe all yoloai-managed state" (`internal/cli/helpcmd/help/cleanup.md`, **shipped UI**) — so the allowlist would have to enumerate adjectives. It also collides with real non-instance resources that share the prefix: `yoloai-resume` (the in-sandbox resume command, `docs/GUIDE.md`), `yoloai-secrets`, `yoloai-bugreport`, `yoloai-smoketest`, `yoloai-tmux`. (b) *Match only bracketed placeholders (`yoloai-<x>`), allowing `<principal>`* — precise enough to run, but 26 hits tree-wide of which ~23 are legitimate, concentrated in `design/research/` and `deprecations.md`. The register cannot describe what it registers without writing `yoloai-<name>`; that is the entry's content, not its drift. (c) *Variant (b) scoped to the tier people build from — `design/plans/` + `architecture/`.* **3 hits across 46 files, 0 true positives:** `code-map.md:477` documents `LegacyCLIInstanceName` as "the pre-D126 `yoloai-<name>` form, used only by migrations"; `init-sentinel.md:29` describes what instances stay as when `PrincipalRename` is skipped; `tamper-resistant-network-isolation.md:115` is an ipset name in a netns. All three correct.
- **The result that settles it:** the tier where drift would be dangerous is already clean, and both real drifts this exercise found sat outside it — `principles/testing-principles.md` and a doc comment in `runtime/tart/tart.go`. Widening the scope to reach them is exactly what reintroduces the false positives. There is no scope on which this gate is both precise and useful. Name drift stays where D126's own remediation and D122 both put it: a rule-2 sweep at rename time, review-caught.
- **Also unaddressed, deliberately:** several review findings are not preventable by any document — "extract the shared scheme rather than shadowing it", "this test's name does not match what it asserts", "this test pins the defect as intended behavior". Those are review-caught, and review catching them is the system working. Do not respond to this finding by trying to write a rule for each of the fifteen.
- **Pointer:** https://github.com/kstenerud/yoloai/pull/44 (13 inline comments + review body); `docs/contributors/archive/plans/apple-container-backend.md:85` (the line read as spec); `docs/contributors/design/findings-resolved.md:929`; `docs/contributors/decisions/working-notes.md` (D128).

- **Rides:** **any** release — documentation and one script fix; no flag, config, default or accepted-input change.

### DF152 — profile-image staleness could not travel with the image, because the interface took neither a context nor a tag (RESOLVED 2026-07-29)

- **Resolved 2026-07-29 — the host-side marker is gone.** `runtime.ProfileImageBuilder` now takes `BuildProfileImage(ctx, sourceDir, tag, checksum, …)` and `ProfileImageChecksum(ctx, tag) (string, bool)`. The caller computes a checksum, the backend stamps it as `runtime.ProfileChecksumLabel` at build time and reads it back, and the caller compares. `.last-build-checksum*` is no longer written by anything.
- **The interface got smaller, which is the tell that this was the right shape.** Three methods became two: `RecordProfileBuildChecksum` disappeared entirely, because stamping happens during the build rather than as a separate bookkeeping step that could be forgotten or fail independently. `ImagePresenceChecker` — added days earlier for the false-success bug — went with it: "no checksum readable" already means "cannot vouch, build", so absence needs no separate question.
- **Three defects collapse into one absence.** DF150 (marker keyed for the base but not the profile), DF152 (a backend name is not a store), DF154's first half (nothing verified the image existed) were all *a record outliving what it describes*. A label cannot outlive its image because it is part of it. The interim `storeKey()` daemon-keying fix from earlier in this finding is deleted along with `profileChecksumPath` — it existed only to make the marker's key better, and there is no key now.
- **Parent staleness moved from mtimes to a chain.** The old rule compared two marker files' modification times, asking a question about the filesystem. `chainChecksum(profileDir, parentChecksum)` folds the parent's value into the child's, so an ancestor change invalidates every descendant at any depth, by construction.
- **Verified end to end on real daemons**, not just compiled. docker: builds and stamps the label, skips on a match, rebuilds when the image is deleted (the label goes with it), rebuilds with a *new* checksum when the Dockerfile changes. containerd: the label survives `docker build` → namespace link → its own store and reads back identical to docker's. **And the DF150 scenario needs no key at all** — delete only containerd's copy and containerd rebuilds while docker still skips, because each store's own image carries the answer.
- **The D125 gate caught the change's own dead code**, which is worth recording: moving the scheme to the orchestrator left `runtime/docker`'s `profileBuildChecksum` test-only, and the gate refused the build. Its tests were retargeted onto `chainChecksum` rather than deleted with it (DF105's rule), and gained the parent-propagation case the old scheme could not express.
- **Filed, not fixed:** [DF156](#df156--a-yoloai-base-rebuild-did-not-invalidate-profile-images-built-on-the-old-base-resolved-2026-07-30) — a `yoloai-base` rebuild still does not invalidate profile images. That gap predates this work and the label scheme preserves it deliberately; closing it is a one-line seed change with a real behaviour cost, and bundling it here would have made a slow rebuild look like a regression from the label work.
- **Rides:** **any** release — no flag, config key, default, or accepted input changes. Existing `.last-build-checksum*` files become inert litter; they are filtered out of build contexts (`legacyMarkerPrefix`) and the first build per profile per store rebuilds once, which is the safe direction.

- **Discovered:** 2026-07-27 · **Workstream:** DF150 fix
- **Severity:** LOW–MEDIUM (same failure shape as DF150 — a skipped build then a pull of a local-only tag — but confined to one backend with more than one daemon configured, rather than any host with two backends)
- **Disposition:** PARKED
- **Description:** DF150 keyed the profile build marker by backend name. That is exact only where one backend name means one store, and the **docker backend is not** — it can be pointed at OrbStack, Docker Desktop or Colima, each a separate image store, and a host-side marker keyed `"docker"` cannot tell them apart. Switch daemons and the marker still says "built", so the build is skipped and the run fails pulling a tag the new store has never seen. **This is not a new discovery so much as an unfinished one:** `baseImageChecksumPath`'s comment (`runtime/docker/build.go`) already states the whole problem and names the remedy — the base image stamps its checksum onto the image itself (`baseChecksumLabel`) and reads it back via `baseImageStale`, so staleness travels with the image, in whatever store holds it. The base path is right; the profile path is the one that never got there.
- **Why DF150 stopped short:** the remedy needs to inspect an image, and `ProfileImageNeedsBuild(profileDir, parentDir string) bool` has neither a `ctx` nor the tag. Closing this changes the `runtime.ProfileImageBuilder` interface — which PR #44 is implementing as of this writing, so changing it now would turn that contributor's rebase into a rewrite. Deliberately deferred on those grounds, not on merit.
- **Fix sketch:** give `ProfileImageNeedsBuild` a `ctx` and the resolved tag, then mirror the base-image split — label-based staleness for docker/podman (multi-daemon capable), host-side keyed marker for backends that are genuinely one store per name (apple, containerd). The label constant and the `checksumLabelStale` helper already exist and generalize with a parameterized label name.
- **Trigger:** PR #44 landing (the interface is then free to move), or any report of a profile image rebuilding-or-not wrongly after a Docker daemon switch.
- **Sequenced in** [`plans/profile-image-lifecycle.md`](../archive/plans/profile-image-lifecycle.md) — build **after** DF154's recovery half, not before: a label read is a better proxy than a filename but still check-then-act, so it is only safe to add once a missing image at launch is recoverable rather than fatal.
- **Re-evaluated again 2026-07-29 (later), and the pause was based on two things I got wrong.** Both are checkable and were checked.
- **(1) This is not an unusual host configuration; it is two first-class backends.** `orbstack` and `docker-desktop` are wizard-offered, documented backend ids, and `ResolveContainerSystem` (`runtime/container_system.go`) maps both to `(BackendDocker, pinned socket)` — the same `docker.Runtime` with `binaryName` `"docker"`, which is the marker key. So two supported backends share one key while having **separate image stores**, and reaching the bug needs no reconfiguration at all: `--backend orbstack` then `--backend docker-desktop` on the same profile. The codebase already knows these are distinct stores — `providerNames` and the recreate hint at `runtime/docker/docker.go:275` warn the user that switching providers strands *containers*. The image half is this finding.
- **(2) Recovery does not cover the explicit-build path, which is where it hurts most.** `yoloai system build <profile>` without `--rebuild` passes `force=false` (`system.go` `buildOne`), so `EnsureProfileImage` consults the marker, skips the build, prints nothing — and the CLI then prints **"Profile image built successfully"** (`internal/cli/system/system.go:163`). No `Create` happens, so `createWithImageRecovery` never fires. The user explicitly asked for a build, was told it succeeded, and the image is absent from the store they targeted. That is a false success report on a direct request, and it is strictly worse than the launch-time failure this finding was originally about.
- **Three ways to close it, and they are not exclusive** — the decision is which, not whether:
  - **(a) Key the marker by daemon, not by backend name.** The Runtime already knows its endpoint (`docker.NewWithSocket` takes the pinned socket), so the key can distinguish OrbStack from Docker Desktop without a `ctx`, an interface change, or a second mechanism. Closes the provider-switch case. Does not detect an image deleted by hand.
  - **(b) Make an explicit build verify rather than trust.** `system build` exists to ensure an image is present; answering it from a marker that can be wrong is what produces the false success. Either probe existence or treat the explicit command as force. Closes the lie; does not fix staleness reasoning generally.
  - **(c) The original fix — staleness on the image (labels).** Subsumes (a) and (b) and matches what the *base* image already does (`baseChecksumLabel` for docker, keyed marker for apple/containerd), so it makes the profile path consistent with the base path rather than adding a divergence. Costs the `ctx`+tag interface change across four backends.
- **Reframed 2026-07-29 (owner): this is a harmony problem, not a risk calculation.** The prior recommendation weighed (c) as expected value against failures nobody had hit yet, and deferred it. That framing misses what the corpus actually says. Every finding in this workstream is one shape — *a mechanism exists on path A and not on its sibling path B, and the gap is invisible until it bites*: the keyed marker (base had it, profile did not — DF150), label-based staleness (docker base had it, docker profile did not — this finding), the subprocess tail buffer (docker build had it, apple's profile build did not — PR #44), the attestation opt-out (docker backend had it, containerd's build path did not — DF153). Four instances, one release. The cost of two mechanisms for one question is not the risk of a rare failure; it is that every new path is a fresh chance to implement the wrong one, and nothing notices.
- **(a) as built makes the divergence worse, and `backend-idiosyncrasies.md` already said so.** Its "Docker: base-image staleness marker was keyed per backend" entry records the base path's decision: *stop tracking freshness with a host-side marker at all*, stamp a label, and it explicitly rejects a patch that keyed the marker by daemon identity. (a) keys the profile marker by daemon endpoint — a better key than the rejected one, and not the same flaw, but the same *shape* the base path deliberately abandoned. It closes a live first-class bug and should stay for now, but it is **interim**: the end state deletes `profileChecksumPath`, `storeKey()`, and the build-context filter's special case along with it.
- **Full harmony looks reachable, and the documented obstacle was a misattribution.** That entry justified keeping markers for apple with *"a Tart VM image, not OCI — can't carry a label"*. That describes **tart**. Apple `container` runs Linux OCI images, so the format is no obstacle; corrected in place 2026-07-29. containerd's justification ("one image store") only establishes that a marker is *sufficient*, not that a label is impossible — and containerd builds via `docker build`, so a `--label` rides along for free and would travel through `docker save`/import into its store. So the target is plausibly **one** mechanism across all four backends, not the two-way split this finding originally proposed.
- **The one open question, and it needs a Mac:** does apple's CLI accept `container build --label` and report labels on inspect? If yes, profile staleness is label-based everywhere and the host-side marker disappears from profile directories entirely. If no, apple keeps a marker for a *stated and verified* reason rather than an inherited wrong one — still a better place than today, and a one-backend exception instead of a two-by-two matrix.
- **Standing recommendation:** (a) and (b) are cheap, independent, and between them close both observed failures; (c) is the principled end state and may never be needed if they land. Build (b) first — a command that reports success without doing anything is the sharpest defect here.
- **Re-evaluated 2026-07-29, after DF154 landed: the payoff shrank and this should not be built on autopilot.** DF152's failure mode is "switch docker daemons, the marker still says fresh, the build is skipped, and the run fails pulling a local-only tag". The last clause is no longer true. `createWithImageRecovery` now catches exactly that Create failure, rebuilds and retries, so the user-visible outcome is a message and a rebuild that had to happen anyway — not a failure. What DF152 would still buy is avoiding *one* failed Create attempt before the rebuild, on a host that switches daemons.
- **That is a smaller prize than the cost**, which is widening `ProfileImageNeedsBuild` to take a `ctx` and a tag across four backends and both call sites, plus a second staleness mechanism to keep consistent with the first. It is the general fix (recover at the use site) having largely obviated the specific one (make each proxy exact) — which is the outcome the ordering constraint was hoping for, and worth noticing rather than proceeding through. **Reconsider if** someone reports the daemon-switch case as painful in practice, or if a second reason to want a tag and ctx in that signature appears — the interface change is then paid for by two things instead of one.
- **The open question is answered, 2026-07-29, on a Mac: apple accepts a build label and reports it on inspect. Harmony is reachable at 4-of-4, not 3-of-4.** Verified against `container CLI version 1.0.0 (build: release, commit: ee848e3)`. `container build --help` lists `-l, --label <key=val>   Set a label`. Building `FROM alpine` / `RUN true` with `container build --label yoloai.test.checksum=abc123 -t yoloai-test-labelprobe:v1 .` succeeded, and `container image inspect yoloai-test-labelprobe:v1` returned the label verbatim:
  ```json
  "Labels" : {
    "yoloai.test.checksum" : "abc123"
  },
  ```
  **Controlled:** the identical build without `--label` produces an inspect output with no `Labels` key anywhere (`grep -i Labels` → no match), so the label's presence is attributable to the flag and not to a default or an inherited `alpine` label.
- **The accessor exists but the JSON path is not docker's, and that is the one real cost.** Apple's is `[<image>].variants[<platform>].config.config.Labels` — **two array levels** before the map: `container image inspect` takes N refs and returns a top-level *array*, and each image carries a `variants` array (one entry per platform, `platform.{architecture,os}`) whose `config.config` is the OCI image config. Docker's is a flat `ImageInspect` → `Config.Labels`. So a shared `checksumLabelStale` helper generalizes over the *label name* but not over the *extraction*; apple needs its own reader that picks a variant (match `platform.architecture`, or accept any when the label agrees across variants). `container image inspect` has no `--format`/`-f`, so this is raw JSON parsing, not a templated field. No new subprocess is needed: `runtime/apple/apple.go::imageExists` already runs exactly this command, and `runContainer` already returns trimmed stdout — `imageExists` just discards it with `_`.
- **What this changes about the design's shape:** the "apple keeps a marker" exception has no remaining justification — not the image format (corrected earlier: apple is Linux OCI, the Tart claim was a misattribution), and now not the CLI either. The end state is one mechanism across all four backends, and the host-side marker leaves profile directories entirely. Scope note: this verifies **apple** only. containerd's "rides along for free via `docker build`" remains reasoned-not-run — cheap to check on Linux, and worth checking before the harmonisation is called 4-of-4 in code.
- **containerd's half is verified too, 2026-07-29 on Linux — the last reasoned-not-run assumption is closed.** A `docker build --label yoloai.test.checksum=…` survives our import into the yoloai namespace on **both** paths: the zero-copy namespace link (covered by `TestIntegration_DockerBuildLabel_SurvivesImportIntoContainerd`, which took the link path on this host) and `docker save | ctr import` (checked by hand — same label, same location). So all four backends are now confirmed capable, not three plus an inference.
- **The readback deliberately walks to the OCI image config, not `images.Image.Labels`.** Those are different things and only one answers the question: `images.Image.Labels` is a *record* label in containerd's bolt metadata, local and lost on re-import, while a build label lives in the image config and travels with the image. Reading the record label would have looked like a pass and meant nothing.
- **So "one mechanism" is precise about the source of truth and honest about the accessor.** One label name, one place the truth lives (the image), and **three** extractions: docker's flat `Config.Labels` via the SDK; apple's `[<image>].variants[<platform>].config.config.Labels` via raw CLI JSON; containerd's config blob read from the content store (image record → manifest → config descriptor → `config.Labels`). That is not a failure of the harmonisation — differing accessors behind one contract is what the runtime layer exists for, and it is exactly how `Exec`, `Prune` and the rest already work. What goes away is the *second source of truth*: the host-side marker, its key, its upgrade path, and the build-context filter that has to hide it.
- **Status: fully specified and ready to build, blocked on nothing.** Remaining work is the `ProfileImageNeedsBuild(ctx, profileDir, parentDir, tag)` signature change across four backends and both call sites, `--label` on each build path (docker and podman already have the helper; containerd inherits it via `docker build`; apple takes `-l, --label`), a per-backend label reader, and deleting `profileChecksumPath`/`storeKey()`/the filter special case with the interim (a) fix.
