- **Pointer:** `runtime/docker/build.go` (`profileChecksumPath` — the caveat is stated in its doc comment; `baseImageChecksumPath`, `baseChecksumLabel`, `checksumLabelStale`, `baseImageStale`); `runtime/runtime_optional.go` (`ProfileImageBuilder`); `runtime/apple/apple.go` (`imageExists`, `runContainer` — the inspect call whose output is discarded). Resolved sibling: [DF150](findings-resolved.md).

### DF149 — `reset` stranded `files/`, `cache/` and `agent-runtime/` at deleted inodes, silently breaking the agent's artifact channel (RESOLVED 2026-07-27)

- **Resolved 2026-07-27** — reset cleared these directories with `os.RemoveAll` + `MkdirAllPerm`, which *replaces* the directory. The host got a fresh inode while the running sandbox's bind mount still resolved to the old, unlinked one, so everything the agent wrote afterwards landed in an orphaned directory (`/proc/self/mountinfo` shows the mount as `…/files//deleted`). Introduced `fileutil.ClearDirContents`, which empties in place and creates the directory when absent, and routed all four reset clearing sites through it (`clearCacheAndFiles`, `clearAgentState`, `reinitLogs`).
- **The invariant already existed for one of the three mounts.** `TestResyncWorkCopy_PreservesWorkDirInode` states the rule exactly for the work copy — which is why `/home/karl/Projects/filetool` survived the reported reset while `files/` and `cache/` did not. The bug was the rule being understood in one place and not applied in the others.
- **Audit result** (the report guessed at this and was half right): all four cleared directories are bind-mounted — `agent-runtime/` (`mounts.go:91`), `logs/` (`:190`), `files/` (`:222`), `cache/` (`:227`). `files/`+`cache/` clear on every reset of a live sandbox, so they were the reported failure. `agent-runtime/` has the same exposure via `--clear-state` and **was not reported** — same bug, found by the sweep. `logs/` is reached only from `prepareResetRestart`, which removes the container first and recreates it after, so a replaced inode strands nothing there today; it was converted anyway so the safety does not rest on that caller-side fact.
- **Why the existing tests missed it:** `lifecycle_test.go:718,781,1056` assert the *contents* are gone, and emptying in place and replacing the directory are indistinguishable from the host side. Only inode identity distinguishes them. New guards: `TestReset_PreservesFilesAndCacheInodes` and `TestReset_ClearState_PreservesAgentRuntimeInode` (`reset_inode_test.go`), plus `ClearDirContents` unit tests; all three assertions were confirmed to fail against the pre-fix code.
- **Rides:** **any** release — a bug fix with no flag, config, default, or accepted-input change.
- **Discovered:** 2026-07-26, from inside a sandbox (`filetool`) whose `touch /yoloai/files/x` returned `ENOENT` while `ls` and `cd` succeeded · **Workstream:** reported as a standalone bug report during agent use
- **Severity:** HIGH — silent in both directions: the agent's writes fail with a misleading `ENOENT` that reads as an agent path bug (it was first reported to the user as "the directory was cleared"), and anything the *user* drops into the host-side `files/` never becomes visible to the agent. On the reporting session it destroyed the only in-sandbox copy of an experiment's held-out task set (recovered — the user had copied it out).
- **Pointer:** `internal/fileutil/fileutil.go` (`ClearDirContents`); `internal/orchestrator/lifecycle/reset.go` (`clearCacheAndFiles`, `clearAgentState`, `reinitLogs`); `internal/orchestrator/lifecycle/reset_inode_test.go`; `internal/fileutil/cleardir_test.go`. Prior art: `reset_resync_test.go:154`.

### DF134 — one IPv6 nameserver in `/etc/resolv.conf` aborts `--network-isolated` entirely (RESOLVED 2026-07-18)

- **Resolved 2026-07-18** — `firewall.py` now classifies nameservers by family and keeps only IPv4 ones for the (IPv4-only) rule install. Extracted a pure, unit-tested `parse_nameservers` (with an `is_ipv4` helper) from `read_nameservers`: a non-IPv4 `nameserver` line is skipped with a loud `network.nameserver_non_ipv4_skipped` log instead of being fed to `iptables -A OUTPUT -d <v6>`, which failed and aborted the whole install (taking the correct v4 rules with it). A v6 nameserver is unreachable from v4-only isolation anyway, so dropping it loses nothing usable; if it was the *only* nameserver, the empty result still trips the loud no-nameservers warning. First unit tests for `firewall.py` landed alongside (`runtime/docker/resources/tests/test_firewall.py`, wired into `make python-test`). Regression-checked end-to-end: a `--network-isolated` sandbox installs its firewall via the sidecar (which runs the refactored `read_nameservers`) and reaches `done` cleanly.
- **Scope note:** this is the "one-line mitigation" the finding always named — *not* full IPv6 support. Keeping v6 nameservers and allowing them via `ip6tables`, plus giving the guest v6 at all, is the **network-families design** (host-family parity; guest gets whatever families the host offers; isolation filters every present family and fails loud on an impossible ask). DF104 stays open under that design.
- **Rides:** **any** release — a bug fix that only *narrows* what a v4-only firewall feeds itself; no flag, config, default, or accepted-input change.
- **Discovered:** 2026-07-17 · **Workstream:** costing proper IPv6 support for DF104
- **Severity:** MEDIUM — a hard failure of a security feature (isolation aborted), failing closed. Latent on this dev host (Docker replaced the container `resolv.conf` with v4 nameservers; a v6 nameserver reaches the container only on an `--ipv6` network / v6-supplying CNI / apple vmnet resolver — none reproducible here), which is why it was never higher.
- **Pointer:** `runtime/docker/resources/firewall.py` (`is_ipv4`, `parse_nameservers`, `read_nameservers`); `runtime/docker/resources/tests/test_firewall.py`; `Makefile` (`python-test`). Related: DF104 (open, folded into the network-families design).

### DF146 — integration-tagged test files escaped the linter entirely, so 32 (really 86) issues sat unseen (RESOLVED 2026-07-19)

- **Resolved 2026-07-19, in two acts.** *The issues:* bringing `--build-tags integration` up surfaced
  not the filed 32 but **86** issues — golangci-lint's default output caps (`--max-same-issues`,
  `--max-issues-per-linter`) had truncated the list, an undercount that was itself a symptom of the
  blindness. All were reconciled so the tagged and untagged runs both pass, on darwin and
  GOOS=linux, with caps disabled: six TestMains called `os.Exit` with defers pending (real bugs —
  the deferred cleanup never ran; each restructured as a thin `TestMain` over a helper returning the
  exit code), a dead helper deleted, an unchecked `os.RemoveAll` handled; the rest were stale nolint
  directives removed, test-edge unchecked errors converted to the idiomatic `_ =` form, scoped
  nolints with reasons on gosec test-fixture false positives, and `.golangci.yml` DF19-allowlist
  entries for the integration-file `GetCuratedHostEnv` callers. The e2e tag hid six more of the same
  classes (`test/e2e/`), fixed when the gate wiring landed. *The blind spot:* on the owner's
  go-ahead, `make lint` and `make lint-cross` now run a **second golangci-lint pass with
  `--build-tags 'integration e2e'`** (matching vet's tag set) and caps disabled, on the host GOOS
  and each cross GOOS — CI runs `make check`, so the gate holds everywhere and the reconciliation
  cannot silently rot.
- **The tension that made it non-trivial:** the same file can need different `//nolint` directives
  per build-tag set (`nolintlint` flags a directive unused under one set that the other requires),
  so the flip could not be blanket — each directive was reconciled so **both** runs pass; where
  `_ =` silences errcheck and gosec's G104 twin together, that form won over a two-linter nolint.
- **Discovered:** 2026-07-19 · **Workstream:** conformance speedup (integration harness work)
- **Severity:** LOW (test-only lint debt, not a shipped defect), but a **gate blind spot** — the
  class of thing that silently rots because nothing looks at it.
- **Pointer:** gate definition `Makefile` (`lint`, `lint-cross`); `.golangci.yml` (DF19 allowlist,
  nolintlint settings); the sweep itself is commit-visible across
  `runtime/*/integration_*_test.go`, `internal/{cli,orchestrator}/integration*_test.go`,
  `runtime/runtimetest/conformance_iface.go`, `test/e2e/`.

### DF145 — subprocess and `.Output()` failures forwarded an opaque exit code, discarding the stderr the caller needs (RESOLVED 2026-07-19)

- **Resolved 2026-07-19, in two phases.** Three shapes, one root cause — an error was wrapped or
  returned without the diagnostic already in hand. **Phase 1 (Linux/cross-platform):** new
  `sysexec.EnrichExitError` folds the stderr Go already captured into `(*exec.ExitError)` on any
  `.Output()` failure into the error message (wrapping with `%w` so `errors.As/Is` still match);
  applied at `runtime/podman/podman.go:286`, and `internal/orchestrator/files.go:145`'s dropped-`%w`
  (which could render as the *empty string* when `cp` failed to start) now wraps the real error.
  **Phase 2 (macOS, verified on Apple Silicon hardware):** the streaming build/VM sites got the
  DF144 `TailBuffer` remedy — `runtime/apple/apple.go` (`container build`, the exact DF144 case) and
  `runtime/tart/build.go` (provision steps, tool verification, `tart pull`, and the imprint write,
  which previously discarded its exec's output entirely) — and the remaining `.Output()` sites
  (`runtime/tart/runtime.go` simctl query, `runtime/tart/build.go` `sw_vers`,
  `internal/envsetup/keychain_darwin.go` `security`) route through `sysexec.EnrichExitError`.
  Hardware probes confirmed the mechanism inputs (`tart pull` and `security` both emit their
  diagnostics on stderr with a bare exit status otherwise); unit tests with fake failing binaries
  pin every remedied shape (`runtime/tart/build_test.go`, `runtime/apple/build_test.go`,
  `internal/envsetup/keychain_darwin_test.go`, plus the Phase-1 `sysexec` tests).
- **Rides:** **any** release — additive error enrichment; no flag, config, default, or
  accepted-input change.
- **Discovered:** 2026-07-19 · **Workstream:** post-DF144 opaque-error audit
- **Severity:** MEDIUM (no data loss, but a build/daemon failure surfaced only `exit status N`, so
  the operator could not tell a missing package from a network fault from a permission error
  without re-running by hand). Cross-backend; the highest-impact sites were macOS-only.
- **Pointer:** `internal/sysexec/exiterr.go` (`EnrichExitError`), `internal/sysexec/tail.go`
  (`TailBuffer`, from DF144); builds — `runtime/apple/apple.go` (`buildBaseImage`),
  `runtime/tart/build.go` (`bootForProvisioning`, `verifyTools`, `pullImage`, `writeImprint`);
  `.Output()` — `runtime/tart/runtime.go` (`QueryAvailableRuntimes`), `runtime/tart/build.go`
  (`hostMacOSMajor`), `internal/envsetup/keychain_darwin.go`, `runtime/podman/podman.go`;
  dropped-`%w` — `internal/orchestrator/files.go`.

### DF144 — a failed image build returned "exited with code 1" with the cause dropped, invisible to `--json`/embedders (RESOLVED 2026-07-19)

- **Resolved 2026-07-19** — the three image-build exec sites (`runtime/docker/build.go` base + profile, `runtime/containerd/image.go`) streamed the build subprocess's combined stdout+stderr to an `output` writer but, on failure, returned a generic `"%s build exited with code %d"` — the actionable cause (a permission error, a missing apt package, a network failure) reached the user only via the live stream. Interactively that stream is the terminal, so a human saw the real error buried under yoloai's opaque line; under `--json` the stream is `io.Discard` and library embedders pass their own writer, so the cause was **lost entirely**. New `sysexec.TailBuffer` (an `io.Writer` keeping the last N lines, mutex-guarded, memory-bounded) is tee'd into each build via `io.MultiWriter(output, tail)` — the same value on both `cmd.Stdout`/`cmd.Stderr` so os/exec keeps its single-pipe path — and its `ErrorSuffix()` is appended to the returned error. **Verified on the exact reported scenario:** a root-owned `~/.docker/buildx/activity/default` made `yoloai system build --json` fail; the error now reads `docker build exited with code 1\nlast output:\n  ERROR: open …/.docker/buildx/… permission denied` instead of the bare code. Unit tests cover the buffer (retention, unterminated final line, CR-stripping, memory bound, suffix formatting) and the end-to-end wiring through a real failing subprocess.
- **Rides:** **any** release — additive error enrichment; no flag, config, default, or accepted-input change.
- **Discovered:** 2026-07-19 · **Workstream:** owner asked whether the root-owned `~/.docker/buildx` failure (hit while verifying the DF137 image label) surfaces an actionable message
- **Severity:** LOW (diagnosability — the build still fails correctly; only the *legibility* of the failure was poor, worst for `--json`/embedders who saw only "exited with code 1")
- **Follow-up shipped 2026-07-19 — `doctor` now diagnoses the ownership root cause proactively:** `yoloai doctor` gained an ownership audit that flags root-owned leftovers (from a `sudo yoloai …` run or a backend writing as root) under the Docker config dir *and* the yoloai TOP data dir, with a `sudo chown -R` remedy per location — so the `~/.docker/buildx` case that motivated DF144 is now caught *before* a build fails, not just surfaced in the failure. New `System.OwnershipAudit` verb + `fileutil.OwnerUID`/`ScanWrongOwner` primitives + a doctor advisory section. Verified end-to-end: seeded root-owned files under both trees were reported (alongside real pre-existing leftovers on the dev host), and the printed `chown` remedy cleared them.
- **Pointer:** `internal/sysexec/tail.go`, `internal/sysexec/tail_test.go` (`TailBuffer`); `runtime/docker/build.go` (`buildBaseImage`, `BuildProfileImage`, `buildErrorTailLines`); `runtime/containerd/image.go` (`buildDockerImage`).

### DF138 — `destroy --all` / wildcard resolves one backend for the whole batch, orphaning sandboxes on other backends (RESOLVED 2026-07-18)

- **Resolved 2026-07-18** — pushed per-sandbox backend resolution down into the library so every caller (`--all`, wildcard, single-name, and the active-work probe) is fixed at once, no CLI change. New `Engine.depsForSandbox` (`internal/orchestrator/engine_lifecycle.go`) loads the sandbox's `environment.json`, and when its recorded `BackendType` differs from the Engine's own it opens a per-sandbox `runtime.New` for the teardown (reusing the Engine's runtime when they match, falling back to it when the metadata is unreadable). `Engine.Destroy` and `Engine.DestroyForOverwrite` now route through it (the latter previously carried the only correct copy of this logic — DF138 was exactly the gap that `DestroyForOverwrite` already closed for clone but `Destroy` did not), and `Engine.NeedsConfirmation` resolves the recorded backend too, failing **safe** (reports needs-confirmation) when that backend can't be opened. **Verified end-to-end on real hardware:** a podman sandbox destroyed via `destroy --all` while the config default backend was docker — pre-fix the docker runtime's Stop/Remove would no-op and orphan the live `yoloai-cli-*` podman container while deleting the record; post-fix the podman container was actually removed. Unit tests cover the non-opening decision branches (reuse-on-match, fallback-on-unreadable). `make check` green.
- **Rides:** **any** release — a non-breaking bug fix; no flag, config, default, or accepted input changes.
- **Discovered:** 2026-07-17 · **Workstream:** pre-release deletion-verb audit
- **Severity:** MEDIUM (orphaned a real instance with the user's work still in it; the on-disk record was deleted, so no yoloai command tracked it until a later same-backend `prune` reaped it)
- **Description:** `internal/cli/lifecycle/destroy.go` resolved the backend once (config default / `SelectContainerBackend`) for a batch; per-sandbox `ResolveBackendForSandbox` was applied only to a single non-wildcard name. For a sandbox created under a *different* backend, `Stop`/`Remove` silently no-op'd (wrong backend), `HasActiveWork` read work through the wrong backend's git path so its refusal was unreliable, and `forceRemoveAll(sandboxDir)` deleted the record anyway (`launch/teardown.go:62`). The real instance was left running, now untracked. The codebase already solved this exact hazard for clone (`DestroyForOverwrite` reads the destination's own `environment.json` backend); `Engine.Destroy` did not.
- **Pointer:** `internal/orchestrator/engine_lifecycle.go` (`depsForSandbox`, `Destroy`, `DestroyForOverwrite`, `NeedsConfirmation`); `internal/orchestrator/engine_lifecycle_test.go`; `internal/orchestrator/launch/teardown.go:62`; `store/environment.go:37` (`BackendType` of record). Related: DF113 (the tart name/instance-adoption sibling, still open).

### DF143 — `destroy --all` counted an already-gone sandbox as a failure, so a concurrent teardown flipped the exit code (RESOLVED 2026-07-18)

- **Resolved 2026-07-18** — `destroyOne` (`internal/cli/lifecycle/destroy.go`) now returns an `alreadyGone` flag: when its `c.Sandbox(name)` pre-flight yields `ErrSandboxNotFound`, the sandbox is already gone, which already satisfies destroy's goal, so it returns `(true, nil)` instead of the error. `executeDestroy` renders that as `"<name>: already gone"` (text) / `Action: "already-gone"` (JSON) and does **not** add it to the failure tally. This aligns the CLI with the library teardown, which has always treated a missing sandbox dir as a no-op (`launch.Teardown` returns `nil, nil`). Regression tests: `TestDestroyOne_AlreadyGoneIsBenign`, `TestExecuteDestroy_AlreadyGoneNotAFailure`.
- **Discovered:** 2026-07-18 · **Workstream:** owner hit it destroying leftover smoke-test sandboxes while the smoke run was still live
- **Severity:** LOW — cosmetic/exit-code only; the sandboxes were in fact destroyed (a subsequent `yoloai ls` was clean). But a non-zero exit on a fully-successful destroy is a real papercut for scripts and for anyone reading the "failed to destroy N sandbox(es)" line.
- **Description:** `--all` snapshots the sandbox list once in `resolveDestroyAll`, then loops. A concurrent teardown (here: the still-running smoke harness tearing down its own `tag-transfer-containerd-vm-*` sandboxes) removes a sandbox dir in the TOCTOU window between the snapshot and its turn in the loop. `destroyOne`'s pre-flight `c.Sandbox(name)` then returns `ErrSandboxNotFound`, which `executeDestroy` counted as a failure. Explicitly-named args cannot hit this — `resolveDestroyArgs` existence-checks them up front — so a not-found in the loop always means "vanished after resolution", i.e. success.
- **Pointer:** `internal/cli/lifecycle/destroy.go` (`destroyOne`, `executeDestroy`); `internal/orchestrator/launch/teardown.go:26-32` (the idempotent library teardown the CLI now matches); `store/paths.go` (`ErrSandboxNotFound`, `RequireSandboxDir`); `client.go` (`Client.Sandbox`).

### DF142 — per-sandbox hostname was wired for the Linux backends but not the tart/apple macOS VMs (RESOLVED 2026-07-18)

- **Resolved 2026-07-18** — split by backend after establishing feasibility on a real Mac:
  - **tart: implemented.** `runSetupScript` now sets the guest hostname in-VM via `sudo scutil --set HostName/LocalHostName/ComputerName <name>` (`runtime/tart/mounts.go`, `setVMHostname`/`hostnameSetCommand`, threaded through `cfg.Hostname`). Verified on a booted `yoloai-base` clone: the default was the base image's generic `Manageds-Virtual-Machine` (identical for *every* tart sandbox), and after the set `hostname` reflected the sandbox name immediately. Passwordless sudo is present in the admin guest and already used across `mounts.go`. The set is best-effort — a failure is logged and swallowed so a cosmetic hostname can never fail Start. Command construction is unit-tested (`mounts_test.go`); the in-VM behavior is integration-only.
  - **apple: deliberately not done.** Two facts, both confirmed on `container` v1.0.0: (1) `container create` has **no `--hostname` flag**, and (2) `sethostname(2)` inside the guest returns `EPERM` unless the container is granted **CAP_SYS_ADMIN** (only `--cap-add ALL`/`CAP_SYS_ADMIN` let PID 1 set it) — a post-start `hostname` exec fails for the same reason. Granting that broad capability for a cosmetic name is the wrong trade. It is also unnecessary: the apple guest's default hostname is already the **full instance name** (`store.InstanceName(principal, name)`, e.g. `yoloai-cli-mysandbox`), which carries the sandbox name rather than an opaque id. Owner steer (2026-07-18): only opaque, no-human-meaning hostnames are worth fixing; where the default is good enough and the fix needs privileges, leave it. If a future `container` release adds `--hostname`, wire it in `Create` (`runtime/apple/apple.go`) like the Docker backend's `container.Config.Hostname`.
  - **seatbelt: out of scope** by construction — it runs agents on the host under the host UTS namespace, so a per-sandbox hostname would rename the Mac.
- **Discovered:** 2026-07-18 · **Workstream:** sandbox-hostname
- **Severity:** LOW (cosmetic — an in-guest status line showed the backend default instead of the sandbox name; no functional impact)
- **Pointer:** `runtime/runtime.go` (`InstanceConfig.Hostname`); `internal/orchestrator/launch/launch.go` (edge resolution, `buildInstanceConfig`); `runtime/tart/mounts.go` (`setVMHostname`, `hostnameSetCommand`), `runtime/tart/tart.go` (`runSetupScript` call), `runtime/tart/mounts_test.go`; `runtime/apple/apple.go` (`Create`, no `--hostname` flag available); `docs/BREAKING-CHANGES.md`.

### DF126 — profile image tags carry no principal, so two principals with a same-named profile collide on one image (RESOLVED 2026-07-17)

- **Resolved 2026-07-17** — added `config.ProfileImageTag(layout, profileName)` (`internal/config/profile.go`), which returns `InstancePrefix(layout.Principal) + profileName` and so goes through D126's empty-principal panic. Routed all three hardcoding sites through it: `ResolveProfileImage` (read side), `profiles.EnsureProfileImage`'s `tag := ...` (write side, `internal/orchestrator/profiles/profile_build.go`), and `ProfileAdmin.Delete`'s cleanup-hint `image := ...` (`profile.go`). Added `config.BaseImage = "yoloai-base"` as the one deliberately-unscoped constant and replaced the three matching literals with it. Extended the existing v4->v5 `PrincipalRename` migrator (`internal/orchestrator/migrate_principal.go`) so `restampPrincipal` also re-stamps `Environment.ImageRef` from `yoloai-<profile>` to `yoloai-<principal>-<profile>` via the same `ProfileImageTag`, leaving `yoloai-base` records untouched — the same unmigrated-sandbox pass D126 already runs, so no second migration was needed. Filed a `## Unreleased` BREAKING-CHANGES entry (`docs/BREAKING-CHANGES.md`, "Profile image tags are principal-scoped").
- **Discovered:** 2026-07-17 · **Workstream:** surveying what else should ride v0.9.0; the archived plan's [open question 4](../archive/plans/cli-is-a-principal.md) left it unanswered and nothing ever filed it
- **Severity:** MEDIUM, latent — it needs two principals sharing one daemon, which nothing in this repo does today (same reachability as DF115 before D126). Consequence if reached: principal A's sandbox silently runs principal B's image. Per D126 decision (4) that is a **naming** matter, not a security boundary — the embedder chooses its principals — so this is filed on the namespacing axis, not as the B2 code-exec issue.
- **Description:** D62 scoped instance names by principal and left image tags unscoped; D126 scoped the CLI but did not reach tags. Three sites built the tag by hardcoding the prefix, none of them via `config.InstancePrefix`:
  - `internal/config/profile.go:274` — `ResolveProfileImage` returned `"yoloai-" + name` (the read side), falling back to `"yoloai-base"`.
  - `internal/orchestrator/profiles/profile_build.go:74` — `tag := "yoloai-" + name` (the write side).
  - `profile.go:274` — `image := "yoloai-" + name` (the delete/cleanup-hint side).
  So two principals each holding a profile named `web` both built and resolved `yoloai-web` in a shared daemon; the second build won and the first principal's sandboxes silently ran it.
- **This is the DF98 class exactly one artifact over, and D126's poka-yoke did not cover it.** D126 made `InstancePrefix` incapable of emitting a bare `"yoloai-"` (it panics on the empty principal) precisely so the hardcode could not be written. These three sites never called it, so they hardcoded the string directly and the gate could not see them. That is worth stating plainly: the poka-yoke guards the *function*, not the *string*, which is the limit DF98 already recorded when a lexical ban on the prefix was rejected. `ProfileImageTag` closes exactly that gap — a fourth caller that hardcodes the prefix is no longer possible without also bypassing a named function everyone else uses.
- **It was not a pure cache rename — it touched persisted state.** `store.Environment.ImageRef` (`store/environment.go:39`) records the resolved tag per sandbox and is read on the launch/restart path (`launch.go:355,877`; `lifecycle/restart.go:197`), with a legacy backfill to `"yoloai-base"` (`environment.go:184-188`). The migrator extension above re-stamps existing records so restart never resolves a tag a rebuild has orphaned.
- **Scope note.** `yoloai-base` is yoloAI's own artifact, byte-identical for every principal, and sharing it is desirable (one copy, one build) — it stays unscoped as `config.BaseImage`. Only *principal-authored* profile images are scoped, per `research/principal-isolation.md` §B2: *"any principal-authored build artifact (custom profile/image) lives in the principal partition with a principal-scoped image tag."* B2's wider question (may principals author profiles at all?) stays the embedder's, per D126(4).
- **Pointer:** `internal/config/profile.go` (`BaseImage`, `ProfileImageTag`, `ResolveProfileImage`); `internal/orchestrator/profiles/profile_build.go`; `profile.go` (`ProfileAdmin.Delete`, `ProfileAdmin.infoBase`); `internal/orchestrator/create/prepare_profile.go`; `internal/orchestrator/migrate_principal.go` (`restampPrincipal`, `restampedImageRef`); `store/environment.go:39,184-188` (`ImageRef` persisted + backfilled); `internal/config/names.go` (`InstancePrefix`, the gate these sites now go through); `docs/BREAKING-CHANGES.md`; `research/principal-isolation.md` §B2; D62; D126(4); DF98.

### DF133 — the provenance hook could not tell "I authored it" from "I never saw it", and blocked its own author (RESOLVED 2026-07-17)

- **Resolved 2026-07-17** — `AUTHORED_TOOLS` now counts a `Write`'s **file_path** (never its body) as provenance, which is the strongest kind there is: composing a file means knowing what it says. Two tests pin the split: authoring clears a citation, and a Write whose *body* merely mentions the doc does not — the latter being the exact hole that WRITE_TOOLS' exclusion from READ_TOOLS exists to close, since otherwise the edit under test clears itself.
- **Discovered:** 2026-07-17 · **Workstream:** writing `agent-failures.md` and `research/uncanny-valley.md` — the hook fired on the commit that created them
- **Severity:** LOW, but the shape is the interesting part: it is a **false accusation**, the failure mode D122 explicitly named as the one that gets a hook disabled (*"an accusation is what gets a hook disabled"*). It had a live, ordinary trigger — create a research doc, then wire it into an index or a sibling doc in the same session — which is simply how a doc gets written. It had not fired before only because no research doc had been authored since the hook landed on 2026-07-15.
- **Description:** `unread_citations` exempted the file *currently* being edited (`cited -= {Path(target).name}`) but nothing authored earlier in the session. `Write` is excluded from `READ_TOOLS` deliberately and correctly — writing a path is not reading it, and without the exclusion the Edit under test would satisfy the check by containing the very citation that triggered it. But that exclusion is about a write's **content**; it silently also discarded the fact that a write's **path** is evidence of authorship. So: `Write(research/uncanny-valley.md)` → cite it from `agent-failures.md` → *"nothing in this session opened that file"*. The only ways through were to drop the citation or open a file you had just composed.
- **The demand was absurd and the action it forced was not.** Told to open the file it had written ten minutes earlier, the agent did — and the read genuinely verified that the claim being made about the doc ("collects moments the interaction stopped resembling a human one in either direction") matched its actual ABOUTME. A gate can be wrong about its reason and right about its remedy; worth remembering before "fixing" one by deleting it.
- **Pointer:** `scripts/check_citation_provenance.py` (`AUTHORED_TOOLS`, `read_tool_inputs`, and the `target` exemption it complements); `scripts/tests/test_citation_provenance.py` (`test_authoring_a_doc_this_session_counts_as_knowing_it`, `test_a_write_whose_body_merely_mentions_the_doc_is_not_authorship`); D122; [DF129](findings-unresolved.md#df129--the-provenance-hook-counts-naming-a-path-as-reading-it-so-a-long-session-launders-its-own-citations) (its opposite: the same hook is too *permissive* about Bash mentions — this one was too strict about authorship, and both come from the same substring-over-tool-inputs design).

### DF125 — D126 made every pre-upgrade orphan permanently unreapable; `system prune` never learned the old prefix (RESOLVED 2026-07-17)

- **Resolved 2026-07-17** by giving each sweep back the CLI's pre-D126 identity — the fix
  [cli-is-a-principal.md](../archive/plans/cli-is-a-principal.md)'s Risks section asked for and the
  implementation skipped. It is deliberately **two different things**, because the backends are:
  - **Label backends (docker, podman, containerd, apple) — permanent, no expiry.** `IsOrphanCandidate`
    now also claims an instance whose `com.yoloai.principal` label is empty, gated on the sweep running
    as the CLI's principal. This is not a compatibility shim: the label is a fact the backend recorded,
    so reading it stays exactly correct while the value simply stops occurring. It is D62's own rule —
    *"enumeration filters by label, not by name-string splitting"* — applied to the identity the CLI
    used to have.
  - **Tart — a heuristic, and the only part with an eventual expiry.** Tart records nothing (DF124), and
    the legacy name is provably ambiguous, so `legacyCLIVMName` claims `yoloai-<S>` minus the namespaces
    that have ever actually been minted (`yoloai-base`, `cli-*`, the test principals' `tNNNNNNN-`). Its
    retirement is tracked as [DF127](findings-deferred.md) with a **trigger, not a release**: a user may
    upgrade 0.8.0 → 0.10.0 directly and still hold legacy VMs, so dropping it on a version schedule would
    abandon exactly the people it exists for.
- **The safety argument, which is why this is not a sandbox-shredder.** Claiming legacy instances is only
  safe because a legacy-named instance is *provably debris* by the time a sweep can see one: `system prune`
  is gated below the current schema, and the v4→v5 migration stamps v5 only after **every** sandbox is
  migrated (any refusal returns before the stamp). Two things now hold that up mechanically rather than by
  comment: `classifySandboxes` also names an unmigrated sandbox's legacy instance into `known` (so a live
  sandbox that merely failed to migrate is protected even if the invariant slips), and
  `TestGateExemptionsAreClosed` pins the gate's exemption set to exactly `help`, `version`, `system migrate`
  — because exempting `prune` would run it below v5, where every LIVE sandbox still carries the legacy name
  and matches nothing in `known`.
- **Verified by execution (2026-07-17, real Docker, dry-run).** The same A/B that exposed the strand now
  flips: `yoloai system prune --dry-run` reports **both** `yoloai-cli-newprobe` and the pre-D126
  `yoloai-strandedprobe`, where before it saw only the former. The gate guard was verified the same way —
  annotating `system prune` as exempt makes both new tests fail and name it.
- Original finding follows.


- **Discovered:** 2026-07-17 · **Workstream:** surveying what else should ride the v0.9.0 release; found by asking what the D126 migration does *not* walk
- **Severity:** MEDIUM, and **time-boxed** — it is a regression this release introduces, and the remedy has a natural expiry (see Disposition). Not data loss: the stranded instance keeps running or sits stopped, holding disk and — on tart — a capped VM slot, which is the scarce resource. The user's recourse is a manual `docker rm` / `tart delete` / `ctr containers delete` **forever**, because no yoloAI command can name it again.
- **Disposition:** FILED, not fixed. **[cli-is-a-principal.md](../archive/plans/cli-is-a-principal.md)'s own Risks section prescribed the fix and the implementation did not deliver it**: *"Mitigate: the migration is the only thing that may rename, it is idempotent, and `system prune` learns the old prefix for one release."* Idempotency landed; the prune half did not. **It must ship in v0.9.0 or not at all** — "for one release" is only meaningful in the release that causes the break; shipped later, the debris is already unreachable and the compatibility window has no one left to serve.
- **Description:** D126's migrator (`internal/orchestrator/migrate_principal.go`) enumerates work from `sandboxesRoot` — it renames the instance of every sandbox that still has a **sandbox dir**. An *orphan* is by definition an instance whose sandbox dir is gone (crash debris, or [DF113](findings-unresolved.md#df113--destroy-frees-the-sandbox-name-while-leaving-the-instance-behind-so-the-next-start-adopts-a-vm-it-never-provisioned)'s destroy-frees-the-name path). Such instances are therefore never migrated, keep their pre-D126 `yoloai-<name>` identity — and every post-D126 sweep now filters them out:
  - **label-equality backends** (docker, podman, containerd's container sweep, apple since `da4d1106`): `instanceLabels` always stamped `LabelPrincipal`, so a pre-D126 CLI instance carries `com.yoloai.principal: ""`. `runtime.IsOrphanCandidate` (`runtime/orphan.go:26-30`) evaluates `"" == "cli"` → **false**.
  - **prefix backends** (tart `prune.go:31,36`): `HasPrefix("yoloai-mybox", "yoloai-cli-")` → **false**.
  - **containerd's netns sweep** (`prune.go:109,133`): requires the stripped name to carry `yoloai-cli-`; an old `yoloai-yoloai-mybox` netns does not.
  - **seatbelt is unaffected** — its identity is a path under its own `SandboxesDir()` and a *bare* dir name, which never carried the prefix (the same property that kept it out of DF115).
- **Verified by execution (2026-07-17, Linux + real Docker, dry-run).** Two identical orphans were planted, differing only in the principal label, and the real `yoloai system prune --dry-run` was run against them: `yoloai-strandedprobe` (`principal=""`, the pre-D126 shape) is **not** selected; `yoloai-cli-newprobe` (`principal="cli"`) **is** selected and reported as `container yoloai-cli-newprobe`. So the sweep is healthy and the omission is exactly the cross-version one. Docker was verified live; the tart/containerd/apple rows above are read from code, not executed.
- **The legacy identity is NOT recoverable from the instance name — proved, not assumed (2026-07-17).** The pre-D126 elided namespace `yoloai-<S>` **overlaps every principal namespace**, because a `SandboxName` may contain `-` while a `PrincipalSegment` may not. Verified by execution against the real grammars: `InstanceName("", "acme-probe")`, `InstanceName("", "cli-foo")` and `InstanceName("", "my-app")` are string-identical to principal `acme`+`probe`, `cli`+`foo`, and `my`+`app` respectively — every component a legal input the parsers accept. **D62's non-collision proof does not cover this**: *"the default value is forbidden as a real `PrincipalSegment`, so a real principal can never collide with the elided form"* establishes only that nobody can *register* `""`; the collision needs no such registration, only a hyphen in a sandbox name — and D62's own motivating example name is `my-app`. This is the third gap found in that proof's reach (DF115 found it silent on prefix *containment*; this is *segment ambiguity*). Note new-vs-new **is** disjoint — `principalSegmentRe` forbids separators, so the first `-` always delimits the principal — so D126's structural guarantee holds going forward; it is only legacy-vs-new that overlaps.
- **Shape of the fix (not yet decided), which the above splits in two.**
  - **Label backends (docker, podman, containerd, apple) — permanent and exact, no expiry.** `instanceLabels` has always stamped both labels, so `LabelSandbox` present **and** `LabelPrincipal == ""` is a *recorded fact* meaning "created by a pre-D126 CLI". Honouring it is not a compatibility hack and needs no sunset — it is precisely D62's own rule (*"runtime enumeration filters by label, not by name-string splitting"*) applied to a value that will simply stop occurring. Admit it **only** when the layout's principal is the CLI's.
  - **Tart — the one real gap, and it must NOT be fixed by admitting `yoloai-<name>`.** That prefix matches `yoloai-acme-*` too, i.e. it rebuilds DF115 by hand. Tart stores no labels (DF124, resolved by retraction: for a label-less backend *"their identity is the instance name plus the DataDir"*), so the name is the only signal — and **that resolution's reasoning holds only for names minted after D126.** It rests on *"principal namespaces are disjoint at the parse boundary, so the name already carries the owner"*, which is exactly the property legacy names lack: they predate the parse boundary and overlap it. DF124 is not wrong; its guarantee simply starts at D126, and this finding is the debris on the far side of that line. The pragmatic matcher — sufficient for *what has actually existed*, which is the only population there has ever been — is: legacy ⟺ `yoloai-<S>` excluding `provisionedImageName` (`yoloai-base`), `cli-*`, and the test-principal shape `^t[0-9]{7}-`. Integrator principals on tart have never existed; if one ever does, this over-reaches, so **this half is dated and deleted next release** while the label half is not.
- **Pointer:** `internal/orchestrator/migrate_principal.go` (`unmigratedSandboxNames` — what the migration walks, and does not); `runtime/orphan.go:26-30` (`IsOrphanCandidate`); `internal/orchestrator/launch/launch.go` (`instanceLabels`, which always stamps the principal, so pre-D126 = `""`); `runtime/tart/prune.go:31,36`; `runtime/containerd/prune.go:109,133`; `store/paths.go` (`LegacyCLIInstanceName`); [cli-is-a-principal.md](../archive/plans/cli-is-a-principal.md) § Risks; D126; [DF113](findings-unresolved.md#df113--destroy-frees-the-sandbox-name-while-leaving-the-instance-behind-so-the-next-start-adopts-a-vm-it-never-provisioned) (which manufactures the debris this strands).

### DF115 — tart's and apple's prune match by name PREFIX, so the empty-principal CLI's sweep reaps every other principal's instances (RESOLVED 2026-07-17)

- **Resolved** across D126 and the apple label-equality fix. Fix (a) landed with D126: the empty principal is gone, so no namespace contains another and the live hazard the title names cannot be constructed. Fix (b) landed for containerd's `reconcileBlockingContainers` and now for **apple**, which lists `container list --all --format json` and filters through `runtime.IsOrphanCandidate` — verified against the live CLI 1.0.0 on Apple Silicon: labels ride at `configuration.labels`, so one list call suffices and the feared per-name `container inspect` fanout was never needed. **seatbelt** was struck as never affected (2026-07-16). **tart** is closed by decision rather than by code — see below. containerd's netns sweep keeps name-matching by necessity: a netns carries no label to compare.
- **The remaining rationale was wrong, and stating that is the substance of this closure.** The 2026-07-16 disposition kept the row open as "a belt against reintroduction", reasoning that prefix-matching is correct "only because no principal is a prefix of another, an invariant nothing enforces at the sweep". That invariant *is* enforced — at the parse boundary, which is where invariants belong. `principalSegmentRe` is `^[A-Za-z0-9]+$` (`internal/config/names.go:42`) and `InstancePrefix` appends a delimiting `-`, so `yoloai-cli-` cannot prefix `yoloai-clix-foo`: the character after `cli` is `x`, never the `-` the prefix requires. Distinct namespaces are structurally non-prefixing for every principal `ParsePrincipalSegment` can mint.
- **So the prescribed regression test proves nothing — measured, not reasoned.** The fix instruction was "plant an instance under a foreign principal and assert the sweep spares it". Built exactly that way against live hardware, that test **passes against the unfixed name-prefix predicate**, because two valid principals are disjoint by construction and both predicates therefore spare the foreign instance. Confirmed by mutating the predicate back to `strings.HasPrefix` and re-running: still green. A test that cannot fail is worse than no test — it certifies the belt while exercising the one thing that was never at risk. This is DF115's own lesson recurring: the finding twice mis-inferred behavior from a name (`InstancePrefix` in seatbelt's prune), and here it mis-inferred a hazard from a prefix.
- **What the fix actually buys** is the other half of `IsOrphanCandidate`, which the finding never emphasised: a container merely NAMED `yoloai-<principal>-*` that yoloai never created carries no `com.yoloai.sandbox` label, and the old predicate destroyed it — a hand-run `container run --name yoloai-cli-x` was collateral damage of `yoloai system prune`. That case is **live, not latent**, and it is the assertion `TestApple_PruneSparesForeignPrincipal` actually fails on when the predicate regresses. The cross-principal assertion is kept beside it as a belt, with a note saying it does not discriminate.
- **tart — closed by decision (2026-07-17, owner's call): for a label-less backend the name IS the identity.** Not implementable as specified, and a side index is not worth building. Tart has no native labels; the map reaches disk only as an incidental whole-struct `json.Marshal(cfg)` into `sandboxDir/backend/instance.json` (`tart.go:272,280`), and a tart orphan is *by definition* a VM whose sandbox dir is gone — the label store vanishes exactly when it would be needed, so D62's "store the map in the sandbox dir" is circular. The alternative, a yoloai-owned index keyed outside the sandbox dir, was weighed and rejected: it adds a persistence surface that must stay consistent with reality — a new class of drift — to guard a hazard that structurally cannot occur (see the paragraph above). Tart instance names are `yoloai-<principal>-<name>` with principals disjoint at the parse boundary, which is a sound identity. See [DF124](#df124--instanceconfiglabels-promises-owner-enumeration-that-tart-and-seatbelt-cannot-deliver-resolved-2026-07-17).
- Original finding follows.

> **Title and audit corrected 2026-07-16** (was "tart's and seatbelt's"). **Seatbelt was never
> affected** — see the audit below. The original audit inferred "matches by prefix" from
> `config.InstancePrefix` merely *appearing* in a prune file; for seatbelt that call is a
> `TrimPrefix` normalising the *known* set, not a candidate filter. This is the same misreading the
> plan warned about for containerd's netns literal, made one file over.

- **Discovered:** 2026-07-16 · **Workstream:** the principal-rename revisit ([cli-is-a-principal.md](../archive/plans/cli-is-a-principal.md)); found while assessing D62's justification for the empty-principal sentinel
- **Severity:** MEDIUM when filed; **now latent-only** — D126 (2026-07-16) deleted the empty principal, so every namespace is mutually disjoint and prefix-matching is correct today. An unprincipaled layout can no longer be constructed at all (`InstancePrefix("")` panics). What remains is the *belt*: prefix-matching is right only because no principal is a prefix of another, an invariant nothing enforces at the sweep. Consequence if the invariant were ever broken is destructive (another principal's instances deleted), so the belt stays worth having.
- **Disposition:** PARTIALLY FIXED (2026-07-16, D126); **the remainder is smaller and different than first filed.** Fix (a) landed: [cli-is-a-principal.md](../archive/plans/cli-is-a-principal.md) deleted the empty principal and named the CLI `cli`, so every namespace is mutually disjoint and the live hazard is gone. Fix (b), the label-equality belt, landed for **containerd's `reconcileBlockingContainers`** (`prune.go:259` now uses `runtime.IsOrphanCandidate`, matching its container sweep). The rest, **re-audited against the code 2026-07-16** rather than inherited from the plan's audit:
  - **apple — OWED, feasible, macOS-locked.** The one genuine remaining belt. It already stamps labels natively at create (`-l k=v`, `apple.go:194`), so the data exists in the daemon; only the sweep ignores it, listing `container list --all --quiet` (names only, `apple.go:466`). Fix: list/inspect with labels and filter through `runtime.IsOrphanCandidate`. Needs a Mac to verify.
  - **tart — NOT implementable as specified; needs a design decision first.** Tart has no native labels, and D62's remedy (*"Tart has no native label → store the map in the sandbox dir"*) does not survive the orphan case: labels reach disk only as an incidental whole-struct `json.Marshal(cfg)` into `sandboxDir/backend/instance.json` (`tart.go:272,280`), and a tart orphan is **by definition** a VM whose sandbox dir is gone (`known` is built from sandboxes whose meta loads, `system.go` `classifySandboxes`). The label store vanishes exactly when it is needed — the remedy is circular. A real fix needs identity keyed *outside* the sandbox dir (a yoloai-owned side index), which is a design question, not a predicate swap. Alternative worth weighing: accept that for a label-less backend the **name is** the identity mechanism, which is sound now that principals are disjoint, and close this row.
  - **seatbelt — NOT AFFECTED; row deleted.** It scopes candidates by *path under its own `SandboxesDir()`*: a process belongs to a sandbox only when an argv token resolves under that root (`parseSandboxProcLine` → `sandboxNameFromPath`, `prune.go:130,~160`). Another principal's sandboxes live under *their* DataDir (D59), so their processes are structurally invisible to this sweep — a stronger guarantee than label equality, and `prune.go:66` says so in as many words. The `config.InstancePrefix` at `prune.go:80` is a `TrimPrefix` normalising the known set from instance names to dir names, never a candidate filter.
  - **containerd's netns sweep** keeps name-matching by necessity — a netns carries no label to compare — and is correct now that principals are disjoint.
- **Description:** `InstancePrefix("")` returns `"yoloai-"`, which is a **prefix of** every principal namespace (`"yoloai-acme-"`), so prefix-matching is not principal-disjoint while any principal is empty. Tart selects orphans with `strings.HasPrefix(name, config.InstancePrefix(r.layout.Principal))` (`runtime/tart/prune.go:31,36`); seatbelt does the same (`prune.go:80`, then `strings.TrimPrefix` to normalise `known` to dir names). The `known` set is built from the caller's **own** `SandboxesDir` (`system.go` `classifySandboxes` → `store.InstanceName(s.layout.Principal, name)`), and an integrator's sandboxes live under *their* `DataDir` (D59: the daemon hands each principal a distinct one), so they can never appear in the CLI's `known`. Every branch therefore lines up to delete them: prefix matches, `known` misses, `provisionedImageName` doesn't guard it. `yoloai system prune` from the CLI reaps every integrator's instances.
- **Docker is immune, and the reason indicts the others.** It filters by label **equality** — `runtime.IsOrphanCandidate`, `labels[LabelPrincipal] == string(principal)` (`runtime/orphan.go:26-30`) — so `""` matches only `""`. That is exactly what D62 specified: "Labels carry identity for fail-closed enumeration… runtime enumeration filters by **label, not by name-string splitting** (Tart has no native label → store the map in the sandbox dir)." Tart, seatbelt and apple implement the name-string half D62 explicitly ruled out, and containerd does for two of its three sweeps — four of six backends did not implement the predicate D62 specified.
- **Why D62's own non-collision proof does not cover this.** D62:380 proves the *elided* name can never collide with a real principal's name — `yoloai-foo` vs `yoloai-acme-foo` are distinct strings, guaranteed by forbidding the sentinel as a real segment. True, and irrelevant to sweeps: distinctness of names says nothing about **containment of prefixes**. The research's Q4 "non-collision proof obligation" reasons entirely about name equality (`principal-namespacing.md`), so the gap was never in scope for it either. This is the same shape as DF98 — the empty sentinel makes wrong code work — except no one hardcoded anything here; the prefix is derived correctly and is still a superset.
- **Verified by execution on Apple Silicon, 2026-07-16** (dry-run only — a real unprincipaled prune here would have deleted the developer's `yoloai-embrace`, which is the defect demonstrating itself). Method: clone `yoloai-base` to `yoloai-acme-probe` to model an integrator's instance; build an **unprincipaled** layout rooted at the real `TOP/library`, exactly as `cliutil/layout.go` does; build `known` the way `system.go`'s `classifySandboxes` does (the CLI's own sandboxes, i.e. `[yoloai-embrace]`); call `Prune(ctx, known, dryRun=true)`. Result: the developer's `yoloai-embrace` is **spared** (it is in `known`) and `yoloai-base` is **spared** (the `provisionedImageName` guard) — and `yoloai-acme-probe` is **selected for deletion**. So the sweep is not over-broad in general; it is wrong in exactly one way, the cross-principal one, and no correct `known` set can fix it because another principal's sandboxes live under *their* `DataDir` and are structurally unknowable to the CLI.
- **The audit (2026-07-16; ~~all six backends~~ — there are FIVE: podman has no separate backend, it embeds `docker.Runtime`).** Prefix-matched and therefore affected: **tart** (`prune.go:31,36`), **apple** (`apple.go:473`, `orphanInstances` at `:501`). ~~**seatbelt** (`prune.go:80`)~~ — **struck 2026-07-16: not affected**, it scopes candidates by path under its own `SandboxesDir()`, not by name prefix (see Disposition). **containerd is split**: its *container* sweep is safe (`prune.go:55` uses `IsOrphanCandidate`), but its **netns sweep** (`prune.go:109` → `selectOrphanNetns:133`) and **`reconcileBlockingContainers`** (`prune.go:249`, which stops and removes what it matches) are prefix-matched. Immune: **docker** (label equality) and **podman** (embeds `docker.Runtime`, `podman.go:2`, and inherits its prune). Note the containerd netns path's literal `"yoloai-"` (`prune.go:111`, `selectOrphanNetns:132`) is **not** a DF98 instance despite appearing to be one: it strips `netnsNameFor`'s own convention prefix (`cni.go:128`, `"yoloai-" + containerName`), not the instance prefix — checked, because it looked exactly like one.
- **Pointer:** `internal/config/names.go` (`InstancePrefix` — the `""` branch is now a panic, D126); `runtime/tart/prune.go:31,36` + `runtime/tart/tart.go:272,280` (where labels do and don't go); `runtime/apple/apple.go:194` (labels ARE stamped), `:466,473,501` (the sweep that ignores them); `runtime/seatbelt/prune.go:66,80,130` (why it is NOT affected); [DF124](#df124--instanceconfiglabels-promises-owner-enumeration-that-tart-and-seatbelt-cannot-deliver-resolved-2026-07-17); `runtime/containerd/prune.go:109,133`; `runtime/orphan.go:26-30` (`IsOrphanCandidate`, the D62-conformant path docker uses); `system.go` (`classifySandboxes`, which builds `known`); D62:379-380; DF19 (why sweeps are principal-scoped at all); DF98 (the same root cause, hardcoded); [cli-is-a-principal.md](../archive/plans/cli-is-a-principal.md).

### DF124 — `InstanceConfig.Labels` promises owner-enumeration that tart and seatbelt cannot deliver (RESOLVED 2026-07-17)

- **Resolved by retraction (2026-07-17, owner's call)** — the honest half of the two options the finding named. `runtime/runtime.go` now says what is true: Docker, containerd and Apple stamp labels where the *backend* keeps them, which outlives the sandbox dir and is what lets `IsOrphanCandidate` identify an orphan; Tart and Seatbelt never read the field, and their identity is the instance name plus the DataDir the sandbox lives under. The comment also states the negative explicitly — do not scope work assuming those labels are readable back — because the promise's entire cost was misdirected design work, and a silently-weakened comment would not have stopped the next reader.
- **Implementing it was rejected, not overlooked.** Identity keyed outside the sandbox dir (a yoloai-owned side index) is the only thing that could deliver enumeration-by-owner for tart/seatbelt. It buys nothing today — nothing reads these labels back, and principal namespaces are disjoint at the parse boundary, so the name already carries the owner — while adding a store that must be kept consistent with the VMs it describes. Rejected on YAGNI, with the reasoning recorded here so the option is re-openable rather than re-discovered. See [DF115](#df115--tarts-and-apples-prune-match-by-name-prefix-so-the-empty-principal-clis-sweep-reaps-every-other-principals-instances-resolved-2026-07-17).
- Original finding follows.

- **Discovered:** 2026-07-16 · **Workstream:** re-auditing [DF115](#df115--tarts-and-apples-prune-match-by-name-prefix-so-the-empty-principal-clis-sweep-reaps-every-other-principals-instances-resolved-2026-07-17)'s remaining half after D126 landed; found while asking whether tart could adopt the label-equality predicate at all
- **Severity:** LOW as a live defect (nothing reads these labels back today, so nothing is broken); MEDIUM as a *contract* defect — it is a documented promise on the substrate interface that misdirects design work. It already has: DF115's original fix ("tart and seatbelt adopt label equality") was scoped on the strength of this sentence and is not implementable for either.
- **Disposition:** FILED, not fixed. The honest repair is either to implement the promise (identity keyed outside the sandbox dir — see DF115's tart row) or to retract it and say plainly that tart/seatbelt identity is the instance name and the DataDir path. That is a design decision, not a comment edit, so it is filed rather than papered over.
- **Description:** `runtime/runtime.go:96-101` documents `InstanceConfig.Labels` as: *"Backends with native label support (Docker, containerd) apply them directly; backends that persist their config as JSON (Tart, Seatbelt) carry them in that record **so an embedder can enumerate instances by owner**."* The final clause is false for both named backends:
  - Neither `runtime/tart/` nor `runtime/seatbelt/` contains a single reference to the `Labels` field. They do persist it, but only incidentally — `json.Marshal(cfg)` marshals the whole `InstanceConfig` into `sandboxDir/backend/instance.json` (`tart.go:272,280`; `seatbelt.go:168,172`). Nothing ever reads it back, and nothing marshals it *as labels*.
  - The record lives **inside the sandbox dir**, so it cannot support enumeration by owner in the one case enumeration exists for: an orphan is precisely an instance whose sandbox dir is gone. The labels vanish with the thing that would have identified them.
  - Contrast the backends where the promise holds: docker/containerd stamp labels into the daemon, and `apple.go:194` passes `-l k=v` to its CLI — all three survive the sandbox dir's deletion, which is why `runtime.IsOrphanCandidate` works there.
- **Why it matters beyond tidiness.** D62 chose label equality over name-splitting *"(Tart has no native label → store the map in the sandbox dir)"*, and this comment is that parenthetical hardened into an interface contract. Both share one unexamined assumption: that a sandbox-dir-scoped record can identify an instance whose sandbox dir no longer exists. Every downstream plan that reads this sentence will keep scoping work that cannot be built.
- **Pointer:** `runtime/runtime.go:96-101` (the claim); `runtime/tart/tart.go:272,280` and `runtime/seatbelt/seatbelt.go:168,172` (the incidental marshal); `runtime/apple/apple.go:194` (where it is genuinely honoured); `runtime/orphan.go:26-30` (`IsOrphanCandidate`, the consumer the promise implies); D62:379; [DF115](#df115--tarts-and-apples-prune-match-by-name-prefix-so-the-empty-principal-clis-sweep-reaps-every-other-principals-instances-resolved-2026-07-17).

### DF123 — the default (in-place) reset refreshes only the workdir, silently skipping aux `:copy` dirs, while telling the user everything was reverted (RESOLVED 2026-07-16)

- **Resolved** in workdir-materialization stage 3: `resetInPlace` now loops `meta.AuxDirs()` and
  resyncs each `:copy` dir through `workcopy.Materialize` (InPlaceAndPrune), the same way the
  restart path already reset them. It was a missing caller loop, not a materialization defect, so
  the coordinator did not fix it on its own — the fix rode in with stage 3 because the loop body is
  now one `Materialize` call. Verified by `TestReset_InPlace_ResetsAuxCopyDir`, the DF123 repro
  inverted (aux agent-change reverted, aux upstream arrives); it fails against the missing loop.
- Original finding follows.

- **Discovered:** 2026-07-16 · **Workstream:** none — found while researching the workdir-materialization plan, checking whether the aux path diverges from the workdir path (it does, in two ways; this is the live one).
- **Severity:** MEDIUM — no data loss (the workdir resets correctly, and the aux *source* on the host is never touched), but the reset is incomplete and **misreported**: the agent's changes to an aux `:copy` dir survive a reset that announces "All previous changes have been reverted and any new upstream changes are now present", and upstream changes to the aux source never arrive in the sandbox. A user who resets to discard the agent's aux-dir work, or to pick up an aux-dir upstream change, silently gets neither.
- **Disposition:** PARKED — filed, not fixed. The fix is a caller-loop change (make `resetInPlace` reset aux dirs the way the restart path does), and it is cleanest to land alongside the [workdir-materialization](../archive/plans/workdir-materialization.md) consolidation, whose per-dir `Materialize` is exactly what the loop would call.
- **Description:** `Reset` has two paths. The restart path (`prepareResetRestart`) resets the workdir **and** aux dirs — `resetWorkdir` then `resetAuxDirs` (`reset.go:325,331`). The in-place path (`resetInPlace`, the **default** when the container is running on a HostSide backend) resets **only** the workdir: it calls `resyncWorkCopy(*meta.Workdir(), …)` (`reset.go:439`) and never loops `meta.AuxDirs()`. Nothing masks it — in-place reset deliberately does not recreate the container (the agent keeps running), so there is no VM re-setup or re-copy to cover the gap the way the recreate covers DF122.
- **Verified by execution, 2026-07-16.** A sandbox with a workdir and one aux `:copy` dir, both carrying an agent-created `agent.txt`, both sources advanced with an `upstream.txt`; a running HostSide mock so `Reset` takes the in-place path; then `Reset`:
  - **workdir** → `agent.txt` **absent**, `upstream.txt` **present** — reset as promised.
  - **aux dir** → `agent.txt` **present**, `upstream.txt` **absent** — untouched.
- **Reachability.** HostSide backend (docker/podman/containerd/apple/seatbelt), container running, at least one aux `:copy` dir (`--dir path:copy`, D81 multi-workdir), default `yoloai reset` (no `--restart`). SandboxSide (tart) is unaffected — it always upgrades to restart, which resets aux. So this is HostSide-only, and it is the common path there.
- **A second, non-live divergence noted in passing** (not filed, per the DF122 lesson): `resetAuxCopyDir` (restart path) omits the SandboxSide baseline-deferral that `resetCopyWorkdir` and `createCopyBaseline` both have (`reset.go:216` calls `baseline.WorkCopy` with no `LocalityOf` check). On tart the recreate's unconditional `ExecuteVMWorkDirSetup` re-baselines every copy dir including aux and overwrites it, so it never bites — the same masking that retracted DF122. Recorded here so the consolidation removes the inconsistency; not a bug on its own.
- **Fix:** `resetInPlace` must reset aux `:copy` dirs, mirroring `prepareResetRestart`. The in-place strategy (copy-in-place + prune, no `RemoveAll` of the live-mounted dir) applies to aux dirs identically, so this is a loop, not new logic — and it is one call to the planned `Materialize` per aux dir.
- **Pointer:** `internal/orchestrator/lifecycle/reset.go:429-457` (`resetInPlace`, no aux loop), `:315-333` (`prepareResetRestart`, which does loop via `resetAuxDirs`), `:223-243` (`resetAuxDirs`), `:473-475` (`resetNotification`, the "all reverted" claim it breaks). Related: DF117/DF120 (same in-place function, workdir side), [workdir-materialization.md](../archive/plans/workdir-materialization.md) (the consolidation this fix belongs with).

### DF119 — every backend now confines git, so the confinement gate's downgrade path is dead code, its doc comment is false, and the test mock models a backend that no longer exists (RESOLVED 2026-07-16)

- **Resolved by deletion + enforcement.** The decision this entry parked — delete the downgrade path or keep it for a future unconfined backend — was made: **delete it, and make confinement a hard requirement** so the deletion loses no protection. `workspace.PreserveGit` (the `stripHistory`/`confined` → `preserve`/`downgraded` function) is gone; history preservation is now just `!stripHistory`, inlined in `workcopy.Materialize`. `HistoryNotice.HistoryDowngraded` and create's downgrade warning are gone. The runtime conformance suite's `ConfinementImpliesGitExecer` — which *skipped* an unconfined backend — became `BackendConfinesWorkCopyGit`, which **asserts** every backend confines, so a future unconfined backend fails conformance loudly instead of silently reintroducing the RCE. The divergent `mockDockerRuntime` now confines, like real docker.
- **Why this is safe, not a lost defence.** The downgrade never fired (no backend triggers it) and was only ever a partial mitigation — confinement is the real defence, and `git.NewSandbox`'s dispatch (the *other*, live consumer of `GitRunsInConfinement`) still routes work-copy git into confinement. So the net posture is unchanged today and strictly better against regression: the silent degrade became a loud enforced invariant. The dispatch predicate itself was left untouched.
- **The false comments** (three sites) were already fixed 2026-07-16; that half is unchanged. Original description follows.

- **Discovered:** 2026-07-16 · **Workstream:** none — read while establishing whether the work copy's git ever runs on the host (it does not, which is what makes DF116's blast radius the create path only).
- **Severity:** LOW — nothing misbehaves. It is a correctness-of-the-record problem, and the record is what the next reader reasons from.
- **Disposition:** PARTIALLY ADDRESSED — the false comments are corrected in place (recorded here per rule 7); the dead code and the divergent mock are PARKED, because deleting the downgrade path is a decision about whether an unconfinable backend is ever expected, not a cleanup.
- **Description:** `GitRunsInConfinement` is `FilesystemLocality == LocalitySandboxSide || GitExecInConfinement` (`runtime.go:312-318`). Tart satisfies the first; docker, podman, containerd, apple and seatbelt all set `GitExecInConfinement: true`. **It is therefore true for all six backends**, with three consequences:
  - ~~Its own doc comment still says "False for seatbelt (no container — host git, documented residual)" (`runtime.go:308`)~~ — **fixed 2026-07-16.** The claim was **mirrored in three places**, which is why a grep for it was worth running rather than fixing the one site that was noticed: `runtime.go:308` (`GitRunsInConfinement`), `runtime.go:253-254` (the `GitExecInConfinement` field doc, which also listed only docker/podman/containerd and omitted apple), and `runtime_optional.go:91` (`GitExecer`, "runs host git for the rest (seatbelt, nil)"). All three described the world before seatbelt gained a sandbox-exec profile around git, and all three now say what is true: confinement, not a container, is the predicate, and seatbelt satisfies it by other means. A fourth site is *unreachable rather than false* and is left alone deliberately: `runtimetest/conformance_iface.go:88` skips with "host-side git backend", which no real backend can now trigger — it belongs with the dead-code limb below, not with the comments.
  - `PreserveGit`'s `downgraded` return (`copy_gitignore.go:61-69`) can only fire when `confined` is false, which no real backend now produces — so the warning at `prepare_dirs.go:270-274`, and the `:copy`→copy-strict auto-fallback it announces, are unreachable in production.
  - `mockDockerRuntime` sets `FilesystemLocality` but **not** `GitExecInConfinement` (`create_prepare_test.go:453-461`), so it models a backend no real one matches, and tests through it exercise the dead downgrade path. The warning is plainly visible in `go test ./internal/orchestrator/create/ -v` output, which is how this was noticed.
- **Not verified:** whether the downgrade path should be *deleted* or the gate *loosened* — i.e. whether a future unconfined backend is expected. That is a decision, not a fact, and it belongs with whoever owns [confine-host-side-git](plans/confine-host-side-git.md).
- **Pointer:** `runtime/runtime.go:308` (the false comment), `:312-318` (`GitRunsInConfinement`); `runtime/seatbelt/seatbelt.go:47`, `runtime/docker/docker.go:57`, `runtime/podman/podman.go:43`, `runtime/containerd/containerd.go:50`, `runtime/apple/apple.go:73`, `runtime/tart/tart.go:62`; `internal/workspace/copy_gitignore.go:61-69` (`PreserveGit`); `internal/orchestrator/create/prepare_dirs.go:270-274` (the unreachable warning); `internal/orchestrator/create/create_prepare_test.go:453-461` (the divergent mock).

### DF120 — every reset baselines a dirty source at HEAD where create commits it, so `diff` reports the user's own uncommitted work as the agent's and `apply` then refuses (RESOLVED 2026-07-16)

- **Resolved by removing the second opinion, not by editing three call sites.** `baseline.WorkCopy`
  (`internal/orchestrator/baseline`) is now the only place a copy-mode work copy's baseline is
  established, and create and all three reset paths call it. The entry's own remedy said to prefer
  the shared helper over the three edits precisely because create's one site and reset's three
  drifting apart is what produced this; a fourth caller would have done it again.
- **Verified:** `TestResyncWorkCopy_DirtySource_BaselinesLikeCreate` asserts the property that
  actually matters rather than the mechanism — after a reset of a dirty source, `git diff <baseline>`
  is **empty**, because the agent has done nothing. It fails against the old `HeadSHA`. The helper's
  own tests cover the four shapes a work copy arrives in (dirty repo, clean repo, non-repo, repo with
  no commits).
- **Two adjacent items surfaced while fixing it.** DF121 (`:rw` stored a baseline it does not track)
  was a real defect and is fixed here. DF122 looked like a second one — reset baselining before the
  SandboxSide check — but the hardware test later **disproved** it: a tart reset recreates the VM,
  and the recreate refreshes the work dir unconditionally, so the ordering never mattered. See
  DF122's entry. The consolidation still earned its keep by raising the question; the answer just
  turned out to be "no bug".

- **Discovered:** 2026-07-16 · **Workstream:** none — noticed while reading the baseline logic for [DF117](findings-resolved.md), investigated on request rather than left as a hunch.
- **Severity:** MEDIUM — it fails closed, and nothing is corrupted or lost: `apply` refuses and the host repo is untouched. But `diff` does not fail closed, it answers **wrongly**, and a sandbox reset against a dirty repo cannot deliver its work until the user commits or reverts on the host. Not CRITICAL by the policy above (no data loss, no security, no regression — this is long-standing).
- **Disposition:** PARKED — filed, not fixed. The fix is small and named below, but it changes what every reset records as its baseline, which wants its own change and its own tests rather than a ride on a docs pass.
- **Description:** Create commits a dirty source's work tree into the copy's own repo before handing it to the agent — `createBaselineForGitRepo` → `BaselineUncommittedChanges` (`prepare_dirs.go:357`), the "yoloai: pre-session state" commit — so the agent starts from a clean tree and a diff shows only what the agent did. **No reset does this.** All three reset baseline sites take `HeadSHA` instead: `reset.go:188` (restart, workdir), `:223` (restart, aux dirs), `:438` (in-place, via `resyncWorkCopy`). So after a reset the baseline is the source's HEAD while the work tree holds the source's *uncommitted* content, and everything downstream reads that difference as the agent's doing.
- **Verified by execution, 2026-07-16**, against the real functions with the same fixture (a repo with one commit, `app.js` modified but not committed):
  - **create** (`setupWorkdir`): baseline `dfc53e04` ≠ source HEAD `1b3a037c`; work copy `git status --porcelain` → **empty**; last commit → `"yoloai: pre-session state"`.
  - **reset** (`resyncWorkCopy`): baseline `90d42e0b` **==** source HEAD; work copy status → **`M app.js`**; last commit → `"initial"`.
  - `yoloai diff` after that reset, with the agent having done **nothing**: `app.js | 3 ++-`, `1 file changed, 2 insertions(+), 1 deletion(-)` — the user's own work in progress, attributed to the agent.
  - **`apply` then refuses.** The patch's context is HEAD's content, but the host work tree already holds those lines, so it cannot apply. Reproduced with raw git: `git apply` → **exit 1**, `git apply --check` → **exit 1**, host file unchanged. That is the same command yoloAI gates on — `CheckPatch` runs `git apply --check` (`ops.go:145-149`) and `ApplyDiff` calls it before doing anything (`copyflow/apply.go:95-99`) — so the refusal happens inside yoloAI's own precondition, not by luck.
- **Why this is a real sequence, not a contrived one.** Create *warns* about a dirty source and continues (`checkDirtyRepos`, plus the "changes will be visible to the agent and could be modified or lost" notice), so working from a dirty repo is expected and handled. The reset docs describe the intended use as "host repo got new upstream commits… user wants to update the agent's copy" (`commands.md`) — i.e. reset is for the case where the host has moved on, which is exactly when a developer is also likely to have work in progress.
- **Fix:** have the reset paths baseline the way create does, via `BaselineUncommittedChanges` rather than `HeadSHA`. It commits into the **work copy's** repo, never the source's, so it is safe by the same argument that makes create safe — and since DF116, a work copy can no longer hold a `.git` that points anywhere else. Best done by giving create and reset one shared "baseline this work copy" helper: the three sites drifting from create's one is what produced this, and a fourth caller will do it again. Not verified: whether `:rw` mode has an analogous gap (its baseline is `HeadSHA` of the *source*, `prepare_dirs.go:296`, which may well be right for a live mount) — the check is to reset a `:rw` sandbox against a dirty repo and read what `diff` reports.
- **Pointer:** `internal/orchestrator/lifecycle/reset.go:188`, `:223`, `:438`; `internal/orchestrator/create/prepare_dirs.go:350-360` (`createBaselineForGitRepo`, the behaviour reset should share), `:296` (the `:rw` baseline, unchecked); `internal/git/ops.go:58-79` (`BaselineUncommittedChanges`), `:145-149` (`CheckPatch`); `copyflow/apply.go:95-99` (apply's precondition). Related: DF117 (same function, fixed; this was found reading it).

### DF121 — `:rw` records a baseline it declares it does not track, so `sandbox info` prints one while `yoloai baseline` denies it exists (RESOLVED 2026-07-16)

- **Discovered:** 2026-07-16 · **Workstream:** none — found by checking whether `:rw` shared DF120's gap, which was asked for explicitly. It does not; it has a different defect.
- **Severity:** LOW — nothing misbehaves and no decision rests on the value. It is a product that answers a question two ways.
- **Disposition:** ADDRESSED-IN-PLACE, recorded here per rule 7.
- **Description:** `createDirBaseline`'s default branch recorded `HeadSHA(dir.Path)` — the source's HEAD at create — for `:rw` and `:ro`. **Nothing reads it.** `diff` substitutes the literal `"HEAD"` for a live mount (`copyflow/diff.go:268`), `tags` does the same (`tags.go:47`), `dirWorkReason` returns early for non-copy modes, `reset` refuses `:rw` outright, and `checkBaselineMode` rejects it with a user-facing "baseline is not tracked for :rw directories" (`copyflow/baseline.go:184-185`). One consumer used it: `sandbox info` printed it.
- **Verified by execution against the real CLI, 2026-07-16.** A `:rw` sandbox over a repo with uncommitted work:
  - `environment.json` → `mode=rw  baseline_sha=a713fff7…`
  - `yoloai baseline advance rwprobe` → **"baseline is not tracked for :rw directories"**
  - `yoloai baseline log rwprobe` → **same refusal**
  - `yoloai sandbox rwprobe info` → **`Baseline:    a713fff7b5464e09381114b158af650f579d24d6`**
  - So the product both denies and displays the same baseline, depending on which command is asked. The displayed value is also inert: `RWDiff` runs `git diff HEAD` against the live directory, so it goes stale the moment the user commits, and the number `info` shows then describes nothing at all.
- **`:rw` does not have DF120's gap**, which was the question that started this: there is no work copy to baseline, and the user's edits and the agent's are literally the same edits to the same files, so a live mount cannot separate them and no baseline could. `git diff HEAD` is the honest answer and is what it does.
- **Resolved:** the default branch records nothing, which is what the rest of the code already believed. `sandbox info` prints the Baseline line only when one exists, so `:rw` sandboxes stop claiming one. No consumer changes, because there were none. Tests: `TestCreateDirBaseline_RWTracksNoBaseline`, `TestCreateDirBaseline_RODirTracksNoBaseline`.
- **Pointer:** `internal/orchestrator/create/prepare_dirs.go` (`createDirBaseline`, the default branch); `copyflow/baseline.go:182-190` (`checkBaselineMode`, the contract); `copyflow/diff.go:268` and `internal/orchestrator/tags.go:47` (both substituting `"HEAD"`); `internal/git/ops.go` (`RWDiff`, `git diff HEAD`); `internal/cli/sandboxcmd/info.go:154-156` (the one printer).

### DF122 — ~~reset baselined before checking for a SandboxSide backend, so a tart reset re-copied on the host and left the VM untouched~~ — NOT A BUG, disproven by the hardware test its own entry asked for (2026-07-16)

- **Discovered:** 2026-07-16 · **Workstream:** none — surfaced while consolidating the baseline call sites for DF120. **Retracted 2026-07-16** after the hardware test this entry named was actually run.
- **Severity:** none — there is no defect. Originally filed MEDIUM.
- **Disposition:** RETRACTED. The code change made under this number stays as a consistency cleanup (see below), but it fixes nothing user-visible, so nothing here is a fix.
- **The claim, and why it looked right on paper.** `resetCopyWorkdir` checked `IsGitRepo → HeadSHA` before the SandboxSide deferral, where create checks the deferral first. For tart the staging copy carries a `.git`, so `HeadSHA` returned a SHA, the deferral was skipped, and a non-empty SHA suppresses the `reset.go` gated call to `ExecuteVMWorkDirSetup` (`Mode == "copy" && BaselineSHA == ""`). That reasoning is correct as far as it goes — and it goes one call site too short.
- **Why it is wrong.** A tart reset does not just re-copy; it **recreates the container** (tart is SandboxSide, so `Reset` auto-upgrades to restart, which destroys the VM and boots a new one). And `recreateContainer` re-runs `ExecuteVMWorkDirSetup` **unconditionally** for copy mode — `restart.go`, comment: *"Always re-run when recreating: the old VM was destroyed, so its local work directory no longer exists even if BaselineSHA is already set."* So there are **two** VM-setup calls in a reset, and the unconditional one at `restart.go` always fires. The gated one at `reset.go` that this finding was about is redundant belt-and-braces for tart. The VM is refreshed on every tart reset regardless of what `resetCopyWorkdir` returns.
- **Disproven by execution on real tart, 2026-07-16.** `TestIntegrationTart_ResetRefreshesVMWorkDir` plants a marker inside the VM, moves the host source on with an upstream commit, resets, and checks the VM. It **passes against the pre-fix ordering** (85s): marker **GONE**, upstream **PRESENT**. The reset log shows `reset complete → starting sandbox → recreating container → launching container` — the recreate, and with it the unconditional refresh. If DF122 were real, the marker would have survived; it did not. This is the test the entry itself said would "settle the user-visible half", and it settled it against the finding.
- **What the unit test actually proved.** `TestResetCopyWorkdir_SandboxSide_DefersBaseline` asserts the returned SHA is empty and fails against the old ordering — true, but it tests the *mechanism* (does `resetCopyWorkdir` defer?), not the *consequence* (does the VM get stale?). The consequence never followed from the mechanism, because the second call site covers it. A green unit test on a mechanism is not evidence of the bug the mechanism was blamed for — the lesson DF98 keeps teaching.
- **The code change stays, as cleanup not fix.** `resetCopyWorkdir` now checks the deferral first and routes through `baseline.WorkCopy`, matching create and avoiding a throwaway SHA that `restart.go` overwrites anyway. That is part of DF120's "one helper" consolidation and is harmless; it is retained on those grounds alone. The two tests are kept as guards for the *real* behaviour: `TestIntegrationTart_ResetRefreshesVMWorkDir` fails if `recreateContainer`'s unconditional refresh is ever gated, and the `launch` fake proves `ExecuteVMWorkDirSetup` execs the setup — neither guards a DF122 that does not exist.
- **Pointer:** `internal/orchestrator/lifecycle/restart.go` (`recreateContainer`, the unconditional `ExecuteVMWorkDirSetup`); `internal/orchestrator/lifecycle/reset.go` (`resetCopyWorkdir`, the redundant gated call); `internal/orchestrator/integration_tart_test.go` (`TestIntegrationTart_ResetRefreshesVMWorkDir`, the disproof). Related: DF120 (the consolidation that raised the question).
