### DF116 — a linked worktree as a `:copy-all` workdir commits into the user's real repo, host-side, before the sandbox starts — reintroducing Q91 (RESOLVED 2026-07-16)

- **Resolved:** a work copy can no longer hold a `.git` that names anything outside itself.
  `workspace.RemoveGitLink` drops any non-directory `.git`, and `CopyProjectDir` applies it
  unconditionally, so create, restart-reset and aux dirs are covered by construction rather than
  by each caller remembering. The in-place reset inherits it by calling `CopyProjectDir` too
  (see DF117). Create warns, keyed off the **source**, that a worktree's history was not
  preserved — `:copy` never copied the link either, so the copy side has nothing left to notice.
  Verified against the real CLI: before, one `yoloai new wt:copy-all` moved the user's `feature`
  branch to a "yoloai: pre-session state" commit and dropped a tracked `node_modules/lib.js`
  from it; after, the host repo is untouched. Tests use a real `git worktree add` and each fails
  without the sever. Preserving worktree history properly is a separate,
  unbuilt feature: [worktree-history.md](plans/worktree-history.md).

- **Discovered:** 2026-07-16 · **Workstream:** none — ad-hoc question ("what happens if someone starts yoloai with a workdir that's actually a worktree?"). Not found by an audit; the answer to the question *was* the finding.
- **Severity:** CRITICAL — observable data loss on the host, outside the sandbox, with no agent involved. It violates the one guarantee the whole product rests on: your real directory is not touched. Escalated per the policy above rather than parked.
- **Disposition:** ESCALATED — filed, not fixed. The fix is one line and named below, but it lands in the create path's git handling and wants a test tier that does not exist yet (no test in this repo has ever run `git worktree add`), so it is written down rather than smuggled in.
- **Description:** When the workdir is a **linked worktree**, `.git` is a *file* holding `gitdir: /main/repo/.git/worktrees/<name>`. `:copy-all` short-circuits to `CopyDir` (`copy_gitignore.go:35-37`), which never excludes `.git`, so the gitlink is copied **verbatim** — still an absolute path into the user's real repo. `IsGitRepo` is existence-only (`os.Stat`, `ops.go:347-350`), so the copy tests as a healthy repo. `createCopyBaseline` then runs **unsandboxed host git** — `git.NewHost(d.Layout)`, `create.go:572`, for every backend — which follows the pointer into the real repo, so `HeadSHA` **succeeds**, and control reaches `createBaselineForGitRepo` → `BaselineUncommittedChanges` → `git add -A` + `git commit` against the host worktree's **shared index and branch ref**.
- **The rescue path exists and is skipped precisely when it is needed.** `createBaselineForGitRepo` already has the correct remedy — `RemoveGitDirs` (which *does* handle a `.git` file, `copy.go:310-338`) then a fresh `Baseline` — but it is the `HeadSHA`-*failed* branch (`prepare_dirs.go:336-345`). A gitlink makes `HeadSHA` succeed **by reaching into the host repo**, so the healthy-repo branch is taken instead. The defect and the thing that would have caught it are gated on the same condition, in opposite directions.
- **This is Q91, reintroduced by a refactor.** [questions-resolved.md](questions-resolved.md) §91 diagnosed this exact failure ("the copy's `.git` file still points to the original repo's object store — git operations in the container would affect the host repo") and fixed it in `gitBaseline`: Lstat → remove the link → fresh `git init`. That fix now lives in `copyGitDir` (`copy_gitignore.go:76-91`), which is only reachable from the gitignore-honoring branch. `:copy-all` returns before it. The regression is invisible to the test tier because the only gitlink coverage (`copy_test.go:398-399`, `container_config_test.go:261,278`) exercises `RemoveGitDirs` directly — a function not on the normal create path — using synthetic `gitdir:` strings, never a real `git worktree add`.
- **Verified by execution with real git, 2026-07-16** (against the real functions' exact command sequence, on scratch repos — not against a user repo, for obvious reasons). Method: `git init main` → commit → `git worktree add ../wt -b feature` → `cp -Rp wt wtcopy` (modelling `CopyDir`) → run `IsGitRepo`/`HeadSHA`/`BaselineUncommittedChanges`'s literal commands in the copy. Results:
  - `os.Stat(.git)` → **true** (it is a file); `git rev-parse HEAD` in the copy → **succeeds**, returning the host worktree's HEAD.
  - `git add -A` + `git commit -m "yoloai: pre-session state"` → **succeeds**, and the **original** worktree's `feature` branch moved to the new commit, its `git status` went **clean**, and the object landed in `main/.git`'s store. The user's uncommitted work was committed onto their branch under yoloai's authorship, leaving no visible trace that anything happened.
  - **The data-loss variant, also reproduced:** `CopyDir` strips `node_modules`/`.build`/`DerivedData`/`__pycache__`/`xcuserdata` from the copy *after* cloning (`copy.go:169-190`, via `removeBuildArtifacts`). With a **tracked** `node_modules/lib.js`, those strips surface as staged deletions and the baseline commit **removed the file from the user's real `feature` branch** (`git ls-tree feature` → `app.js` only; the file survives on disk, now untracked).
- **Trigger conditions, exactly.** All three must hold; the third is what makes this more than theoretical: (1) mode is `:copy-all` — the default `:copy` is safe, see below; (2) a HostSide backend, i.e. **everything except tart** (`LocalityHostSide` is the zero value, `runtime.go:286`; tart defers the baseline into the VM and returns an empty SHA, `prepare_dirs.go:308-313`); (3) the copy is dirty against the shared index, since `BaselineUncommittedChanges` early-returns on a clean `status --porcelain` (`ops.go:61-63`). Limb (3) is met either by a **dirty source** — which at least costs the user a `checkDirtyRepos` prompt (`prepare_dirs.go:62`) — **or, with no prompt at all, by a perfectly clean repo that tracks any build-artifact path**, because the strips manufacture the dirt. That second route is the one reproduced above and the reason this is CRITICAL rather than MEDIUM: it needs no user error and emits no warning.
- **Why `:copy` (the default) is safe, and the second defect that hides in it.** `git ls-files` never lists `.git`, so `copyFileList` cannot copy it, and `copyGitDir` then declines the gitlink (`!info.IsDir()` → `return nil`). The copy has no `.git`, `IsGitRepo` is false, and `Baseline` does a fresh `git init` — Q91's intended behaviour. **But it is silent:** the user asked for `:copy` (history preserved, `PreserveGit`) and silently received `:copy-strict` semantics. The `downgraded` warning (`prepare_dirs.go:270-274`) only fires for the unconfined-backend gate, never for this skip, so `git log`/`blame`/`bisect` are simply gone with no explanation. Note the skip's comment cites `copy-mode-history.md` as authority, but that plan documents only the **submodule** case (`copy-mode-history.md:182-184`) and never addresses linked worktrees — the citation is broader than the decision it points at.
- **Tart fails closed — verified 2026-07-16.** Its host-side baseline is deferred, so the host repo is never written; the staged copy carries the gitlink into the VM, where `SetupWorkDirInVM`'s `git init && git add -A && git commit` (`tart.go:195-200`) runs against a `.git` file naming a path the VM does not have. Every one of those commands aborts with `fatal: not a git repository: /…/worktrees/wt`, so `:copy-all` + worktree on tart is a failed create, not a corrupted repo. The message names the host path and no cause, which is a poor error but a safe outcome.
- **Also unfixed at filing: the reset limb, which reaches the *default* mode.** `resetInPlace` — the default reset — syncs with `rsync -a --delete`, which does not go through `CopyProjectDir` and so mirrors the worktree's `.git` file into the work copy, over the baseline repo created at create time (rsync reconciles the dir→file type change). `IsGitRepo` then passes, and `HeadSHA` reads **the host repo's HEAD** through the link and records it as the sandbox's baseline. Verified with real rsync + git: the copy's fresh baseline was replaced by the dangling gitlink and both the copy and the host reported the same HEAD (`fb4dc388`). No host write — `HeadSHA` only reads — but the sandbox's git is broken and its baseline names a commit no repo it can see contains. **This hits plain `:copy` too**, so the default mode is safe at create and broken by the first reset.
- **The fix, and the one this finding first recommended and should not have.** ~~Make `IsGitRepo` `os.Lstat` and reject a non-directory `.git`~~ — **wrong, and dangerous**: `IsGitRepo` serves two caller classes with opposite correct answers, and for a *host* path a linked worktree genuinely **is** a repo. Rejecting it there would send `copyflow/apply.go:95`'s `isGit` false for a worktree target, routing a real apply into `applyNonRepo`'s `git apply --unsafe-paths` path — the traversal hazard [findings-resolved.md](findings-resolved.md) §DF66/C1 exists to describe — and would also break `apply.go:164` (`ApplySeries` refusing a worktree), `tags.go:132` and `transfer_tags.go:46` (silently no tags), and `ops.go:101` (`RWDiff` silently returning *no diff* for a `:rw` worktree). The predicate is not the pivot; the **work copy's contents** are. Fix instead by making a work copy that can never hold a link: sever any non-directory `.git` at the copy boundary (`CopyProjectDir`) and after `resetInPlace`'s rsync, then let `IsGitRepo` keep its existence semantics and report false because the link is simply gone. Warn off the **source**, not the copy — `:copy` never copies the link, so nothing on the copy side could notice it. Do not read the link's target: it may be absolute (worktree), relative (submodule, or a worktree under git 2.48+ `worktree.useRelativePaths`), or a symlink, and none of those is usable from a work copy — the `.git` entry's *kind* decides, never its text. Genuinely preserving worktree history is a feature, not this fix: [worktree-history.md](plans/worktree-history.md).
- **Pointer:** `internal/workspace/copy_gitignore.go:35-37` (the short-circuit), `:76-91` (`copyGitDir`, the Q91 fix's current home); `internal/git/ops.go:347-350` (`IsGitRepo`, the pivot), `:60-77` (`BaselineUncommittedChanges`), `:38` (`Baseline`'s "assumes all .git entries have already been removed" precondition, which `:copy-all` violates); `internal/orchestrator/create/prepare_dirs.go:322-330` (`createCopyBaseline`), `:333-345` (`createBaselineForGitRepo`, the skipped rescue), `:62` (dirty prompt), `:270-274` (the notice that doesn't fire); `internal/orchestrator/create/create.go:572` (`git.NewHost` — unsandboxed, every backend); `internal/workspace/copy.go:169-190` (`isBuildArtifact`), `:310-338` (`RemoveGitDirs`); `runtime/runtime.go:286` (`LocalityHostSide` zero value); `runtime/tart/tart.go:195-200` (in-VM baseline). Related: [questions-resolved.md](questions-resolved.md) §91 (the original fix this regresses), §92 in [questions-abandoned.md](questions-abandoned.md) (why worktrees aren't a copy strategy), [copy-mode-history.md](plans/copy-mode-history.md):182-184.

### DF117 — the default `yoloai reset` rsyncs the host tree verbatim, re-importing the `.gitignore`d secrets `:copy` exists to exclude and the history `--copy-strict` exists to strip (RESOLVED 2026-07-16)

- **Resolved: rsync is gone from reset entirely.** `resyncWorkCopy` now re-copies through
  `CopyProjectDir` — the same dispatch create uses — and then prunes the copy to the file set
  `ProjectFileSet` names, so a reset reproduces the copy create would have made instead of
  approximating it with a second set of rules. Because reset runs create's own copy code, "reset
  honors the copy's semantics" stopped being a property to gate and became true by construction.
- **The design in the plan was not what got built, and the plan records why.** It kept rsync and
  fed it an allowlist (`--files-from`). Dropping rsync outright turned out to be strictly better:
  the whole class of pattern bugs disappears with the patterns, and so does a booby trap the
  investigation turned up — `rsync -a --files-from` does **not** recurse into a listed directory,
  but `rsync -a -r --files-from` **does**, so adding an `-r` that looks redundant next to `-a`
  would silently re-import the artifact trees. The premise for keeping rsync ("a differential
  sync never leaves a window where the tree has vanished") survives without it: `CopyDir` and
  `copyFileList` overwrite **in place** and never replace the destination directory, so the inode
  the container's bind-mount resolves to survives, which is the property that actually mattered.
  Verified with `os.SameFile`, and asserted by `TestResyncWorkCopy_PreservesWorkDirInode`.
- **Cost, accepted:** unchanged files are re-copied rather than skipped, so a reset now costs what
  a create costs. A size+mtime skip in `copyFile` would restore differential speed, but it is the
  same quick check that caused DF118, so it was not added on speculation.
- **Verified:** the six new `TestResyncWorkCopy_*` tests fail against the old rsync behaviour and
  pass after; the three that pass under both are exactly the things rsync already did right
  (discarding agent changes, preserving the inode, baselining a plain dir). Removed rsync as a
  host runtime dependency: `pull-requests.md`'s required-tooling table and the two obsolete
  `exec.LookPath("rsync")` test preconditions went with it. Tart's in-VM rsync is the guest's and
  is untouched.

- **Discovered:** 2026-07-16 · **Workstream:** none — found while verifying the reset limb of DF116. Unrelated to worktrees; DF116 was only the reason anyone read `resetInPlace` closely.
- **Severity:** CRITICAL — a security issue by the policy's own definition. It silently voids the guarantee `:copy` is *for*, and the one `--copy-strict` is *for*, on the command users are told is the safe way to start over.
- **Disposition:** ESCALATED — filed, not fixed. The remedy is a real design task, not a one-liner (see below), and it is not this branch's subject.
- **Description:** `resetInPlace` is the **default** reset — `Reset` only takes the restart path on `--restart` or when the container isn't running (`reset.go:89-99`). It syncs with `rsyncDir`, which is `rsync -a --delete src/ dst/` with **no excludes at all** (`reset.go:481`): it consults neither `.gitignore`, nor `IncludeIgnored`, nor `StripHistory`, nor `isBuildArtifact`, nor `isBugreportFile`. So it mirrors the host directory into the work copy wholesale, re-importing precisely what create went to lengths to keep out. `copy_gitignore.go`'s own ABOUTME states the contract this breaks: gitignored files — "secrets like .env/*.pem, .aws/, local config" — must "never get copied into a sandbox where the agent could read them".
- **Verified by execution, 2026-07-16** (`rsync 3.4.4`, modelling `rsyncDir`'s exact command against a `:copy`-shaped work copy). Source repo gitignores `.env` and `node_modules/`; the work copy is built as `:copy` builds it, with `.env` correctly **absent**. After one `rsync -a --delete orig/ work/`:
  - `.env` → **present in the work copy**, contents `AWS_SECRET_KEY=hunter2`. The agent can read it.
  - `node_modules/` → present, re-importing the build artifacts `CopyDir` strips.
  - `.git/` → present, which is the `--copy-strict` limb: that flag exists for "repos with unrotated secrets in history" (`new.go:72`), and one reset hands the whole history back.
- **Why the obvious fix is wrong.** "Use `CopyProjectDir` like the restart path" is already refuted in place: `rsyncDir`'s comment (`reset.go:473-479`) records that rsync is deliberate, because in-place reset runs with `dst` bind-mounted into a **live** container and a differential sync never leaves a window where the tree vanishes under the agent, which wipe-then-copy would. So the fix has to keep a differential sync while teaching it the copy's file set. **Design: [reset-honors-copy-semantics.md](../archive/plans/reset-honors-copy-semantics.md).**
- **This entry's own first suggestion was wrong** (~~`--exclude-from` fed by `git.ListProjectFiles`, plus `--delete-excluded`~~) and is struck rather than left to be followed. Three reasons, the middle one verified by execution 2026-07-16: (a) `ListProjectFiles` returns the files to *include*, so a denylist needs the complement — a second query that can drift from the one create trusts; (b) rsync excludes are **patterns**, and a file named `weird[1].env` **survives** an `--exclude-from` line of `/weird[1].env`, because rsync reads `[1]` as a character class — the secret leaks *because* we tried to exclude it (backslash-escaping does work in rsync 3.4.4 but is not clearly documented for filter rules, so it is version-dependent behaviour under a security boundary); (c) a denylist **fails open** — anything unexcluded leaks — which is the wrong direction for the one control between an agent and a `.env`. The plan uses an allowlist (`--files-from`, verified to treat paths literally) plus a prune pass, so a file we fail to enumerate is *missing* rather than *leaked*.
- **Pointer:** `internal/orchestrator/lifecycle/reset.go:481` (`rsyncDir`, no excludes), `:473-479` (why rsync, which constrains the fix), `:89-99` (in-place is the default), `:393` (the call); `internal/workspace/copy_gitignore.go:1-5` (the ABOUTME stating the violated contract), `copy.go:169-190` (`isBuildArtifact`); `internal/cli/lifecycle/new.go:72` (`--copy-strict`'s stated purpose). Related: DF116 (found alongside), DF118 (the same rsync, a different defect).

### DF118 — in-place reset's rsync merges the host's `.git` into the work copy's instead of replacing it, and can leave refs pointing at deleted objects (RESOLVED 2026-07-16)

- **Resolved by deleting the merge, not by out-arguing it.** `resyncWorkCopy` removes the work
  copy's `.git` outright and lets `CopyProjectDir` put the source's back, so the two repos are
  never unioned and there is no ref for a quick check to skip. Removing it first also restores the
  CoW clone, which needs a fresh destination — so the correct thing is also the faster one here.
  The narrow same-second mtime coincidence this entry described is now unreachable rather than
  unlikely, which is the better kind of fixed.
- **Verified:** `TestResyncWorkCopy_ReplacesGitRatherThanMergingIt` resyncs a work copy whose
  baseline repo is unrelated to the source's — precisely the case that produced
  `fatal: bad object HEAD` — and asserts the resulting repo is readable and adopts the source's
  history. It fails against the old rsync behaviour.
- Fixed alongside DF117 because they were the same call site, and the `.git` half could not be
  made correct without answering this.

- **Discovered:** 2026-07-16 · **Workstream:** none — surfaced by a test written for DF116, which failed for this reason rather than its own.
- **Severity:** MEDIUM — real but narrow. Corrupting the work copy costs the agent's sandbox, not the user's repo, and the trigger needs a same-second mtime coincidence (below). Filed because the failure mode is silent and the recorded baseline outlives it.
- **Disposition:** PARKED — filed, not fixed. Shares a call site with DF117 and probably a fix; neither belongs in a bugfix branch.
- **Description:** `rsync -a --delete` reconciles trees **file by file**; it does not replace a directory wholesale. When the work copy's `.git` and the host's `.git` are different repositories, the sync unions them: `--delete` removes objects the host lacks, while each ref file is copied only if rsync's quick check says it differs. Ref files are **always 41 bytes**, so size never discriminates them and the decision rests entirely on mtime, at 1-second granularity. If a ref's mtime matches, rsync skips it — and the work copy keeps a ref pointing at an object `--delete` just removed.
- **Verified by execution, 2026-07-16** (pure rsync and git, no yoloAI code — so this is rsync's semantics, not a yoloAI bug in the usual sense). Built two unrelated repos, `orig` (HEAD `ef79a94`) and `work` (HEAD `b7bbd48`), each `git init` + one commit within the same second, then `rsync -a --delete orig/ work/`:
  - `work/.git` → still a directory, **merged** rather than replaced.
  - `git rev-parse HEAD` in `work` → `b7bbd48`, i.e. **work's own old ref survived** the sync.
  - `git log -1` in `work` → **`fatal: bad object HEAD`**. The ref points at an object that no longer exists.
  - `HeadSHA` returns that SHA **without error**, so `resyncWorkCopy` would record a baseline naming a missing object and `diff` would fail later, far from the cause.
- **Production reachability, honestly.** The coincidence is rare, and the common paths escape it: a `:copy` work copy's `.git` is a CoW clone of the host's, so their refs start identical, and any divergence (agent commits, or the user commits) moves one ref's mtime and rsync then copies. The exposed case is a work copy whose `.git` is structurally unrelated to the host's — `--copy-strict`, or a fresh baseline — resynced within a second of the host's last ref write. The reproduction hit it instantly only because a test does everything in the same second. **Not verified against a real backend**; the check that would settle how often this bites is a `--copy-strict` sandbox reset in a loop against a repo committed to concurrently.
- **Pointer:** `internal/orchestrator/lifecycle/reset.go:481` (`rsyncDir`), `resyncWorkCopy` (which reads `HeadSHA` from whatever the merge left behind); `internal/git/ops.go:23-28` (`HeadSHA`, which reports a ref without proving its object exists). The test that must not assert through this: `TestResyncWorkCopy_FromNormalRepo_AdoptsHostHistory` in `internal/orchestrator/lifecycle/reset_resync_test.go`, which starts from an empty work copy for exactly this reason. Related: DF117 (same call site).

### DF111 — in-VM git fails exit 69 because tart setup switches `xcode-select` onto the mounted Xcode *before* accepting its licence — ADDRESSED-IN-PLACE (root cause found; the original diagnosis was wrong)

- **Discovered:** 2026-07-15 · **Workstream:** releasetest on Apple Silicon (post-DF94 rerun); root-caused and fixed on the Mac 2026-07-15
- **Severity:** MEDIUM — a **shipped** bug, not a test bug. D113 routes copy-mode work-copy git **inside** the VM for tart, so real agent work sits in the same window the tests caught. Measured incidence **1 failure in 10** runs of `TestIntegrationTart_GitCorruption`. Not CRITICAL: the window is milliseconds wide in practice, it fails closed (git errors, nothing is corrupted), and production's baseline path already retries around it.
- **Disposition:** ADDRESSED-IN-PLACE — accept the licence **before** the switch, scoped with `DEVELOPER_DIR`. Verified against a live reproduction: **1/10 failures before, 0/40 after** (p ≈ 0.015 that 40 clean runs happen by chance at the observed 10% rate), with zero exit-69 occurrences.
- **Root cause.** `TartBackend.setup` ran `sudo xcode-select --switch <mounted host Xcode>` and only *then* `sudo xcodebuild -license accept`. Apple's `/usr/bin/git` is a shim that resolves the **active** developer dir and exits 69 ("You have not agreed to the Xcode license agreements") whenever that dir is an Xcode this VM has not accepted. Between those two calls every in-VM git fails. The window cannot be closed by provisioning: the acceptance record lives in the VM's own `/Library/Preferences`, so it dies with each ephemeral VM (DF112), and the base image cannot pre-accept because the record is **version-keyed** and the host's future Xcode version is unknowable at build time. Verified on this host: Xcode **26.5**, `IDEXcodeVersionForAgreedToGMLicense` = **26.4** (the host itself had not accepted 26.5), and every fresh VM carried **no** record at all — so the accept is load-bearing on every single start.
- **Why callers land in it.** The host does not wait for this script when no secrets are staged: the barrier at `launch.go:802` is `if hasSecrets`, and `hasSecrets` is literally `secretsDir != ""` (`launch.go:128`) — false for the tier's agent `test`. That barrier's own comment says it exists to stop the host deleting the secrets temp dir before the guest reads it; it is **not** a "setup finished" barrier, and only incidentally lands after `setup()` when it runs at all.
- **The fix.** `sudo env DEVELOPER_DIR=<mounted Xcode> xcodebuild -license accept` **first**, then the switch. `DEVELOPER_DIR` out-ranks `xcode-select` for the shim, so the licence is accepted for the target Xcode while the active dir is still the CommandLineTools git that already works — after which the switch cannot open a gap. Verified in isolation before the loop: on a fresh clone with Xcode mounted `:ro` as the backend mounts it, `switch` → `git` = **exit 69** and `git add` = **exit 69**; with accept-first, git = **exit 0** across the switch and the record reads 26.5.
- **The original diagnosis was wrong, and both its open questions are answered.** It read "the tart base VM's git needs an Xcode licence agreement" and asked whether the acceptance was missing from `yoloai-base` provisioning or inherited from host state. **Neither.** A raw `tart clone yoloai-base` + `tart run` runs `git --version`, `git init`, `git status` and `git add` all at **exit 0**, with `xcode-select -p` = `/Library/Developer/CommandLineTools`, which needs no licence. The base VM's git is fine; the breakage is introduced by yoloai's own setup, seconds later. And `git --version` **does** trigger it — but only once the active dir is an unlicensed Xcode, which is why probing a raw base VM shows nothing and sends the reader toward the base image.
- **Three wrong turns, recorded because each was defensible and each cost real time.**
  1. **The storm.** `backend-idiosyncrasies.md` attributes this exact string (it is in its Symptom Index) to the `xcodebuild -runFirstLaunch` security-scan storm. AGENTS.md says read that file *before* diagnosing a backend problem; skipping it meant re-deriving an answer the file already had a competing entry for. But the entry does not hold here: backgrounding a real `-runFirstLaunch` while probing once per second gave **90/90 exit 0** for git and zero misses for both `which tmux` and a direct stat of `/opt/homebrew/bin/tmux` — its *primary* symptom. Firstlaunch measured **5s and 8s** against its "60-120s+", and its persistence premise is false (DF112).
  2. **"The window is unreachable."** Widening the window to 25s with a temporary sleep and running the test **passed**, which read as proof no caller enters it. It was the opposite of proof: the sleep delays the **accept**, not the switch, so it pushes callers *past* the accept and converts a 10% flake into a certain pass. A negative result from an instrument that suppresses the signal is worth nothing, and this one nearly retired the correct diagnosis.
  3. **"It is not reproducible."** The tier passed ~5 consecutive times, then 9 in a row, and both were read as evidence. At a 10% rate that is unremarkable sampling. What settled it was looping the single test 20× — the failure landed on run 10, verbatim. **A green tier proves nothing about a 10% flake**; only a loop does.
- **What ruled out the rival mechanism, rather than argued against it.** DF114's returncode check was landed in the same commit, so a failed accept now logs `tart.xcode.license.failed` with stderr instead of claiming success. Across all 40 post-fix runs it fired **zero** times — the accept never fails in practice, so the "silently failed accept" mechanism (which produces an identical symptom, permanently) is excluded for these runs by instrument, not by inference. That is also why the check was landed *with* the fix and not after it.
- **Pointer:** `runtime/monitor/sandbox-setup.py` (`TartBackend.setup` — accept-before-switch, plus the returncode check from DF114); `runtime/tart/build.go` (`configureXcodeInVM` — same ordering, same reorder); `internal/orchestrator/launch/launch.go:128,802` (`hasSecrets` gating the barrier); `internal/orchestrator/launch/vmworkdir.go:69,78,115-130` (the retry that let production absorb this); `internal/orchestrator/integration_tart_test.go` (the unwrapped execs that caught it); `runtime/tart/tart.go:719` (Xcode mounted `ReadOnly`). Related: DF114 (landed together, and the instrument that excluded itself), DF112 (why no base image can pre-accept), D113 (in-VM git confinement).

### DF114 — `sandbox-setup.py` never checked the Xcode licence accept, then logged "xcode license accepted" regardless — ADDRESSED-IN-PLACE

- **Discovered:** 2026-07-15 · **Workstream:** DF111 root-cause work on Apple Silicon
- **Severity:** MEDIUM (no live failure was ever caught — but that is the finding: the code was built so one could not be)
- **Disposition:** ADDRESSED-IN-PLACE — the accept's returncode is now checked, and a failure logs `tart.xcode.license.failed` with stderr, to syslog and the structured log. Deliberately **not** retried: a failure here must stay legible, because silently retrying it into working is what would make the next DF111 undiagnosable.
- **Description:** `TartBackend.setup` ran `sudo xcodebuild -license accept`, captured the result, **never inspected `result.returncode`**, and then unconditionally emitted `log_debug("tart.xcode.license.done", "xcode license accepted")`. The neighbouring `xcode-select --switch` checked its returncode and syslogged on failure, so the omission was local to the accept.
- **Verified on hardware — a failed accept is permanent, and it lied.** Fresh `yoloai-base` clone, Xcode mounted `:ro`, replicating setup's exact order with an accept forced to fail (run without root): `xcode-select --switch` → exit 0; `xcodebuild -license accept` → **exit 69**; `git --version` → **exit 69 on 5/5 probes over 5s**; no `IDEXcodeVersionForAgreedToGMLicense` record written. The VM is broken for its whole life — not transiently — and the code's next act was to log that the licence was accepted.
- **It earned its keep immediately.** Landed alongside DF111's fix, it served as the instrument that excluded its own mechanism as DF111's cause: across 40 post-fix runs it fired zero times, establishing that the accept does not fail in practice and that DF111's 1-in-10 was the ordering window, not a silent accept failure. A finding that can rule itself out is worth landing early.
- **Also corrected:** `build.go`'s sibling `configureXcodeInVM` called its accept best-effort with the comment "license might already be accepted or not required". Verified false on both counts — a fresh VM has **no** acceptance record, so it is never already accepted and always required.
- **Pointer:** `runtime/monitor/sandbox-setup.py` (`TartBackend.setup`, the checked accept and `tart.xcode.license.failed`); `runtime/tart/build.go` (`configureXcodeInVM`). Related: DF111 (the symptom this would have hidden), DF112 (why the record cannot survive a VM).

### DF110 — test principals are unique per PROCESS, so a crashed run's leaked VM is adopted by the next run as "already running" — ADDRESSED-IN-PLACE

- **Discovered:** 2026-07-15 · **Workstream:** releasetest on Apple Silicon (post-DF94 rerun); fixed on the Mac 2026-07-15
- **Severity:** MEDIUM (a green tier can be reported against a VM the run never provisioned; here it produced a confusing failure, but the same shape could produce a false PASS)
- **Disposition:** ADDRESSED-IN-PLACE — mechanism reproduced on Apple Silicon by planting a VM at the colliding name, fix verified against that same reproduction, and `make integration-tart` then passed twice back-to-back with nothing cleaned between.
- **Description:** `testutil.UniqueTestPrincipal` mints `t0000001`, `t0000002`, … from an `atomic.Uint64` that **starts over in every process**. Its own doc says so ("process-unique"), and for its stated purpose that is enough: it exists so a test's prune sweep can never touch the developer's real `yoloai-<name>`/`yoloai-base` resources (DF19), and process-scoping achieves that. What it does not give is isolation **between runs** — every run's first tart test is `t0000001`, so `store.InstanceName` yields the identical `yoloai-t0000001-tart-lifecycle` each time.
- **The failure chain, reproduced end-to-end 2026-07-15.** (1) an earlier run died by timeout — DF94 records `panic: test timed out` with a `wait4` stack — and a panic skips `t.Cleanup`, so the VM leaked despite `t.Cleanup(destroySandbox)` at `integration_tart_test.go:114`; (2) the next run recomputed the same instance name; (3) `DetectStatus` found that VM running → `StatusActive` → `lifecycle.Start` took the "already running" no-op (`start.go:370-372`) — note that branch's notice goes to `n.infof`, i.e. into `StartResult.Notices`, **not** the logger, which is why the log shows `starting sandbox` and then simply nothing; (4) work-dir setup therefore never ran, so `BaselineSHA` was empty (`:137`) and the in-VM `git -C` hit a path the leftover VM never had. Cloning `yoloai-base` to `yoloai-t0000001-tart-lifecycle`, booting it, and running the tier reproduces every link: 0.90s against 43–50s for the passing siblings, no `recreating container` line, empty baseline, same exit-128 git error.
- **Correction to the original filing's evidence.** The first version cited the failure text naming `TestIntegrationTart_FullLifecycle2070812500` — "a per-run temp suffix, which is the proof it was provisioned by a different run." **That is not proof, and the inference runs backwards.** The test builds that path from its *own* `t.TempDir()` and hands it to the in-VM git, so the suffix is necessarily the *current* run's: the reproduction shows `2851253160` for the identical failure. It proves the VM lacks the path; it says nothing about who provisioned the VM. The conclusion survived because links (1)–(3) carry it on their own, but the entry asserted a verified-sounding proof it did not have. Recorded because a wrong evidence chain that arrives at a right answer is precisely the kind that gets reused.
- **Also corrected: the "leftovers are still there" intuition.** The absence of `yoloai-t*` VMs after a failing run is not evidence against adoption — the adopting test's own `t.Cleanup` destroys the VM it adopted (confirmed: the planted VM is gone after the run). So the debris is consumed by the very run it breaks, and an operator who looks afterwards finds a clean `tart list`. Steady state looks innocent; that is why this needed a planted reproduction rather than an inspection.
- **Fixed by (a) — reaping, not refusing.** `testutil.ReapLeakedInstances` sweeps the test's own principal namespace before the tier provisions anything, naming each leftover it destroys in the test log. The determinism of `t0000001` is load-bearing and deliberately kept: prune is principal-scoped, so debris stays findable, whereas a PID/random principal would orphan it forever under a name nobody can reconstruct (DF19). Safe by construction — `runtime/tart/prune.go:29-36` scopes the sweep to `InstancePrefix(principal)` (`yoloai-t000NNNN-`) and skips the base image, so it cannot reach a developer's real `yoloai-<name>` or `yoloai-base`. Reaping was chosen over failing loudly (owner's call, 2026-07-15) so a timeout-leak self-heals instead of leaving the tier red; the leftover is still named in the log, so a persistent leak stays visible rather than silent.
- **Verified:** the planted-collision reproduction now logs `reaped leaked vm "yoloai-t0000001-tart-lifecycle"`, provisions its own VM (`recreating container` present), and passes in 126.74s — the same input that produced the 0.90s false start. `YOLOAI_TEST_TART_VM=1 make integration-tart` then passes twice consecutively with nothing cleaned between (all four `TestIntegrationTart_*` plus the `runtime/tart` conformance suite), the second run reaping nothing because the first cleaned up properly.
- **Pointer:** `internal/testutil/isolation.go` (`UniqueTestPrincipal`, `ReapLeakedInstances`), `internal/testutil/tart.go` (`TartStoreLayout` — its doc called the collision concurrent-only and "theoretical"; the sequential-leak case is the one that bit, and is now what the doc describes), `internal/orchestrator/integration_tart_test.go` (setup reaps before create), `runtime/tart/prune.go:29-36` (principal scoping), `internal/orchestrator/lifecycle/start.go:370-372` (the StatusActive no-op). Related: DF94 (the timeouts that leaked the VMs), DF19, D62 (principal namespacing), and **DF113** — the same adopt-what-you-did-not-provision shape in shipped code, filed not fixed.

### DF94 — the Tart lifecycle tier had never run; verify it on Apple Silicon — RESOLVED (D117; first run on Apple Silicon 2026-07-15 found and fixed three latent bugs)

- **Discovered:** 2026-07-15 · **Workstream:** contributor-docs sweep (D117)
- **Severity:** MEDIUM (the tier is now wired on; its first run found three latent bugs)
- **Disposition:** ADDRESSED-IN-PLACE (D117) — first run happened 2026-07-15 on Apple Silicon; see the outcome note below
- **Description:** `internal/orchestrator/integration_tart_test.go` — the tier that boots a real macOS VM and runs `sandbox-setup.py` end to end — was gated on `YOLOAI_TEST_TART`, which **nothing set**: not the Makefile, not CI, not any script. `releasetest` exports `YOLOAI_TEST_TART_VM` (no relation), which gates the *conformance* suite in `runtime/tart/` — a suite that clones multi-GB VMs, exercises start/exec/stop, and never touches `sandbox-setup.py`. So the lifecycle tier self-skipped on the only platform that can run it, while a busy sibling with a near-identical name made the tier look covered. D112's plan quoted this exact line in its scope-gate keep-list and never noticed the two names differed (`plans/mandatory-infra-test-policy.md:114-117` — its own "re-confirm during implementation" note was never actioned). Unified onto `YOLOAI_TEST_TART_VM` under D117, so `make releasetest` now runs it. **This has consequently never executed.** A tier that has never run is unlikely to pass first time; expect unrelated Tart failures and budget for them rather than meeting them mid-release. It immediately found one bug already (the `xcode_mount` TypeError, fixed under D117) — that crash is the kind of thing it exists to catch.
- **Outcome (2026-07-15):** It ran, and the prediction held — a tier that has never run does not pass first time. It did not fail at what it tests; it failed before reaching it, three times over. (1) **It re-downloaded the ~30 GB base image on every test.** `tartIntegrationSetup` built its Layout from `testutil.IsolatedHome`, so `TART_HOME` (→ `<HomeDir>/.tart`) pointed at an empty temp store instead of the real `~/.tart` where `yoloai-base` lives — DF19 exactly, in a suite whose own neighbours documented the trap. At ~15 MB/s that is ~35 min per test against a 10m timeout, so it presented as `panic: test timed out` with a `wait4` stack, indistinguishable from a wedged VM. Fixed by `testutil.TartStoreLayout`: temp DataDir for state, real HomeDir for tart's store, unique principal for instance names. (2) **`EnsureSetup` rebuilt `yoloai-base` per test** — `needsBuild` reads its checksum from `layout.CacheDir()`, under the isolated DataDir, so the record was never present and it re-cloned/re-provisioned a ~29 GB VM into the real `~/.tart` every time. Fixed by seeding via the new exported `(*tart.Runtime).RecordBuildChecksum`, mirroring what the docker tier already does in this package's TestMain. (3) **Three of the four tests never started their VM** — `FullLifecycle`, `MultipleAuxDirs` and `GitCorruption` went straight from `createSandbox` to `WaitForActive`, but create only provisions (see `startAndWaitActive`'s own doc); only `VMLocalStorageVerification` had it right. `FullLifecycle` additionally asserted a `BaselineSHA` that only `Start` populates. All three now start explicitly. Cost note: none of this was findable from the failure output, because `EnsureSetup` was handed `io.Discard` — see DF97.
- **Pointer:** `internal/orchestrator/integration_tart_test.go:47`, `Makefile:405` (releasetest), `runtime/tart/integration_test.go:78`; `internal/testutil/tart.go`; `runtime/tart/build.go` (`RecordBuildChecksum`); DF96, DF97.

### DF97 — tests discard the output of slow, failure-prone setup, turning every stall into an unreadable hang — RESOLVED (gated: TestRepoHygiene_IntegrationSetupOutput_IsNotDiscarded)

- **Discovered:** 2026-07-15 · **Workstream:** DF94 Tart lifecycle verification (D117)
- **Severity:** MEDIUM (it does not break the build; it removes the evidence needed to diagnose one, repeatedly and by default)
- **Disposition:** ADDRESSED-IN-PLACE — the discard gate (`TestRepoHygiene_IntegrationSetupOutput_IsNotDiscarded`) now bans `io.Discard` as an argument to `EnsureSetup`/`Setup` in any file requiring the `integration` build tag, and the five live sites (`integration_helpers_test.go:169,209,254`; `integration_macos_test.go:84,111`) now pass `testutil.LogWriter(t)`. The build-tag scope is the discriminator: `engine_test.go` passes `io.Discard` to `EnsureSetup` five times against a fake runtime that emits nothing, and every one is correct — a gate flagging those would be dead on arrival. The gate was verified to fail by injecting the banned shape into a tracked file, checking the injection was present and compiling first. The `integration_tart_test.go` / `integration_main_test.go` sites named below were already fixed before this landed; `warmDockerBase` is the `bytes.Buffer`-into-the-error pattern for the no-`*testing.T` case.
- **Description:** `mgr.EnsureSetup(ctx, io.Discard)` appears in the integration test bootstraps, and that one line hid two separate failures in a single session. In `integration_tart_test.go:70` it swallowed the base-image pull banner — `Pulling base macOS VM image` / `This is a one-time download (~30 GB)` — so a ~35-minute download presented as a wedged VM and a bare 10m timeout panic (DF19/DF94). In `integration_main_test.go:100` it swallowed a docker build's stderr, reducing a real failure to `EnsureSetup failed: docker build exited with code 1` with no cause attached. A slow step and a hung step are indistinguishable once their output is gone, and that is precisely when you need it. **A blanket forbidigo ban on `io.Discard` is the wrong fix**: of ~60 sites, most production ones (`client.go:133`, `system.go:427/724`, `engine.go:233`, `launch.go:1242`, `ptybridge/bridge.go:71`, the `--json` writers in `cli/system` and `cli/lifecycle`) are `if out == nil { out = io.Discard }` — the correct implementation of a documented `Default: io.Discard` contract — so a ban would force ~20 reflexive nolints and teach the habit that defeats it. The rule wanted is narrower and argument-positional: in `_test.go`, `io.Discard` must not be the output argument to a chatty, long-running callee (`EnsureSetup`, `Setup`). forbidigo matches the expression, not the call context, so it cannot express this; `repo_hygiene_test.go` already parses Go and can. `testutil.LogWriter(t)` (added under D117) is the replacement, so the rule has somewhere to point.
- **Pointer:** `internal/orchestrator/integration_main_test.go:100`; `internal/testutil/logwriter.go`; `repo_hygiene_test.go` (the AST-rule host); DF94.

### DF98 — every host-state backend hardcoded the instance prefix: tart, seatbelt and containerd — RESOLVED (D117/D119; all three fixed, each unit-pinned)

- **Discovered:** 2026-07-15 · **Workstream:** DF94 Tart lifecycle verification (D117)
- **Severity:** MEDIUM — a principal-scoped Layout broke every sandbox path on three of six backends; unreachable without a principal, which is why nothing caught it
- **Disposition:** ADDRESSED-IN-PLACE (D117/D119) — all three fixed and unit-pinned
- **Description:** `runtime/seatbelt/seatbelt.go` declared `const instancePrefix = "yoloai-"` and stripped it in `sandboxName`, while `prune.go:80` derived its prefix properly from `config.InstancePrefix(r.layout.Principal)` — the same self-contradiction D117 found in tart. It was **confirmed reachable**, and more broadly than tart: the orchestrator hands the backend an instance name (`launch.go:146` builds `store.InstanceName(st.Layout.Principal, st.Name)` into `InstanceConfig.Name`), and nine call sites — Create, Start, Stop, Remove, the log paths — did `filepath.Join(r.layout.SandboxesDir(), sandboxName(...))`. With `Principal: "acme"`, stripping only `yoloai-` from `yoloai-acme-mybox` leaves `acme-mybox`, so every one of those paths pointed at a directory that never existed. Fixed exactly as tart's was: `instancePrefix` and `sandboxName` became methods deriving from `r.layout.Principal`. **The initial filing of this finding guessed it was probably unreachable, reasoning from `ResolveCopyMount` taking a sandbox name; that guess was wrong and cost two minutes to disprove.** The general lesson is the one D117 keeps re-learning: no test set a principal against any backend, so a public API (`ClientCreateOptions.Principal`, D58/D59) was broken on two of them simultaneously. The durable fix is a cheap gate — ban the `"yoloai-"` literal outside `internal/config/names.go` — since both defects are a hardcoded string a grep can find, and neither needed a VM to catch.
- **The third instance, and the pattern (2026-07-19 follow-up):** `runtime/containerd/lifecycle.go`'s `sandboxDirForName` did the same thing — `strings.TrimPrefix(name, "yoloai-")` feeding `SandboxesDir()` — reached from eight call sites (Create, Start, logs, CNI setup). Fixed and pinned the same way; verified by running the test on real Linux in a container, and by confirming it fails against the old logic with `sandboxes/acme-mybox`. **The three affected backends are exactly the three that keep sandbox state on the host filesystem** (tart, seatbelt, containerd). docker, podman and apple never derive a host path from an instance name — their state lives in a daemon — so they were structurally immune rather than more careful. That is the shape of the class: the bug is in the instance-name → host-path mapping, and it exists wherever that mapping does.
- **Why no gate catches this**, though one was attempted (D119's proposed lexical ban on the `"yoloai-"` prefix): the defect is semantic, not lexical. `"yoloai-"` names at least three distinct things in this repo — the instance prefix, containerd's deliberate netns marker (`cni.go:128`, documented at `prune.go:125`, which prepends it to a name that *already* carries the instance prefix), and profile image tags (`profile_build.go:74`). Only the first is principal-scoped, and a lexical ban cannot tell them apart, so it would need a standing allowlist — which D121 argues is its own rot. The durable check is a per-backend principal-scoped unit test, now present on all three; the open question is whether it should live in the shared `runtimetest` conformance harness instead, so a future backend inherits it rather than remembering it.
- **Pointer:** `runtime/tart/tart.go`, `runtime/seatbelt/seatbelt.go`, `runtime/containerd/lifecycle.go`; their `*_test.go` principal-scoped cases; `internal/orchestrator/launch/launch.go:146`; `client.go:124`; `internal/config/names.go` (`InstancePrefix`, the authoritative source); DF94.

### DF100 — two different gates in `repo_hygiene_test.go` both called themselves "Gate C" — RESOLVED (gates renamed for what they check)

- **Discovered:** 2026-07-15 · **Workstream:** the D119-D121 agent-error gates plan (`design/plans/agent-error-gates.md`), while allocating a letter for a new gate
- **Severity:** LOW (a naming collision in test-file prose; nothing miscompiled, and both gates ran)
- **Disposition:** ADDRESSED-IN-PLACE — the letters are gone. Each gate is now named for what it checks: the ABOUTME gate, the citation gate, the test-gate liveness gate, the complexity gate, the discard gate. A subject name cannot collide the way a positional letter can, and the test function names underneath were already unique because the compiler enforces it — the letters were a second, hand-maintained identifier for things Go already names uniquely.
- **Description:** `TestRepoHygiene_TestGates_AreSetBySomething` introduced itself as "Gate C", and so did the complexity-suppression pair (its section header, then "Gate C part 1" and "Gate C part 2"). So `Gate C` resolved to two unrelated gates, while the file's own scope log printed "Gate C scope:" for only one of them. The section headers (A, B, then C on the complexity pair) disagreed with the per-test labels. This was the duplicate-ID class of D121 and `scripts/next-id.sh` one level up: the letters were allocated by reading the file for the highest one, which is the same partial search that mints duplicate D-numbers.
- **Why renaming was rejected:** relabelling the complexity pair to a free letter would have re-armed the trap for the next gate, which is exactly what D121's rejected alternatives warn about ("update 'Thirteen' to 'Sixteen' ... re-arms the trap for §17. The count had to go, not be corrected"). An ordered list of hand-assigned letters is the class; removing the class removes the collision.
- **Pointer:** `repo_hygiene_test.go` (gate section headers and per-test docs); the swept references in `findings-unresolved.md` and `design/plans/`. `decisions/working-notes.md` keeps its letter references — append-only history is exempt from the sweep (AGENTS.md rule 2), and D122's text is frozen as written.

### DF85 — Codex 0.144 folder-trust onboarding prompt blocks the agent (untrusted workdir → agent exits) — RESOLVED (applyWorkdirTrust runs on every Codex launch)

- **Discovered:** 2026-07-14 · **Workstream:** DF82 broker generalization (D115) — unmasked once brokering cleared the Codex login blocker.
- **Severity:** MEDIUM (Codex is non-functional on 0.144 for any workdir the user hasn't previously trusted on the host; independent of brokering — affects `--no-broker` too).
- **Disposition:** ADDRESSED-IN-PLACE — the launch path now marks the container workdir trusted on every Codex launch (see implementation below); verified live (0 trust prompts, agent runs in the workdir, prompt delivered). Kept here as a record; the `Description`/`Trigger` capture the behavior for future reference.
- **Implementation:** `agent.Definition.WorkdirTrust` (a new declarative `*WorkdirTrustPatch{RelPath, Patch}`) is applied unconditionally at launch by `launch.applyWorkdirTrust` (in `LaunchContainer`, after `brokerCredentials`, before `buildAndStart`), which writes `[projects."<st.Workdir.ResolvedMountPath()>"] trust_level = "trusted"` into `agent-runtime/config.toml` via `agent.patchCodexWorkdirTrust` (a go-toml map round-trip that preserves the user's config and any broker `openai_base_url`). Codex declares it; it runs brokered or not, and re-applies each launch (config.toml is re-seeded from the host every launch).
- **Description:** On launch, Codex 0.144 shows a **"Do you trust the contents of this directory?"** onboarding dialog for any working directory not recorded as trusted in `~/.codex/config.toml` (`[projects."<path>"] trust_level = "trusted"`). yoloAI launches Codex interactively and pastes the user's task prompt, which lands in that dialog instead of Codex's input box, and the agent **exits (status 0)** to a fall-to-shell. The existing launch flags do **not** suppress it: `--dangerously-bypass-approvals-and-sandbox` governs command-execution approvals and `--dangerously-bypass-hook-trust` governs hook trust — neither covers folder trust. Verified: with `[projects."<workdir>"] trust_level = "trusted"` present, Codex shows **0** trust prompts and starts normally. There is **no global trust-all / disable-folder-trust config key** (confirmed against the shipped binary) — trust is strictly per-project-root. So the workdir the container runs Codex in (the mirrored mount path) must be marked trusted at launch. This is the Codex analogue of Claude's onboarding suppression / Gemini's `folderTrust:false`, but it is **workdir-path-dependent**, so it needs a launch-time, path-aware `config.toml` write (a static `ApplySettings` patch can't know the path), and it must apply to **every** Codex launch (brokered or not).
- **Trigger / fix:** at Codex launch, write `[projects."<container-workdir>"] trust_level = "trusted"` into `config.toml` for the resolved container workdir path (yoloAI knows the mount path). Must compose with the broker's `openai_base_url` patch on the same file (`agent.patchCodexBaseURL` already round-trips the whole TOML map, so a shared codex-config step or an added map key is the natural home). Consider also `hide_full_access_warning`/`hide_world_writable_warning` notices if they surface. Revive when someone makes Codex usable end-to-end on 0.144, or when a user reports Codex quitting to a shell right after launch.
- **Pointer:** `internal/agent/agent.go` (codex `InteractiveCmd`/`Definition`), `internal/agent/broker_codex.go` (`patchCodexBaseURL` — the existing codex config.toml patch), `internal/orchestrator/launch/launch.go` (`patchBrokerConfigFiles` — launch-time config patching, but only runs when brokering); `docs/contributors/backend-idiosyncrasies.md` (Codex entry).

### DF86 — a wedged tart vmnet session poisons networking for all NEW VMs on the host — RESOLVED (detection across doctor, ls/info, and the smoke harness)

- **Discovered:** 2026-07-14 · **Workstream:** smoke-test triage (macOS full tier, runs `20260714-164931.720`)
- **Severity:** MEDIUM (whole tart backend unusable on the host while one wedged VM stays running; agents in fresh sandboxes spin on `ConnectionRefused` until the smoke sentinel times out)
- **Disposition:** ADDRESSED-IN-PLACE — full detection surfacing shipped and verified live against the wedged VM: `yoloai doctor` (`runtime/tart/netcheck.go`, `runtime.NetLivenessReporter`; `network: WEDGED`, exit 1), `ls`/`sandbox info` net-health (`runtime.SandboxNetHealthProber`; `idle (net-dead)`, `Net health:` line, `net_health` in `--json`), and smoke-harness fail-fast (pre-flight abort + in-loop check + wedge fingerprint; the lane that burned 2×180s now aborts instantly with the recovery directive). Idiosyncrasies entry extended (scope escalation + new symptom-index row); plan archived to `archive/plans/tart-network-liveness.md`.
- **Description:** The known long-idle vmnet wedge (idiosyncrasies entry of 2026-07-13) was assumed to affect only the wedged VM itself. A full-tier smoke run showed otherwise: with a week-old wedged `tart run` alive, every freshly created tart VM also came up net-dead (`en0` link-local, `bridge100` on the re-picked `192.168.139.x/23` subnet, no `bootpd` serving leases), failing all tart smoke lanes while every other macOS backend passed. Restarting the wedged VM is the recovery for the whole host, not just for that VM.
- **Pointer:** `docs/contributors/backend-idiosyncrasies.md` (vmnet-wedge entry, "Scope escalation"); `runtime/tart/netcheck.go`; smoke run `.testcache/runs/yoloai-smoketest-20260714-164931.720`

### DF87 — vmnet wedge has a second, false-healthy variant: a stale DHCP lease on a superseded subnet — RESOLVED (classifyGuestAddr subnet check; repro pinned by TestNetLiveness_StaleLeaseVM)

- **Discovered:** 2026-07-14 · **Workstream:** DF86 net-liveness validation (the post-restart smoke re-run exposed it)
- **Severity:** MEDIUM (the net-liveness detection shipped for DF86 reported `ok` for a VM that was verifiably net-dead — a false negative is worse than no detection)
- **Disposition:** ADDRESSED-IN-PLACE — `classifyNetLiveness` no longer trusts a non-empty `tart ip` result: the returned address must fall inside some host `bridge*` interface subnet (gathered in-process via `net.Interfaces()`, no extra subprocess). Outside every bridge subnet → wedged with a `stale DHCP lease: guest has <ip> but no host bridge is on that subnet (...)` detail; no bridge interfaces at all → unknown. All three surfaces (doctor, ls/info, smoke) inherit the fix through the shared classifier.
- **Description:** After restarting the DF86-wedged VM, bootpd re-ACKed its old lease from `/var/db/dhcpd_leases` (~400 stale entries): the guest held `192.168.65.2` / gateway `192.168.65.1` while the only vmnet bridge was `192.168.139.3/23` — DNS dead, 100% loss to the bridge — yet `tart ip` returned the address (it reads host lease records, not liveness) and detection said `ok`. Two subtleties verified live: (1) `tart ip` output proves nothing about connectivity; (2) the check must consider **all** `bridge*` interfaces, not just `bridge100` — vmnet can allocate a new bridge per session (a later restart produced a healthy guest at `192.168.65.2` on a fresh `bridge101` at `192.168.65.1/24` while `bridge100` still sat on `192.168.139.x`; that guest verifiably reached the API, so subnet-mismatch-vs-bridge100 alone would have been a false positive).
- **Pointer:** `runtime/tart/netcheck.go` (`classifyGuestAddr`, `hostBridgeSubnets`); `docs/contributors/backend-idiosyncrasies.md` (vmnet-wedge entry, stale-lease variant)

### DF81 — Unpinned runc/crun version floor: no defense against known container-escape CVEs — RESOLVED (advisory version-floor caps for runc + crun, surfaced via doctor)

- **Discovered:** 2026-07-14 · **Workstream:** sandboxing-blog research (gap analysis)
- **Severity:** MEDIUM
- **Disposition:** ADDRESSED-IN-PLACE — advisory version-floor capability checks added for runc (Docker) and crun (Podman), surfaced via `doctor` and a non-blocking launch-time warning (see implementation below).
- **Description:** Neither the Docker nor Podman backend ever checked the host's OCI runtime version. Three runc CVEs disclosed 2025-11-05 (CVE-2025-31133, CVE-2025-52565, CVE-2025-52881 — masked-path/`/dev/console` mount-race container escapes) are fixed only in runc 1.2.8 / 1.3.3 / 1.4.0-rc.3+; a host on an older runc has a live escape with no warning from yoloAI at any point. crun has an analogous confirmed fix (the `.krun_config.json` symlink-escape, GHSA-f42g-r5jj-qh4j, fixed in crun 1.20) plus a newer masked-path-class fix (CVE-2026-47766) whose exact fixed version tag wasn't pinned down by research — see Trigger below. Added `Advisory bool` to `caps.HostCapability` (`runtime/caps/caps.go`) so a failing check is informational-only: `doctor` reports it via the existing NeedsSetup tier (no doctor code changes needed), while the blocking `CheckIsolationPrerequisites` path (`FormatError`) now skips advisory failures entirely — never blocks, never prompts. A new unconditional check in `launch.LaunchContainer` prints a one-line `Warning: ...` (reusing the existing `filterAvailablePorts` writer precedent) whenever a sandbox actually launches against a below-floor runtime, covering `new`/`start`/`restart`/`reset` uniformly.
- **Trigger / fix:** already implemented this pass. Follow-up (now resolved): crun **1.28** (released 2026-05-27) confirmed as the fix for CVE-2026-47766 (changelog: "do not follow rootfs /dev symlinks"), verified against the upstream release notes. The crun floor has been raised from 1.20 to 1.28 (`crunVersionFloorMeets` in `runtime/podman/caps.go`) — 1.28 supersedes 1.20, so the higher floor alone now covers both fixes.
- **Pointer:** `runtime/caps/caps.go`, `runtime/caps/common.go` (`NewOCIRuntimeVersionFloor`), `runtime/docker/caps.go`, `runtime/podman/caps.go`, `internal/orchestrator/launch/launch.go` (`LaunchContainer`).

### DF83 — vm-enhanced (Kata+Firecracker) CVE exposure verified: no floor needed now, but the install hint is broken — RESOLVED (CVE question verified; install hint repointed at a live doc)

- **Discovered:** 2026-07-14 · **Workstream:** sandboxing-blog research (gap analysis)
- **Severity:** LOW (verified no live CVE exposure) / MEDIUM for the separate install-hint defect
- **Disposition:** ADDRESSED-IN-PLACE for the CVE question (verified, no action needed); ADDRESSED-IN-PLACE for the install-hint defect (fixed this pass)
- **Description:** Two CVE IDs surfaced in earlier secondary-source research (CVE-2026-5747, CVE-2026-1386) were verified against NVD and the firecracker-microvm GitHub security advisories directly. Both are real, both are genuine Firecracker VMM vulnerabilities: **CVE-2026-5747** (GHSA-776c-mpj7-jm3r, virtio-pci OOB write, opt-in `--enable-pci` only, CVSS 7.5–8.7, affects 1.13.0–1.14.3 and 1.15.0, fixed in 1.14.4/1.15.1) and **CVE-2026-1386** (GHSA-36j2-f825-qvgc, jailer symlink-following arbitrary host-file overwrite, CVSS 6.0, affects ≤1.13.1 and 1.14.0, fixed in 1.13.2/1.14.1). Kata Containers' own `versions.yaml` (main branch) pins `assets.hypervisor.firecracker.version: v1.12.1` — below the affected range of both CVEs, so yoloAI's `vm-enhanced` mode has no current exposure to either. Kata itself has six unrelated 2025-2026 CVEs, but the two hypervisor-specific ones (CVE-2026-24834, CVE-2026-47243) affect Cloud Hypervisor/QEMU, not Firecracker. **Separately:** the install hint in `runtime/containerd/caps.go` (`buildKataShimV2Cap`, `buildKataFCShimV2Cap`) and `containerd.go` — `sudo apt install kata-containers` — did not correspond to any currently maintained package; Kata's apt/OBS repo was archived in 2021 and only ever covered the legacy 1.x series. Fixed by replacing all three call sites with the verified-current `kata-manager.sh -o` install-only command (a new shared `kataManagerInstallOnly` constant in `runtime/containerd/caps.go`), confirmed verbatim against upstream `utils/README.md`. Also fixed a second, related instance of doc rot found while verifying this: the devmapper snapshotter's `Fix` step pointed at a dead Kata doc URL (`docs/how-to/containerd-kata-fc-for-ubuntu.md`, 404), replaced with the live `docs/how-to/how-to-use-kata-containers-with-firecracker.md` (verified 200).
- **Trigger / fix:** CVE question needs no action now — revisit if Kata's `versions.yaml` ever bumps Firecracker into the 1.13.0–1.15.0 range without also picking up 1.14.4/1.15.1/1.13.2/1.14.1. Install-hint fix: already implemented this pass.
- **Pointer:** `runtime/containerd/caps.go` (`kataManagerInstallOnly`, `buildKataShimV2Cap`, `buildKataFCShimV2Cap`, `buildDevmapperSnapshotterCap`); `runtime/containerd/containerd.go` (`InstallHint`); upstream `kata-containers/kata-containers` `versions.yaml`, `utils/README.md`.

### DF84 — Direct-delivery (`--no-broker`) Codex is broken for API-key users: the env var doesn't authenticate Codex 0.144 — RESOLVED (applyDirectCredential materializes auth.json when not brokered)

- **Discovered:** 2026-07-14 · **Workstream:** DF82 broker generalization (D115) — surfaced while wiring/verifying Codex brokering.
- **Severity:** MEDIUM (a shipped agent is non-functional for a common auth setup; pre-existing, not a regression — masked until now because brokering is Codex's default path).
- **Disposition:** ADDRESSED-IN-PLACE — the launch path now materializes the real key into `auth.json` when Codex is not brokered (see implementation below); verified live (`--no-broker` Codex: `auth.json` holds the real key, `codex login status` = "Logged in using an API key", no login prompt).
- **Description:** Codex CLI 0.144 does **not** authenticate from a bare `OPENAI_API_KEY`/`CODEX_API_KEY` environment variable — `codex login status` reports "Not logged in" and the TUI prompts for login (verified empirically, see `backend-idiosyncrasies.md` → "Agent CLIs: base-URL override for brokering differs per CLI"). It reads a logged-in `~/.codex/auth.json` (`{"auth_mode":"apikey","OPENAI_API_KEY":<key>}`). yoloAI's **direct** (non-brokered) credential delivery hands Codex the key as an env var / `/run/secrets` file and seeds `auth.json` **only** from a host `~/.codex/auth.json` (an `AuthOnly` seed, skipped when an API key is present). So a user with an `OPENAI_API_KEY` but no prior host `codex login` got a Codex sandbox that immediately asked to log in. D115's **brokered** path already generated a working placeholder `auth.json`; this closes the `--no-broker` / unsupported-backend gap.
- **Implementation:** `agent.Definition.DirectCredentialFile` (a new declarative `*DirectCredentialFile{RelPath, EnvVars, Render}`) is applied by `launch.applyDirectCredential` (in `LaunchContainer`, right after `applyWorkdirTrust`, while `secretEnv` still holds the real key). It writes `auth.json` = `{"auth_mode":"apikey","OPENAI_API_KEY":<real-key>}` (`agent.renderCodexAuth`, shared with the brokered `patchCodexAuth`) **only** when a credential is still present in `secretEnv` — a brokered launch removed it and wrote the placeholder, so this is a no-op then; the direct path (`--no-broker`, unsupported backend, `--network-none`) still has it. Codex declares `EnvVars: ["OPENAI_API_KEY","CODEX_API_KEY"]`.
- **Pointer:** `internal/agent/agent.go` (`DirectCredentialFile`, codex def), `internal/agent/codex.go` (`renderCodexAuth`), `internal/orchestrator/launch/launch.go` (`applyDirectCredential`); `docs/contributors/backend-idiosyncrasies.md` (Codex auth.json entry).

### DF93 — a stray non-version tag `show` is published on origin — RESOLVED

- **Discovered:** 2026-07-15 · **Workstream:** contributor-docs sweep (D116)
- **Severity:** LOW · **Disposition:** RESOLVED (maintainer deleted it 2026-07-15)
- **Description.** `refs/tags/show` was published on origin. It was not a mistyped `git show`: it pointed at the *same annotated tag object* as `v0.5.2` (`7df2a308`), carrying v0.5.2's complete release notes on v0.5.2's commit — a duplicate ref, not a stray commit. Verified identical before deletion, so nothing was lost with it; `v0.5.2` is untouched. Worth noting the release workflow triggers on `v*`, so `show` never could have cut a release — the risk was a reader mistaking it for a version.
- **Fix.** `git push origin :refs/tags/show`.
- **Pointer:** `refs/tags/v0.5.2` (the surviving tag object).

### DF90 — `scripts/audit/*.sh` have zero callers, while `shell.md` holds one up as the canonical example — RESOLVED

- **Discovered:** 2026-07-15 · **Workstream:** contributor-docs sweep (D116)
- **Severity:** LOW · **Disposition:** RESOLVED (deleted 2026-07-15, under D125)
- **Description.** Four scripts under `scripts/audit/` — `error-handling.sh`, `import-graph.sh`, `observability.sh`, `runtime-interface-shape.sh` — each "reproduces the <topic> check from the 2026-05 audit". Nothing called them: not the Makefile, not CI, not another script. The maintainer confirmed never having run one. `shell.md` meanwhile held `error-handling.sh` up as "a canonical example" of the shell standard, and the standards index listed `scripts/audit/*.sh` as a whole category — so the docs pointed contributors at code the project had already stopped using.
- **Fix.** Deleted, and every surface that named them swept (`shell.md` ×4, `standards/README.md` ×1). The canonical example is now `scripts/next-id.sh`, which is live and load-bearing.
- **Why deleting was safe — the checks are gated now, which is the point.** Each script's subject is covered by a linter that runs on every `make check`, rather than by a script nobody ran: `errorlint` (error wrapping/comparison — `error-handling.sh`), `sloglint` (slog conventions — `observability.sh`), `depguard` (import direction — `import-graph.sh`). The fourth, `runtime-interface-shape.sh`, counted methods to answer a one-time design question (F2) that the architecture-remediation program settled. This is D125's rule reaching shell: speculative code is deleted, and a check worth keeping becomes a gate, not a script.
- **Pointer:** `docs/contributors/standards/shell.md`, `.golangci.yml` (errorlint/sloglint/depguard).

### DF89 — the release workflow's node24 action bump is statically verified but has never executed; validate with an rc tag — RESOLVED (v0.9.0-rc.1, 2026-07-15)

- **Discovered:** 2026-07-14 · **Workstream:** node20 action-runtime clearing (post-v0.8.0)
- **Severity:** MEDIUM (the release path is the one flow no local gate can reach; a break surfaces at tag time, i.e. mid-release)
- **Disposition:** PARKED — do before the next real `vX.Y.Z` tag
- **Description:** `goreleaser-action@v6` (node20) → `@v7` (node24) and `attest-build-provenance@v2` (composite → `actions/attest@v2.4.0`, node20) → `actions/attest@v4` (node24) landed on main as `94a3cbe3` + `c2f74355`. Both were verified **statically only**: every input we pass still exists in the new `action.yml`, `.goreleaser.yaml` is `version: 2` matching v7's `~> v2` constraint, `attestations: write` is already granted, and `actions/attest` with no predicate/sbom-path inputs auto-generates SLSA build provenance (upstream README). But `make check` does not and cannot execute workflows — the release job only runs on a `v*` tag push, so none of this has actually run. Validate with an annotated `v0.8.1-rc.1`: `prerelease: auto` marks it a GitHub prerelease, then confirm the 15 assets, the cosign signature, and `gh attestation verify <file> --repo kstenerud/yoloai`. **Fix DF88 first** or the rc clobbers the stable Homebrew cask. Teardown afterwards is `gh release delete` + `git push --delete origin <tag>`; note two residues are permanent and unremovable — the Go module proxy caches any pushed tag immutably (benign: `@latest` ignores prereleases), as does the attestation store.
- **Pointer:** `.github/workflows/release.yml:39` (goreleaser-action), `:52` (actions/attest); commits `94a3cbe3`, `c2f74355`. Related: DF88.
- **Outcome (2026-07-15): it executed, and it all worked.** `v0.9.0-rc.1` cut against `e8038dd1`; run 29443619885, every step green. `goreleaser/goreleaser-action@v7` (node24) and `actions/attest@v4` (node24) both ran for the first time — the two bumps this finding exists for. 15 assets (bins/deb/rpm/sbom + `checksums.txt.sig`/`.pem`), `prerelease: true`, and `releases/latest` still correctly serves v0.8.0. The SLSA provenance is real and bound to the artifact: hashing the actual downloaded `linux_amd64.tar.gz` and querying `/attestations/sha256:<digest>` returns a sigstore bundle. **DF88's guard held** — the tap's newest commit is still v0.8.0 and the cask still serves `version "0.8.0"`, so the rc did not clobber stable. The prerelease skip added the same day let the rc past the changelog gate with `## Unreleased` still populated, exactly as designed (its entries ship under the real v0.9.0).
- **Full acceptance discharged 2026-07-15.** `gh attestation verify` was run on the Mac against **all 9 attested subjects** (4 tar.gz, 2 deb, 2 rpm, checksums.txt — the `dist/*.tar.gz,dist/*.deb,dist/*.rpm,dist/checksums.txt` subject-path globs all matched). Every one returned `✓ Verification succeeded!` with `Build workflow: .github/workflows/release.yml@refs/tags/v0.9.0-rc.1`, signer repo == build repo, OIDC issuer `token.actions.githubusercontent.com`, predicate `slsa.dev/provenance/v1`. The workflow ref is the load-bearing line: it binds each artifact to *this tag's run*, not merely to some run in the org. As a closing cross-check, the attested `checksums.txt` agrees with all 8 digests `gh` computed independently from the downloaded files, so the provenance and the checksum manifest corroborate rather than merely coexist. The `.sbom.json`/`.sig`/`.pem` assets are deliberately not subjects and would (correctly) fail `verify`.
- **Note for the real tag:** the rehearsal deliberately left `## Unreleased` populated. v0.9.0 must drain it into a `## v0.9.0` heading or the precondition step will (correctly) refuse the tag. Teardown of the rc is optional: `gh release delete v0.9.0-rc.1` + `git push --delete origin v0.9.0-rc.1`, but the Go module proxy and attestation store cache it permanently either way — benign, since `@latest` ignores prereleases.


### DF109 — the release notes' empty-body guard was a no-op: `%(contents:body)` emits a newline, so `[ -s ]` never fires — RESOLVED (fixed on discovery)

- **Discovered:** 2026-07-15 · **Workstream:** DF89 rc rehearsal (found by *executing* the precondition step locally instead of reading it)
- **Severity:** MEDIUM (latent; it would have shipped a release whose notes were empty — the exact outcome the guard exists to prevent) · **Disposition:** RESOLVED (fixed on discovery)
- **Description.** The "Verify release preconditions" step guards against a tag whose annotation has no body, because the release notes come from `git tag -l --format='%(contents:body)'` and an empty body means an empty release. The guard was `if [ ! -s "${RUNNER_TEMP}/release-notes.md" ]`. But a **subject-only annotation does not produce an empty file** — `%(contents:body)` emits a bare newline, so the file is exactly 1 byte, `-s` calls it non-empty, and `[ ! -s ]` never fires. Verified directly: `wc -c` = 1, `xxd` = `0a`. The guard passed a subject-only rc tag cleanly.
- **Fix.** Test for non-whitespace content rather than file size: `if ! grep -q '[^[:space:]]' "${RUNNER_TEMP}/release-notes.md"`. Re-ran the full matrix against the shipping step — lightweight tag → rejected, subject-only annotation → rejected (was accepted), annotated-with-body rc → through, real tag with a non-empty `## Unreleased` → rejected.
- **How it survived.** The guard was added *this week*, in the same sweep that filed DF89 — the finding about release code that is "statically verified but has never executed". It was itself never executed: workflow steps only run at tag time, `make check` cannot reach them, and the shape (`[ ! -s $file ]`) reads as obviously correct. It was found by extracting the step's shell body and running it against real annotated tags, which is the same move DF89 asks for and cost about two minutes. A gate nobody has watched fail is not a gate — including, evidently, the ones written to enforce that idea.
- **Pointer:** `.github/workflows/release.yml` ("Verify release preconditions"). Related: DF89 (the rc that would have run past it).

### DF108 — the netfs unit tests exercised a parallel copy of the classifier, not the code that runs — RESOLVED (fixed on discovery)

- **Discovered:** 2026-07-15 · **Workstream:** D124 sweep, "no speculative API" pass (surfaced by running `unused --tests=false`, which the normal lint cannot see)
- **Severity:** LOW (both copies were correct, so nothing was broken — the defect is that the tests vouched for code production did not call) · **Disposition:** RESOLVED (fixed on discovery)
- **Description.** `store/netfs_linux.go` had `isNetworkFilesystemMagic(magic) bool`, a pure classifier whose own doc comment called it "the primary unit-test target for FS detection". It had exactly one caller: its test. The production probe, `networkFilesystemName`, repeated the `networkMagics` map lookup inline instead of calling it. So `TestIsNetworkFilesystemMagic` asserted against a *sibling* of the shipping logic. Both were right, which is precisely why nothing would have caught them diverging: a change to one copy leaves the other green.
- **Fix.** Replaced it with `networkMagicName(magic) (string, bool)` — the same pure classifier, but returning the name production actually needs — and made `networkFilesystemName` call it. Now the tested function is the shipped function, and the test additionally asserts the returned name rather than only a bool.
- **Pointer:** `store/netfs_linux.go`, `store/netfs_linux_test.go`

### DF107 — the architecture-doc path gate suffix-matched, so a row could name a file from another package — RESOLVED (D124)

- **Discovered:** 2026-07-15 · **Workstream:** D124 architecture-doc gate
- **Severity:** LOW · **Disposition:** RESOLVED
- **Description.** `TestRepoHygiene_ArchitectureDocRefs_Resolve` accepts a Go path that suffix-matches any tracked file, because the docs abbreviate. A row under `### workspace/` naming `apply.go` therefore passed on the strength of `copyflow/apply.go`. Found by hand, not by the gate: `internal/workspace/` listed `apply.go` and `diff.go` (both in `copyflow/`), `internal/config/` listed `errors.go` (it is `yoerrors/`), `create/` claimed `context.go` (it is `internal/envsetup/`).
- **Fix.** Added `TestRepoHygiene_ArchitectureDocSections_NameRealFiles`, which reads each table row against **its own section heading** rather than the whole tree: the first backticked token ending in `/` is the section's package, that package must be a real directory, and each row's file must exist inside it. Scope: 28 package sections, 108 file rows. It caught two more on landing that the whole-tree check could not see (`internal/config/errors.go`, `internal/orchestrator/fileutil.go`). Prerequisite: `code-map.md`'s section headings are now real paths — the abbreviations (`agent/`, `config/`, `orchestrator/`) were the reason tolerance was needed, and `extension/` named a directory that does not exist at that path (the package is `internal/cli/extension/`), so every row under it had been scoped to nothing.
- **Pointer:** `repo_hygiene_test.go` (`docSectionFileRefs`), `docs/contributors/architecture/code-map.md`

### DF106 — the orchestrator façade's comments still named `package sandbox`, which no longer exists — RESOLVED

- **Discovered:** 2026-07-15 · **Workstream:** D124 architecture-doc sweep
- **Severity:** LOW · **Disposition:** RESOLVED
- **Description.** Seven comments across `internal/orchestrator` claimed to keep "the public sandbox API stable" or to let "existing callers in package sandbox continue to compile" — in files declaring `package orchestrator`. No `package sandbox` exists anywhere in the tree; it was renamed. The comments were the last surviving record of the old name, and `data-flows.md` had inherited it (`sandbox.ProbeWorkData`), which is how it surfaced. Filed as two files; the grep found five more.
- **Fix.** All seven rewritten to describe the leaf carve they actually document. Note the ABOUTME gate checks that a header exists, not that it is true — this class stays review-caught, as D124 records for narrative generally.
- **Pointer:** `internal/orchestrator/{aliases,inspect,lifecycle,notice,profile_build}.go`, `internal/orchestrator/create/create.go`, `internal/orchestrator/testhelpers_test.go`

### DF105 — two functions in the seed and diff paths were kept alive only by their own tests — RESOLVED

- **Discovered:** 2026-07-15 · **Workstream:** D124 architecture-doc sweep (found while verifying data-flows.md's call chains)
- **Severity:** LOW · **Disposition:** RESOLVED
- **Description.** `envsetup.CreateSecretsDir` had eight callers, all in its own test file, and none in production — the launch path calls its two halves separately so the broker can rewrite the map in between. Its doc comment offered "an embedder can stage credentials on an isolated path" as justification, but it lives under `internal/` and nothing re-exports it, so no embedder could reach it; `stagingRoot` is honored by `StageSecretEnv`, which is the live path. `copyflow.loadAllDiffContexts` was likewise test-only, and returned at most one context since the diff surface became workdir-only.
- **Fix.** Both deleted, with the `DiffContext` type that only `loadAllDiffContexts` produced. **The `CreateSecretsDir` tests were retargeted, not deleted:** the dead wrapper was the only test entry point into `ResolveSecretEnv`/`StageSecretEnv`, so removing them wholesale would have silently stripped all coverage from the credential-staging path. The combining wrapper now lives in the test, which is the right home for it — production must *not* combine the halves, because the broker step goes between them.
- **Why the linter missed it.** `unused` counts a test caller as a caller, so a function whose only user is its own test is invisible to the normal lint. `unused --tests=false` surfaces the class; the remaining hits tree-wide are GOOS artifacts (`runtime/seatbelt/` code whose real caller is in a `_darwin.go` file excluded on Linux), so a standing gate needs per-GOOS runs — not built. See D125.
